1. Introduction, Outline, and Review
====================================

Hey everyone and welcome to Natural Language Processing with Deep Learning in Python, Part 6. This course is all about combining natural language processing. Also known as NLP with the magic of deep learning as you might already know. NLP has been around for a very long time but just recently deep learning has been used to transform the fields in the same way that it's transformed computer vision and reinforcement learning. So at a very high level what is NLP.

And why should you want to learn it. Well when we picture AI we often picture ourselves talking to a machine. This is in many ways one of the ultimate achievements of AI when we can have a conversation but that conversation is so smooth and natural We can't tell if we're talking to a machine or a human. And you might think Well that sounds a lot like science fiction but scientists are hard at work every day making this a reality. So you may have noticed all the major tech companies have their own personal assistant nowadays.

Apple has Siri. Microsoft has Cortana Google has Google Assistant, Amazon has Alexa. Competition breeds innovation. So I'm very hopeful that we can make a lot of progress in NLP in the near future. But let's dig a little deeper. What technologies are required to make something like a personal assistant to actually work. So first when you speak into a microphone that's just sound waves. So the first step is that we need to turn those sound waves into words. We call that speech to text and this is a classic problem in NLP.

What happens after we turn what you say into text. Well then we need to figure out what you mean. You can think of this as sort of a text classification problem we take in some text and we try to predict what you're trying to say. For example you might say Hey Siri can you get me a movie ticket for Deadpool on Saturday night and from this the system has to figure out a few things first that you wanted to buy a movie ticket. Second what movie you want to see.

And third when and where you want to see it then it might ask you to confirm those things. So the system also has to be able to convert text into speech. This is called, quite appropriately, text-to-speech. Now a system like this is a result of several state of the art components and it requires tens or hundreds of engineers to make it work. Of course in order to build such a system you first need to be able to build its components. This is the era of deep learning.

And of course deep learning is being used to build state of the art versions of many of these components. And that brings us to this course. What are these components and how do they work. So we started this video at quite a high level almost at the level of science fiction robots that you can converse with. Of course this is a deep learning course and we aren't concerned with science fiction. We're concerned with the low level. How do these components work.

How do they make use of deep learning and how can we build them this starts out with one of the most important advances in deep NLP research, which is word embeddings. Of course, before doing this we'll do a quick review to get you up to speed on all the knowledge you'll need to make it through the rest of the course. Word embeddings allow you to map words into a vector space. Once you can represent something as a vector you can perform arithmetic on it.

So this is where the famous King minus man equals Queen minus woman comes from. We'll be looking at two of the most popular algorithms for finding word embeddings which are word2vec and GloVe. Next we'll look at how word2vec and GloVe, although developed independently and on the surface seem totally different are actually very similar. Next we'll move on to using deep neural networks for NLP. Of course the central architecture used in deep NLP is the RNN the recurrent neural network recurrent neural networks are special kinds of neural networks that allow us to model sequences.

And the reason why that's useful of course is because a sentence is nothing but a sequence of words. After that we'll look at an even more powerful model of deep neural networks for NLP. You might notice that sentences come in all shapes and sizes. Some are very short like one or two words long but you can have very long sentences like one that's 30 words long but how do we as humans make sense of such long sentences. Well the truth is we don't conceptualize sentences as sequences of words but rather we give it a hierarchical structure.

A sentence is made up of a group of phrases and each of those phrases could be made up of smaller phrases. We don't make sense of sentences by thinking of each word from start to ends but rather by the relationships between these phrases. In other words a sentence is more like a tree and therefore one might infer that a neural network shaped like a tree or a recursive neural network might be the best type of neural network for tasks such as text classification.

In fact recursive neural networks have led to state of the art results. This is one of the most technically challenging sections of any of my deep learning courses. So it's really going to test your limits when it comes to understanding algorithms and your Theano or TensorFlow coding ability. So just to summarize this lecture and give you a short overview of the outline of this course first we're going to look at word embeddings. This is going to involve learning about famous algorithms like word2vec and GloVe.

Second we're going to look at how RNNs can be used to solve deep NLP tasks. And finally we're going to look at how recursive neural networks a much more powerful but technically challenging type of model can be used to obtain superior results on deep NLP tasks. Thanks for listening and I'll see you in class.

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class in this lecture. I want to make a quick note that every example in this course will be done in both Theano and TensorFlow. So what does this mean and why are things this way. To give you a little bit of a history lesson. Theano is the ancestor of all the deep learning libraries that exists today. The deep learning libraries that are popular today were heavily inspired by Theano which came with notable features such as GPU acceleration and automatic differentiation.

Now Theano was and still is a great tool for learning deep learning because the syntax in Theano looks a lot like what you see in NumPy. So if you can code in NumPy you can code in Theano without much extra effort. In addition because a lot of other deep learning libraries are newer they don't yet have some of the features that Theano has. So if you want to do something really specific you might be stuck with coding it in Theano. Theano is very first principles based. What that means is you can take your math equation and when you're trying to implement that in code in Theano, what you see in code is very similar to the math. In newer libraries like TensorFlow,

This is often not the case. Instead what you end up doing a lot of the time is you have some equation and you know how you do it in NumPy. But when you try the same thing in TensorFlow it doesn't work. So what you then have to do is you have to search through TensorFlow documentation to find the right function for the thing you're trying to do. And sometimes that function doesn't exist. So what can you do about this. Well truthfully oftentimes the answer is nothing but despite how awesome T.A.

is development of a library has stopped. And so originally when these courses were built Theano was the best library to build them in because not only did it have all the necessary features it allowed you to build things using first principles rather than having to look through the documentation to find the right name for the right function. So it was nice to do things in Theano. And today you can still do things in Theano if you so choose. Luckily development on other popular deep learning libraries is happening at a rapid pace.

TensorFlow seems to be releasing a new version monthly. Of course it's got Google behind it and we know that Google isn't going anywhere. They also care a lot about productionization of machine learning code because Google's machine learning systems have to serve results to real users. And so you can feel confident that if your company wants to productionize its machine learning code, TensorFlow is a safe choice. Recently, Twitter is one example of a company who has made this decision and so to make a long story short every code example in this course will be done in both Theano and TensorFlow.

If you don't care about Theano then you can safely skip those lectures because the equivalent lecture will also be done in TensorFlow if you care about both then you can watch both. Remember that the general pattern we're going to follow in this course is going from theory to code. So first we discuss theory which means new concepts new equations new algorithms and so forth. Next we implement that theory in code. So in the code lectures there will be no new concepts no new algorithms and no new equations.

We simply implement what we talked about in the previous lecture. So normally it would just be one code lecture, but in the case where we have Theano, then it's going to be both a Theano lecture and a TensorFlow lecture both implementing the same thing.

### 2. Beginner's Corner Working with Word Vectors

1. What are vectors
===================

![46.png](./images/46.png)
![47.png](./images/47.png)
![48.png](./images/48.png)
![49.png](./images/49.png)
![50.png](./images/50.png)
![51.png](./images/51.png)
![52.png](./images/52.png)
![53.png](./images/53.png)
![54.png](./images/54.png)
![55.png](./images/55.png)
![56.png](./images/56.png)
![57.png](./images/57.png)
![58.png](./images/58.png)

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to talk about machine learning and feature vectors at a very basic level. This section is designed for those of you who may not meet the prerequisites of this course. So at the very least you'll have material in this course that you can follow along with work on some code and so on. So what are feature vectors and why do we need them.

Let's start with a basic machine learning problem. Suppose I'm given a person's height and weight and I would like to predict whether or not this person is a male or a female in order to do this. I need to have some data to learn from. So I take these measurements for a bunch of different people. I measure their height and their weight and I classify them as male or female. Once I do this I can plot all these points on a grid as you can see here. So I want one thing to be very very apparent to you and that is machine learning is nothing but a geometry problem.

All it is is this I have a bunch of blue dots and I have a bunch of red dots and I want to find some line or a curve that can separate these red dots and blue dots machine learning is not magic. In fact it's just geometry. So anyway what's the point of all this. Well the point is each of these dots you see these are feature vectors. A vector is just a list of numbers and these numbers can be used to predict the class that this input belongs to. So let's take a more NLP related example.

Suppose I have some books about biology and physics and I would like to be able to separate the biology books and the physics books using only a feature vector that is related somehow to its content. I claim that I can do this simply by counting up certain words in each book. So for example suppose I chose the words chromosome and gravity. Now I can make a table for each book I can see how many times that the word chromosome appear. How many times does the word gravity appear.

Now what happens when I plot these data points. Well quite predictably the biology books are going to have the word chromosome appear a large number of times whereas the physics books are going to have the word gravity appear. A large number of times it's also allows me to ask questions. For example if I have a new book and I've counted up the words in this new book and I plot this point in my grid but I don't yet know whether this is a biology book or a physics book I can use this line that I found to make a prediction about whether or not this new book is a biology book or a physics book.

So I can see right now that it falls on the side of the line that has more red dots. So it predicts that this is a physics book. OK so what if I flip this table sideways. Now I can think of my words as samples and the books as features. Let's just add some more words to be sure. So I'm going to look at the words mitochondria friction and energy. Each cell In this table still tells me how many times a certain word appears in a certain book. And once again I can plot these vectors on a grid.

And of course you should notice something interesting all the words which are related to each other tend to show up close together. In other words they form clusters. So what we can conclude from this is that counting the number of times a word appears in a document seems to yield good feature vectors for words we can say that these vectors are meaningful because they are positioned in space in such a way that makes logical sense. All the words from the biology book show up close together as they probably should keep in mind this is pretty simplistic.

All I've done is counted up how many times each word appears in each document. OK so what's the problem with counting. Actually there's nothing wrong with it. In fact it can yield a very accurate classifiers but it does have some properties that some people might consider undesirable. For example consider the fact that words like "the", "a", "in", "to", "and", "but" and so on probably appear many times in all documents. So they give us very large feature values even though they are totally meaningless words that count for these words is probably so large that they overshadow all the other words.

These are called Stop words and one common technique is to simply remove them from the data set before doing any machine learning. But there is another technique you may have heard of called TF-IDF we're not going to go into the details of TF-IDF because that's not the focus of this course but the gist of it is this. We know that words that appear in many documents are probably less meaningful and therefore we can weight each vector component which in this case is a word by something related to how many documents that word appears in.

So intuitively speaking we might do something like Word count divided by document count. So the numerator tells us how many times does this word appear in this document. And the denominator tells us how many documents does this word appear in total. Now in practice we do some transformations on these like taking a log. But again TF-IDF is not something we're going to learn about in this course so we won't discuss these details. Of course you're encouraged to look into them if that's something you're interested in.

Wikipedia happens to list many variations of TF-IDF. Now there's one really important takeaway I want you to get from this lecture and this is that I want you to pay attention to the shape of the tables we're dealing with. I want you to kind of burn this into your brain remember and don't forget it. The shape of the table we're interested in is V by D. V is the vocabulary size. Or in other words total number of possible words. D is the vector dimensionality which if we're doing something like counting up the number of times that word appears in a book is the total number of books we have.

So whether you're doing pure counts or you're doing TF-IDF the size of the thing we're interested in is V by D. What this means is we have in total V words and for each word we have a vector of length d to represent that word. And by the way you're going to see the term word embedding used a lot. So what is a word embedding. Well you'll be happy to know it's just a new word but not a new concept. You in fact already know what a word embedding is a word embedding is just a fancy name for a feature vector that represents a word in other words.

We can take a categorical object a word in this case and then map this object to a list of numbers. Or in other words a vector we say we've embedded this word into a vector space. So that's why we call them word embeddings. But don't be scared by this name just remember that when we say word embeddings all we mean is a vector that represents a word.

2. What is a word analogy
=========================

![59.png](./images/59.png)
![60.png](./images/60.png)
![61.png](./images/61.png)
![62.png](./images/62.png)
![63.png](./images/63.png)
![64.png](./images/64.png)
![65.png](./images/65.png)
![66.png](./images/66.png)
![67.png](./images/67.png)
![68.png](./images/68.png)
![69.png](./images/69.png)
![70.png](./images/70.png)
![71.png](./images/71.png)

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. One of the most popular applications of word embeddings is word analogies. This is where the famous King minus man equals Queen minus woman comes from. So the topic of this lecture is what are word analogies and how can we calculate them. First let's just talk about analogies in general. We have king is to man as Queen is to a woman. This makes sense because King is the head of the royal family and it's a male. Queen is the head of the royal family and as a female.

So if you subtract a man from King you might think of that as a vector representing head of the royal family without the gender component. Now I realize this sounds really complicated and I think word analogies are better understood through example. So let's look at some. How about King minus Queen equals Prince minus princess on both sides. We have a male member of the royal family. Subtracting a female member of the royal family who are of the same generation.

How about France minus Paris equals Germany minus Berlin on both sides. We have a country subtracting a famous city in that country. How about Japan minus Japanese equals China minus Chinese on both sides. We have a country. Subtracting the term used to refer to the people of that country. How about Brother minus sister equals uncle minus aunt on both sides. We have the male member of the family. Subtracting a close female member of a family. How about walk minus walking equals swim minus swimming.

This shows us that we've learned something about verb tense so there are many more analogies like this that you can try but hopefully this gave you an intuitive sense for how they work. So how can we visualize and calculate word vectors. As usual it's very helpful to think of these geometrically. First let's recall that a word embedding just means the word vector. In other words if I have a grid we can say that each word is just represented by a dot on this grid.

Now what happens when I subtract one vector from another vector. Well of course this just gives me the vector between those two vectors. So if I say the difference of the two vectors on the left is approximately equal to the difference of the two vectors on the right. What am I really saying. Well I'm saying that these two different vectors are approximately the same. Now we know that a vector has two components a direction and a magnitude. So when I say that these two vectors are approximately the same.

What I'm saying is their magnitude and direction are very close to one another later in this lecture when we look at cosine distance you'll see that we only compare direction. This brings us to the next topic of this lecture which is how do we actually find these analogies. So the way that we can do that is this. We know that there are four words in every analogy. So what we can do is we take three of these words and we try to find the fourth word.

Notice how since everything is a vector we can just rearrange this equation. So King minus man equals Queen minus woman becomes king minus man plus woman equals queen. So I just move woman to the other side but we're pretending we don't know queen so we use a question mark so we can phrase this problem as given that King minus man plus woman find the fourth word represented by this unknown vector. Now how can we do that. Well, you know that King minus man plus woman is going to give us some arbitrary vector.

Now our job is to find the word most associated with that vector. And of course we can do that using distance. In other words what I want to do is find the word in my vocabulary whose word vector is closest to King minus man plus woman as an exercise. I want you to think about how you might do that in pseudo code if you want to stop or pause the video. Now is the time on the next slide. We'll look at the solution. So hopefully you thought about how we can solve this problem given a vector.

I would like to know which word in my vocabulary is best represented by this vector. In other words I want to know which word's vector is closest to this new vector. And I can do that simply by looping through every word in my vocabulary. Checking the distance between the word vector and my test vector and keeping track of which word had the closest vector. Pretty simple. Note that in the code we'll be looking at two ways of doing this. The first way is pretty much a direct translation of what you see here.

This is the most intuitive thing to do but we know that for loops in Python are slow. And so the second method We'll look at is a way to use built in functions in the NumPy stack to do all the distance calculations at the same time which makes things a lot faster. Now you may have noticed that on the previous slide I didn't actually show you how the distance function is implemented. Maybe you assumed we would use Euclidean distance or maybe you didn't but in actuality there are multiple options here.

In practice it's actually more common to use the cosine distance which is related to the cosine of the angle between two vectors. Note that we need to invert the cosine of the angle because it returns one when the two vectors are close and minus one when the two vectors are far apart. So we subtracted from one and the result is that if the vectors are very close together we get one minus one equals zero. But if the vectors are far apart we get one minus minus one which is two.

Now you might ask when it comes to your personal application should you choose Euclidean distance or cosine distance. That's not an easy question to answer. You can consider the fact that it's more common to use cosine distance. But at the same time there is no definitive choice. Ultimately you should choose what yields the best results for your application. One remarkable fact about neural word embedding algorithms is that they can find these analogies at all after you learn about these algorithms specifically where it's effect and GloVe.

You'll see that these algorithms have no concept of analogies. What they want to optimize is totally unrelated to word analogies. So the fact that word analogies suddenly emerge out of the training process is a very intriguing result. Remember that in all cases we're still dealing with a V by the word embedding matrix. This is the case where they were just using raw word counts or TF-IDF or word2vec. Yet raw word counts and TF-IDF do not give us good analogies.

So if you're doing NLP research and you want to look at why certain algorithms give us good analogies and others don't. That would certainly be an interesting topic.

3. Trying to find and assess word vectors using TF-IDF and t-SNE
================================================================

![72.png](./images/72.png)
![73.png](./images/73.png)
![74.png](./images/74.png)
![75.png](./images/75.png)
![76.png](./images/76.png)
![77.png](./images/77.png)

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. So one thing that was nice about the examples in the previous lecture was that we could see them as you may recall we as humans can only see up to three dimensions. It's hard to represent three dimensions on a flat surface so a two dimensional data set makes the most sense when we're doing simple examples. But we know that in the real world when we're doing something like TF-IDF

and we want to have vectors that are derived from lots of data then our dimensionality is going to be really large. This is just because we're going to have lots of documents. So the number of documents we have is going to be our dimensionality at the same time. We know that we have algorithms such as t-SNE that can both reduce the dimensionality of high dimensional data while at the same time preserving the structure of that data. In other words t-SNE is going to let us visualize that data even though it originally came from a high dimensional source.

The important point is we know that any word embedding is going to be a table of shape V by D. And we know that this is exactly what TF-IDF can give us. So the point of this lecture is forget about word2vec and GloVe for a moment. We already have algorithms like TF-IDF that give us word embeddings. The question is do these word embeddings make sense. Now we already know that classic algorithms like TF-IDF are not going to give us good word analogies.

However that shouldn't stop us from just trying to see what happens. Furthermore we can still look at word similarity. In plain English that means let's look at nearby words and make sure that they make sense. For example we expect dogs to be close to canine. We'd expect programmers to be close to software. We'd expect sushi to be close to sashimi and so on. So the code for this lecture is in the file tfidf_tsne.py in the course repo. You're welcome to look at it if you want but it's pretty straightforward so I'm not going to walk through it.

The script is basically just a few steps. Step number one. Load in the data. This can be either from the Brown corpus or Wikipedia. If you've downloaded some Wikipedia data and preprocessed it into the right format step number 2, transform the data using TF-IDF this is going to give us a matrix of size V by D. Step number 3. Transform the data again into two dimensions using t-SNE. Step number 4. Plot the data on a scatterplot. We're also going to try to do some analogies but you'll see that TF-IDF is not successful at this task.

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at some pre-trained GloVe vectors found by the people at Stanford where GloVe was invented these days. You can find pre-trained versions of many types of neural networks. So it's always worth checking out to see what you can find. Often these weights were trained on way more data and with way more tuning of hyper parameters than we could possibly do.

So if you're going to use word embeddings for some other downstream task it's a great idea to start with the pre-trained weights that are available online. In this lecture we're going to look at how to load in pre-trained word vectors and how to calculate some analogies and some nearest neighbors. Both of these will give us confidence that these word vectors make sense to start. I want to look at word vectors pre-trained using the GloVe algorithm which we're going to study later in the course.

The reason I want to start with GloVe is that these vectors are provided in plain text and on a relatively smaller vocabulary than the pre-trained word2vec vectors. This has some advantages. First it's going to allow us to parse the file using plain python so you can actually look at the file. Check out the format for yourself and be more confident that the code makes sense. And because we'll be loading in the word vectors manually we get to store them in a way that's convenient for us and we can implement the code to find analogies and nearest neighbors that we talked about earlier.

So to download these pre-trained word vectors you want to copy and paste this URL into your browser as you can see. This is already in the file pre-trained glove.py. So you don't want to type it out manually. Now the only things we need to import in this file are NumPy and a function called pairwise distances from scikit-learn. You'll see why we need this function very shortly. Next thing we need is to define our distance functions. This is so you can compare the results of using Euclidean distance versus cosine distance.

It's as you'll see both of these work quite well. Next we have a function to find analogies as discussed. It takes in three words. And our job is to find the fourth word. The first thing we need to do in this function is to check if these words are in our vocabulary. If any of them are not we can't do the calculation. So we just return next we get the word vectors for these three words in this function. I'm going to call them king men and woman even though that's not what they really are.

But they should help you understand this function since you can always remember the analogy. King minus man equals Queen minus woman. So we assign the king Minus Man plus woman to a vector called V not. And our job now is to find the word whose vector is closest to V not to do that we first initialize our minimum distance so far. min_dist to infinity. And we set best_word to a blank string. Next we loop through each word in our word2vec dictionary inside the loop.

We calculate the distance of the current vector to our test vector v not. We call this d, and if this d is better than our current min_dist then we set min_dist to d. And we set best_word to the current word. Once this is done we can print the result. Note how we can also check the distance only if the word is not one of the original three words. This helps improve the results a little bit since often times these words will end up being very close together.

Now remember this version of find_analogies is the naively implemented version because in python we prefer not to use for loops if we don't have to so below this we have another implementation of find_analogies making use of scikit-learn's function pairwise_distances. Let me explain a little bit about how this works. So suppose we have two separate lists of vectors and we would like to find the distance between each vector in the first list and each vector in the second list.

So suppose the first list is of shape N1 by D, and the second list is of shape N2 by D, then the result will be an N1 by N2 matrix where the (i, j)th entry will be the distance between vector I from the first list and vector J from the second list. You can imagine this kind of function might be useful for doing a nearest neighbor search on a set of test vectors as you would during k-nearest neighbor. For us, nearest neighbor is in fact exactly what we are doing except our first list contains only one vector v not and our second list contains all the word vectors in our embedding matrix.

The result of this of course is going to be one by V where v is our vocabulary size but the extra one dimension isn't useful. So we can reshape this to just be a 1D array of length V. Now we can call the argmin instance method to get the index of the minimum distance. And this of course refers back to the closest word so we can use the idx2word dictionary to map back from index to word and then we can print the result. Next we have a function to find the nearest neighbors.

So before we were doing word analogies but an even simpler task is to look at a word and then look for the words very close to it. If the word embedding makes sense then you're going to find related words nearby. So unlike with analogies, nearest neighbors should always make sense even for non neural embeddings like plain word counting or TF-IDF. Next we actually load in the pre-trained word vectors. So I assume that you've unzipped the zip file and place the results into the large files directory which is adjacent to the class folder.

Note that the zip file contains embeddings of different dimensionalities but I've chosen 50 for simplicity. Next we loop through each line of the file and remember you're strongly encouraged to check out the file for yourself so you can see what's in there and verify that this code makes sense. So each line in the file is just a space separated list of items. The first item is the word and every item after that are the vector components. So we can do line.split to get a list and assign the first element of the list to be the word and everything after that to be the corresponding vector.

At this point we can store this information in three ways that will be useful later. First we want a word2vec mapping. So the key is the word and the value is the vector next we'd like to have a big embedding matrix so we can store that as a list of numbers. For now since we don't know how many lines there will be and then cast it to a big NumPy array later. Finally we have an idx2word mapping which lets us map back from index to word once that's done we can find some analogies and also do some nearest neighbor searches.

Let's look at the results so let's scroll up to the analogies. OK so the first one. King minus man equals king minus woman doesn't really make sense. But we could have avoided that if we left out the three original words but we didn't do that. In the faster version of find_analogies we have France minus Paris equals Britain minus London. That makes sense. France minus Paris equals Italy minus Rome. That makes sense. Paris minus France equals Rome minus Italy.

That makes sense. France minus French equals England minus English. Not quite. Japan minus Japanese equals China minus Chinese. That makes sense. Japan minus Japanese equals Italy minus Italian. That makes sense. Japan minus Japanese equals Australia minus Australian. That makes sense. [unclear] Einstein minus scientist equals Matisse minus painter. I don't know you can check if that's the name of a painter.

[unclear] Man minus woman equals he she that makes sense. Man minus woman equals uncle minus aunt. Yes. [unclear] minus wife. Not quite. Actor minus actress Yes father minus mother yes Queen minus princess. Not quite. Nephew minus niece equals uncle minus aunt. That makes sense. France minus Paris equals Japan minus Tokyo. That makes sense. France minus Paris equals China minus Beijing. That makes sense. [unclear] and I think we have some repeats.

Probably OK so let's look at the nearest neighbors so we have king prince. queen, and so on. So those all make sense. France French. Belgium makes sense. Japan Japanese. And then a bunch of other East Asian countries that makes sense. Einstein relativity and then a bunch of other physics names and words that make sense. Woman Girl man mother that makes sense. Nephew cousin brother grandson that makes sense. We have a bunch of months. It looks good. Rome Naples Venice Italy.

That makes sense as well. So you can see that for the most part these vectors make a lot of sense.

Phrases are individual tokens joined by an underscore. So for example New York would be represented as New_York. As you can imagine this is probably a good idea since the phrase New York has a very different meaning than just the individual tokens. New and York separately. So be prepared to wait the next issue is that the word vectors are provided in a binary format. This allows the file to be smaller in size but it also means you have to figure out the structure of the file if you want to be able to parse it. This library gives us back an object which has instance methods for calculating analogies and nearest neighbors. So we would use those rather than writing the functions ourselves.

That's not ideal for us because it doesn't allow us to think about how analogies and neighbors are calculated. But since we've already done that we don't need to do it again. Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. In this lecture we are going to see how we can directly apply pre-trained word vectors in order to do text classification. You'll see that not only is this quite simple and intuitive but we also get pretty good results. So let's start with how we're going to represent our data. Unlike the rest of this course and this introductory section we're going to use a bag of words model what this means is we don't consider the order of the words in a sentence.

So for example the phrase toy dog would be represented in the same way as dog toy. Even though we know that these are two totally different things. Some examples of bag of words that we've already seen are word counting and TF-IDF. But of course since this is deep NLP we don't want to only use these methods. So how can we apply word vectors such as what we would get from word2vec and GloVe to bag of words. Well it's quite simple. We know that we want to represent a sentence or a document as a single vector first for each word in the sentence.

I can use my list of word vectors to get a vector. Then all I need to do is take the average of all those word vectors very simple so I add up all the word vectors and then divide by the number of vectors. OK so at this point I'm able to convert each document in my dataset into a single feature vector. Now what. Well now it's very simple. I can just plug this into any supervised machine learning classifier. Some examples would be naive Bayes, logistic regression or random forest and so on.

We'll also be using a classifier called extra trees which can also be found inside scikit-learn. One thing you may have realized is in order for this to work. In order for the feature vectors to actually be good feature vectors the individual word vectors have to make sense. So in fact what you're about to see is actually used as an evaluation method for telling us how good or bad a word embedding algorithm is. There are of course other evaluation methods we've already seen such as Word analogies and word similarities.

For example if GloVe vectors give us better classification accuracy than word2vec vectors then we would consider GloVe vectors more useful for this purpose. Of course any of these evaluation methods is just a proxy for how well they'll work on your particular problem with your particular dataset. So ultimately that would be your final criterion to get the data for this exercise. You'll want to go to this URL. Note that there are multiple links on this page so what you want to do is scroll down to where it says Reuters-21578 R8 and download the train set and the test set which are tab separated text files.

This URL is also in the code file itself. So please don't try to type it out manually So the outline of the coming exercise is this. Step Number 1 is of course to load in the data. Step Number 2 is to write a function to convert the data into feature vectors using either pre-trained word2vec or GloVe vectors for this. I'm going to build a class that acts like a scikit-learn data transformer. So it's going to have the functions fit, transform and fit_transform.

You don't necessarily have to do this. Step Number 3 is to transform the data train a classifier and print the train and test the accuracy of the trained classifier. All right so this exercise should be quite simple. If you've been following along so give it a try by yourself and in the next lecture we'll be looking at the code.

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at the code that does text classification using bag of words features based on word vectors from word2vec and GloVe the relevant file for this lecture is bow_classifier.py in the course repo. So hopefully you had a chance to try this out for yourself before starting this lecture. As you'll see it probably doesn't contain much code.

You haven't seen before. So at the top we have all the usual imports. We also have gensim for loading in the word2vec vectors and we have a classifier called extra trees classifier and I've also imported a random forest to give that a try. You are of course welcome to try other classifiers as well. Next we load in the data. As usual I've assumed you've put the data into the folder large files adjacent to the class folder. Since the data is tab separated we can use the Pandas function read_csv to read in the data into a DataFrame.

The data does not come with headers so we set header equals None and then rename the headers to label and content afterwards. Next. As promised we want to have some data transformer's that follow the format of scikit-learn the interface to these will look the same as TfidfVectorizer, for example. So first up we have GloVeVectorizer. In the constructor, We basically do the exact same thing you saw earlier when we computed word analogies. In fact I copied and pasted this code from that script so we loop through each line of the pre-trained vector file parse the vectors.

Store them and then save all the information to instance variables. Next we have the fit function which doesn't do anything. Next we have the transform function. First we need to initialize our data matrix X which is of shape N by D. Next we loop through each sentence in the data we have two counters here little n which indexes the data and emptycount which is going to tell us how many sentences didn't have any words we could find vectors for. So that's how we're going to deal with words that don't appear in the pre-trained word vectors list.

We're simply going to ignore them by doing this. It's possible for a sentence to contain only words that didn't appear in our word vectors. In which case the resulting vector will just be zero. Unfortunately there is not much we can do about this but luckily it doesn't happen often later on when we train deep networks end to end. Embedding is simply another weight matrix in the neural network and so we can use back propagation to fine tune the embedding and any words that don't come pre-trained can still be trained using backpropagation.

OK so inside the loop. First we lowercase the sentence and split it on a blank space. There are other ways to tokenize But this is the simplest. Next we start a list called vecs and that's going to store all the word vectors we encounter. For this document Next we loop through each word and if the word is in our vocabulary and we append its vector to vecs. Once the loop is done we check if vecs contains any vectors. If it does we assign its mean to X of n; if it doesn't we increment emptycount.

And at the end of the outer loop we increment n and after this we have a fit transform function which of course just calls both fit and transform. So that's it for the GloVeVectorizer. Next up we have the Word2VecVectorizer. So this is pretty much the same as GloVeVectorizer. We have the constructor we have the fit function which does nothing the transform function and the fit transform function. So in the constructor all we do is load in the vector using gensim which is just the line of code we saw earlier.

The transform function is pretty much the same as the GloVeVectorizer with a few minor differences. First notice how I'm not lowercasing the sentence. This is because the word2vec vectors contain both capitalized and lowercase word2vec. Notice how I can't use the word vectors like a dictionary. Well technically I could but the object already has a function called get_vector that we can use. The problem is it throws an exception if the word is not found.

So we have to wrap this with a try except if the word isn't found then there's nothing we can do. So we do nothing other than that this function is identical to the one we saw before and of course fit transform is the same as well. Finally we're ready to test everything out. So there's basically only two steps. The first step is to instantiate a vectorizer and transform the train data and the test data. The second step is to instantiate a classifier fit the model and test the model.

So let's run this and look at the results. All right. So we end up doing pretty good.

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. I'm going to give you an exercise not something to do now but as something to keep in mind as you take this course. So I'm going to give you a really quick preview of the models we'll be looking at later in this course. The first is the RNN or recurrent neural network. At its simplest you can think of an RNN as a 3 layer network. The layers are as follows.

The first layer is the embedding layer which just contains the word embedding matrix. So the input to this is a word index and the output is the corresponding word vector the next layer is the RNN unit. This can be a simple RNN unit or GRU or LSTM. The final layer is a regular dense layer that maps the hidden layer's output to one of the output classes. Importantly remember that the first layer contains the word embedding matrix in the section after that we'll be looking at recursive neural networks.

I don't want to get too deep into this right now but the basic idea is that it's a neural network that's structured like a tree each leaf node in the tree represents a word and so of course that word is represented by a word vector. And of course the way we obtain that word vector is by indexing a word embedding matrix. So in both of these models the common theme is that the very first thing that happens in either of these neural networks is that we go through the embedding matrix when the input to your neural network is a word.

Then the first layer is always going to be the word embedding matrix. And of course we know from this section that we can already get pretty good word embeddings from pre-trained models like word2vec and GloVe. So one idea you can try is instead of initializing the word embedding matrix randomly like we normally would you initialize it with word vectors from word2vec or GloVe. This should remind you of the greedy layer-wise pre-training approach we looked at in unsupervised deep learning.

If you don't know what I'm talking about don't worry because I just told you everything you need to know to do this. Another thing you can try is once you've set the word embedding matrix to a pre-trained set of vectors simply don't update them at all. This might sound strange because back propagation promises to find the optimal set of weights but in reality this works quite well. So if you're in Theano simply don't add the word embedding to your updates list.

And if you're TensorFlow set the trainable attribute of the word embedding variable to false. The fact that this works tells us that the word embeddings found by word2vec and GloVe are already quite good. In the course code I haven't done this because I wanted to leave it for you as an exercise so give it a try and let me know how it goes.

2. Bigrams and Language Models
==============================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this review lecture we are going to do what is probably the simplest possible thing we can do in NLP that is to create a bigram language model. Now hopefully this terminology is not new to you but just in case it is let's review a language model is a model of the probabilities of sequences of words as you know in English we call the sequence of words a sentence.

So we have a sentence like The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. Then our language model would allow us to calculate this joint probability distribution. The form this probability distribution takes is what makes up the model. And typically that's going to involve making some assumptions about the structure of language and how sentences are formed. And by the way just so we're super clear a model is never correct a model always makes assumptions. There can be correct assumptions and there can be incorrect assumptions there can be assumptions that are correct.

Most of the time but incorrect. Some of the time there can be assumptions that are incorrect. Most of the time but still yields powerful or useful results. So we use them anyway. This idea is encapsulated in the phrase the map is not the territory. So just keep that in mind whenever we have a model. Some parts will be right and some parts will be wrong but sometimes having a model is necessary. Of course a map may not be 100 percent accurate but that doesn't mean we should stop making maps.

That just means you have to know when the map is useful and when it's not. So back to NLP. Now that we know what a language model is we want to talk about a specific language model called the bigram. We recall that "bi" means two, like in bicycle or binoculars a bigram is just a sequence of two consecutive words in a sentence. So for example in this sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog the bike Reems are "the quick", "quick brown", "brown fox" and so on.

We can also have trigrams for example The quick brown quick brown fox brown fox jumps and so on. We can generalize this concept to n-grams which are sequences of consecutive words. But back to the bigram model the bigram model means what we're going to do is model each possible bigram as a probability. In general we can say that we want to model p w t given w t minus one. This means the probability of seeing a word at time t. Given that I know the word at time T minus one.

So for example my model might tell me that p of brown given quick is 0.5 but p of "the" given "the" is zero. This is because we most likely will not see a sentence containing "the the". The question is how can we find these probabilities. The answer of course is simply counting for example to calculate P of brown given quick. All I need to know is how many times does the word quick appear in my set of documents out of that. How many times does a sequence quick brown appear.

This gives me the maximum likelihood probability for p of brown given quick we can write this out as the count of quick going to brown divided by the count of just quick by itself. So hopefully this has been pretty simple so far. Now just a side note. What do I mean by a set of documents. Well generally speaking we're going to have some data some example sentences to train a model on in this class will be using Wikipedia. Most of the time but there are other data sets out there too like the brown corpus text8 and so on.

You could even scrape your own data if you wanted to but that's what I mean by a set of documents. It's some file or files that contain sentences sometimes will refer to this set of documents as our training corpus. Let's now go back to the idea of a language model. Recall that we want to know the probability of an entire sentence. The question is how can bigram help us do that as per our previous discussion this is going to involve making some assumptions.

Now instead of using our original example the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog which contains nine words. Let's use a shorter example. ABC which is just three words. So I want to know the probability P of ABC the next step is to apply Bayes rule. We know that p of ABC is equal to p of C given AB times p of AB. But now we can apply Bayes rule again. We can say that p of ABC is equal to p of C given AB times p of B given A times p of A. You can imagine that if we had a longer sentence we would just keep applying Bayes rule again and again until we got to our final result because of that we call this the chain rule of probability.

So why is this important. Well let's look at this expression carefully. Hopefully you can see that one of these probabilities is part of our bigram model p of B given A. Importantly we can see that p of C given AB and p of A are not bigrams but that's OK. We can also calculate these using maximum likelihood. If you want to try deriving the expressions for how to calculate these two probabilities I strongly recommend doing so before moving on to the next slide.

So feel free to pause the video before we move on. OK. So how do we calculate p of C given AB and p of A. Let's start with p of A. Well this is just the number of times a appears in the corpus relative to the total number of words. This is just like how you calculate the probability of a coin toss or a die roll. How about p of C given a B. Note that this is a trigram something we discussed briefly earlier in this lecture. Again is just counting the number of times the sequence ABC appears relative to the number of times of the sequence AB appears.

Now as I mentioned earlier we can extend this logic to sentences of any length. So for example if I have a sentence AB CDE then the joint probability is this. But you can see that at some point modeling these n-grams becomes problematic. For example if we go back to the sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. Perhaps this is the only sentence like this in our corpus. We know that the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy turtle is also a totally valid and reasonable sentence.

But if it never shows up in our corpus its maximum likelihood probability is 0. 0 wouldn't be a very accurate probability in this case since we know that in our language this sentence makes complete sense. So one way to overcome the zero probabilities is with what is called the add-one smoothing in particular. Instead of modeling each probability using the maximum likelihood we add 1 to the numerator and V to the denominator where v is the size of the vocabulary doing so ensures that our probabilities always sum to 1 which is necessary for them to form a valid probability distribution so we can see that smooth be given a is the count of a going to be plus 1 divided by the count of a plus V.

And as you can see even if the sequence AB never occurs in our corpus we still get a tiny probability value for it which makes it at least possible. Another modification we can make involves what is called the Markov assumption. The Markov assumption is that whatever you see now depends only on what you saw in the previous step. In other words any older terms just disappear. Mathematically I can write it like this. We call this first order Markov because it only depends on one previous term.

We can also have a second order Markov which depends on two previous terms. We can have third order Markov and so on but for Markov models we typically mean first order Markov. So as an example if we go back to a simple sentence This means that p of E given ABCD is just equal to p of E given D as an exercise try writing out the full joint probability distribution for this sentence. I'll give you a second to pause the video so you can try it for yourself.

So if we were to write the full joint probability it would consist of all bigram and one unigram. Now why is this significant. Well it's because bigram is what this lecture is all about. But other than the obvious if you recall perhaps the sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy turtle never appears in our corpus. That's because anything really long is unlikely to appear in. You can also come up with totally new sentences like The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy cat and the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy rabbit.

These are all totally valid sentences and they totally make sense but at the same time they are unlikely to have appeared in our training data. This is because our training data is very small compared to the entire universe of possible sentences. The key is that long sentences are just more likely to be unique but really short phrases like bigram are going to be very common. So if lazy turtle appears in our corpus a lot then it's going to get a higher probability so it's easier to model the probability for a lazy turtle rather than the probability for the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy turtle which in turn makes the full sentence much more probable.

So now that you know all about bigram and language modeling I'm going to introduce you to the next exercise. What I want you to do is this. We're going to use the brown corpus which is part of NLTK first. Once you've loaded in the brown corpus I want you to create a bigram language model with add-one smoothing. I recommend lowercasing all the words but generally you can apply any tokenization strategy you want. That's not really the key focus of this exercise.

Second I want you to test your language model. Compare the probability of a real sentence versus the probability of just a random sequence of words. Also compare it to a sentence that you generate that you know is a real sentence but that maybe doesn't appear in the training corpus. I want to give you one hint before you start this exercise. Recall that probabilities are always between 0 and 1. We also know that the full joint probability of our bigram model is just the multiplication of each bigram probability in the sentence.

We also know that multiplying small numbers together always yields an even smaller number. The result is that if you keep multiplying probabilities together you're going to very quickly approach zero. And if you hit the limit of numerical precision the computer is just going to round down to zero which is not very useful. So the solution to this is to use the log probability instead. The trick is this. We know that the log function is monotonically increasing.

So if A is greater than B We know that log A is also greater than log B. If you don't believe me you can try any two numbers on your calculator. Another tip. I suggest you implement is that we want to normalize each sentence since probabilities are between 0 and 1. That means log probabilities are always negative or less than zero. That means the longer sentences, the more negative numbers we're going to add together. This means that if we compare raw log probabilities there's always going to be a bias towards shorter sentences shorter sentences will always have a higher log probability simply because they have less negative numbers to add together to solve this problem.

We can just compare the log probabilities divided by the length of the sentence which I have denoted as capital-T. Alright so that's the exercise. Good luck.

Now the next step is just a sanity check to make sure all the tokens I wanted to keep are being kept in the final loop in this function is to convert the sentences using the old index format to the new index format. So it's using the index and new index map back to markov.py. I'm going to start out by walking you through the main section and we can look at any functions we call on the way. Given the previous word so let's scroll over to the get_bigram_probs function to see how it's constructed.

So instead of actually adding it to everything you could just start with it already there. Also notice that with add-one smoothing you don't have to actually add one. We loop through each sentence in the corpus. So if I equal zero that means this is the first word. 4. Neural Bigram Model
======================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to continue our review of some basic concepts. In particular we're going to extend our bigram model a little bit to use a neural model rather than just counting up each word to get the bigram probabilities. You'll recall that our basic model of a neuron is logistic regression. So that's going to be the focus of this lecture.

The first important thing we need to talk about is the fact that logistic regression works on numbers, not words. Our logit is going to take the form of W transpose X where X is an input vector containing numbers. In other words it can't contain any strings which is how a computer represents words. Try typing in cat times two in your Python console. You'll see that this doesn't make any sense. Well the trick is to think of words as categories. We know that the way we treat categories in deep learning is to use one-hot coding.

What this means is that if we have V words in our vocabulary then each word is going to be represented by a V-size vector that contains all zeros except a one in a unique spot specifically for that word. So for example the word "a" might be 1 0 0 0 and the rest are all zeros. The word "an" might be 0 1 and the rest are all zeros and the word zoo might be 0 0 0 all the way up to the end and then a 1. So every word has a one in a unique spot. That's what we mean by one hot encoding.

Now that we know how to represent words as one hot vectors how can we make use of logistic regression to create a bigram model. Well let's first recall how logistic regression works. We have an input vector X and an output vector y they are related by this equation P of y given X equals the softmax of W transpose x. Well this is really interesting because p of Y given X is exactly like a bigram if x is the first word and y is the following word. So how are we going to get our data into a format that logistic regression can understand.

Well actually it's not just logistic regression. It would be the same for any machine learning model such as a neural network which we are going to study next. This is because of the fact that all machine learning interfaces are the same so you can create logistic regression and scikit-learn and call fit(X, Y) or you can create a random forest and call the exact same function with the exact same x and y. This is the nature of machine learning. So we know that in general X is an N by D matrix of input samples and Y is an N by K matrix of output targets. In general D is the number of input features and K is the number of output classes.

But in this very unique case we want D and K both equal V. The vocabulary size this is because the input vector is just a one hot and coded word which is a vector of size V. But the target is also a one-hot coded word which is also a vector of size V. So basically both inputs and targets are of size V. And of course we can just build our X-Y matrix by looping over the training corpus and one-hot encoding each bigram we encounter. Once we have our data we need to know how to train our model to learn from this data.

Now this is not something we're going to review in-depth in this course because you absolutely need to be comfortable with this in order to proceed even if you did learn this in full today,. The rest of this course is going to be too hard for you. So there's no way you can learn logistic regression today and start doing deep NLP tomorrow. So hopefully this is a review. How we're going to train this model is by doing gradient descent essentially we loop through our training data a bunch of times and move w in small steps in the direction of the gradient.

And of course this is the gradient of the cost with respect to W and the cost is the categorical cross entropy which is what we use for classification importantly you should already know how to find this gradient how we vectorize it and so on. None of what you see on the slide should could be new to you especially since we're going to expand on it in the next lecture which is also part of the review. One thing I want you to be really comfortable with is translating these equations to code.

This should be a no-brainer at this point because you've already done this in code before and you've done the exercise of translating these equations into code. It's important to remember that this is the general pattern that this course is going to follow. First we're going to discuss some high level theory then we're going to derive some equations from that theory then we're going to translate that theory into code. The problem many people encounter is that they stop after Step 1.

They learn some high level theory and they mistakenly believe that this is 100 percent of the stuff they need to know. They never bother to get to step two and step three which is the equations and the code. But that is the real test for whether or not you really understand Step 1. Step 1 is just the introduction. So if you can't do step 2 and step 3 then you haven't really passed step one. Unfortunately the biggest mistake some people make is not understanding this.

So through these exercises I hope to get you accustomed to this process your exercise for this lecture is as follows. You're going to train another bigram model but instead of using counts to calculate the probabilities you're going to use logistic regression to approximate the probabilities. As usual you want to plot the training cost as a function of iteration. However one thing I also want you to do is this. Note that both W and the bigram probability matrix we calculated in the previous example are both V by V matrices.

So one question you might have is are these in some way related and of course if we take the softmax of W we should get back an approximation of the bigram probability matrix. Therefore we can also do the opposite. We can replace w in the logistic regression model with the log of the bigram probability matrix. From this we can calculate the cross entropy using that version of W. So to add one more step to this exercise plot not only the cost of the logistic model as a function of iteration but also plot the cross entropy from using the bigram probability matrix as the weight matrix as a horizontal line.

You can think of this as sort of the ideal version of W that our logistic regression is trying to find.

5. Neural Bigram Model in Code
==============================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to look at the code that uses logistic regression to find the bigram probabilities the relevant file in the course repo is logistic.py. So hopefully you had a chance to try to code this yourself first at the top we have all the same imports as before but now we're also importing get_bigram_probs from the markov file we wrote earlier.

This is going to let us compare the bigram probabilities using counts with the probabilities we find with logistic regression. Next we enter the main section. Notice I'm limiting the vocabulary size to 2000 in this code. This is because we don't want to have to wait too long. After this we again get the vocab size the start and end index and the bigram probabilities matrix. After that we have some new stuff. So we start out by initializing a random weight matrix which is what we do for logistic regression.

Next I create an empty list to store the loss per iteration. I set the epochs to 1 and the learning rate to 0.1 remember that these are hyper parameters that you can choose based on experimentation. I'm setting the epochs to one here since this is already going to take pretty long and you'll see that we can get convergence after just one epoch which is probably because most sentences follow the same patterns although you are encouraged to try to go longer if you want.

Next we define the softmax function. Since this isn't built in. This is almost by definition but we also subtract the max to avoid numerical overflow. Next I create a new W, W_bigram, which is set to the log of the bigram probabilities. As I mentioned in the theory lecture This reverses the softmax operation. I'm also going to create an array to store the bigram losses which we are going to calculate in the same loop. Next we start our training loop.

Note that it's good practice to shuffle your data before each epoch. Next we loop through each sentence. The first thing we do in the inner loop is prepend the start index and append the end index. This is because they are also part of our vocabulary. Next we create the one-hot coded versions of the inputs and targets. This uses an indexing trick which we're going to go in-depth into in a later lecture. Next we get our predictions which is just the code implementation of the equation

we looked at in the theory lecture. Next we do gradient descent, which again is just the code implementation of the equation and we looked at it in the theory lecture. Next we calculate the loss and append it to our list of losses. Note how the loss itself isn't actually needed for training the model. It's purely for debugging purposes only the gradient of it is actually needed. Next we create the bigram classes. Note that we only do this if it's the first epoch because of the weights for the count model aren't going to change.

So we only need to calculate these once. Next we print out some debugging information. This is useful so you can make sure that the cost is decreasing at every step. Once we're out of the loop we print the training time. After that we plotted results. The first thing we want to plot is the loss per iteration and as per the exercise I gave you. We also want to plot the loss for the bigram model as a horizontal line. You can think of this as sort of the ideal version of this model.

Next I have a function to calculate the smooth loss. This is because there is going to be quite a bit of variability in the loss as we train the model. Remember that this isn't quite like a normal classification problem with a normal classification problem. You have one input that usually maps to some desired target but for bigram that's not the case when you have an input word. There are many possible next words. So there's no such thing as the correct next word and hence there is no notion of accuracy because of this the loss is going to jump around a lot but we can make it move around less by using exponential smoothing.

We've talked about exponential smoothing in the past but it's not really relevant to this exercise. So if you don't know about it and you want to know more about it please feel free to ask about it on the Q&A. And the last thing we do is once we're done plotting the losses. I also want to plot the probabilities as an image. This allows us to compare visually the probabilities between the logistic model and the bigram model. Theoretically they should be close.

And so these images should look similar. So let's run this and see what we get. All right. So the first thing you want to notice is that this took quite a bit of time and we don't have anything to compare it to so far. But just take note of how long it took. All right. So in this image of the weight comparison is actually pretty interesting. We can see that the bright dots show up in pretty much the same place for both images. So here I'm highlighting some areas where you can see the same pattern in both the left and the right image.

6. Neural Network Bigram Model
==============================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to continue our example of building a bigram model using deep learning techniques. We just did this in logistic regression and we saw how the logistic regression weights are equivalent to the bigram probabilities we found just by counting in this example we're going to extend this idea by using a multilayer neural network instead of just logistic regression.

So let's review how that works. In a simple neural network we now have two layers of weights. Call them W1 and W2 Whereas before, to get the output we take a softmax of W transpose X. Now we have two steps. First we calculate H which is the hidden layer values which is tanh of W1 transpose x. And then we calculate P of y given X from that. So it's softmax of W2 transpose H. Notice that sometimes we use the letter Z for the hidden layer but h is also a conventional choice one neat thing to keep in mind is that the way we represent our data X and Y does not change.

This is due to the fact that all machine learning interfaces are the same. So that's why you can plug in the same data into a random forest or into AdaBoost or any other powerful classifier. How data is represented to these algorithms. Doesn't have to change because the interface is always the same. So just like our logistic model we get p of y given X which we can treat exactly the same as the p of y given X we had before. So now you know how to get the output.

The next step is of course figuring out how to train W1 and W2. We call this the learning phase and by the way just as a general machine learning principle. Again remember that a typical machine learning model always has these two functions. We always have a prediction function and a training function. It doesn't matter if you're writing naive Bayes, k-nearest neighbors or a decision tree they're all going to have the same fit x y and predict functions. So if you recall me saying earlier the P of y given X that we get now is the same as the p of y given x that we got earlier.

This means the way that we calculate our cost. The categorical cross entropy is also the same the only difference now is that we have to update both W1 and W2 And so here I'm showing you these gradients explicitly with respect to either W1 or W2. Now again we've looked at how to calculate these gradients in the past so I hope it's not anything too surprising. We've also translated these in the code before. So if you need an example to work from I'd suggest checking out ann_class/backprop.py. Note that these expressions for the gradient assume are using the tanh activation function.

Also note that we're following this convention that the lowercase character represents a single sample vector and an uppercase character represents a matrix or a batch of those vectors. One question you may have at this point is well why do we bother taking these complicated gradients. Why can't we let TensorFlow Theano do it for us. And the answer to this will become apparent in the next exercise. So just hold that thought for now. We actually will look into some tricks to let us do it as efficiently N.S.A.

and TensorFlow but not that it will actually take more work than just doing it in NumPy directly from the definition. So the short answer is we're going to see how to take advantage of the structure of the data to make us more efficient. But for now you just have to wait using neural networks often requires us to think about hyper parameters. We've already seen one which was the learning rate used for gradient descent but neural networks and deep learning are typically less robust in terms of hyper parameters than linear models like logistic regression.

So we get the added benefit of extra expressiveness but the downside is that we have to be more careful about how we choose these hyper parameters. With the bigram neural network you may have noticed I snuck in a new variable earlier without really talking about it. That is the hidden layer size for this example. We're going to call that D. So W1 is of shape V by D and W2 is of shape D by V where v is the vocabulary size and D is just an arbitrary hyper parameter that we're allowed to choose in our case.

We want to choose these so that it's much smaller than V. There are multiple reasons for this first recall that we've studied auto encoders in the past. Auto encoders are just neural networks that learn to reproduce their input but importantly auto encoders always have this bow tie shape. We call this a bottleneck because we're trying to squeeze in the raw information which is V dimensions into a much smaller space, just D dimensions and we hope to do this without losing any data.

I think we're all intuitively familiar with the concept of compression if I have a very high quality image. It's going to look very nice but it's also going to be a very large file and so on the Web we don't like very large files because it makes our web pages load more slowly. So we have to compress our images so that our pages load faster. The downside to this is that when we compress an image the file size goes down but the quality of the image also goes down.

So we have to find a tradeoff between how much we compress the image and the quality of the image. These days we have very good compression algorithm such as JPEG and MPEG video. These algorithms can compress data at a ratio of about 10 to one or 20 to one without much loss in quality. So one thing you may have noticed about the logistic regression example was that it was very slow. You might think of logistic regression as being faster than a neural network because it's linear and linear is less complicated but here is a great example of an exception to this rule.

Recall that for logistic regression we have a V by V weight matrix. So let's say V is equal to 10 k. So that means our weight Matrix has 10 to the 8 numbers in it. That's 100 million numbers. Now let's look at W1 and W2 if we set D equal to 100 then we have 10 to the 6 plus 10 to the 6 which is equal to 2 million numbers. So by using a neural network we get a 50 to 1 compression ratio. And just like how the logistic regression weights were modeled for the bigram probabilities using counting.

So are the neural network weights you might be wondering how that can be if we have two matrices instead of just one. But the trick is to look at how they combine to get p of y given x. If you write this out what you see is that if you look at it the right way you can see how these combine to give us an approximation of W since X only contains the ones and zeros we can effectively ignore it. And so what we can see is that with a neural network our model of the bigram probability Matrix is just tanh of W1 times W2.

This makes sense because we know that as per the rules of matrix multiplication if we multiply a V by D matrix and a D by V matrix we get out a V by V matrix which is the size of W. So before we move on to the implementation I want you to try to implement what we talked about as an exercise. You can work off of our logistic regression code as a starter template since most of that will remain the same. The only thing you need to do is change the way you make the updates.

Please give this a try before you move on to the next lecture. Good luck.
