8. Improving Efficiency
=======================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in the previous lecture. We looked at how to make our model more efficient by limiting the number of parameters of the model. We saw that surprisingly we could make a neural network that trains faster than logistic regression because of the fact that creating a bottleneck allows us to reduce the number of parameters we have to update. In this lecture we're going to see how we can make this go even faster using some NumPy indexing.

It's one thing to keep in mind is that these indexing tricks are going to be used all throughout this course. So you want to make sure you're comfortable with them before moving on. The first thing we're going to do in this lecture is tackle some inefficiencies in our previous code. One thing you may have noticed is that for every sentence we encountered we would need to create a new one-hot encoded input matrix and the new one-hot encoded target matrix.

If the length of the sentence is N then these two matrices are of size N by V. If you have V equals 10K then you're effectively needing to create 10000 times more data than what you started with. So the question is Is there a way to reduce this inefficiency. So let's suppose I have a three by three weight matrix just containing the numbers 1 through 9. And let's suppose I have a one-hot encoded input vector 1 0 0. The question is what do I get when I multiply this one-hot vector with this weight matrix.

The answer is of course the vector 1 2 3. Now suppose my input vector is 0 1 0. What is the result of multiplying this vector. By this weight matrix the answer is of course 4 5 6 and of course we can do the same example with 0 0 1 as the input vector which gives us 7 8 9 as the output. So what is the pattern here. Let's go back to our first example. 1 0 0. The question is what is this a one-hot encoding of remember that one hot encoding puts a 1 wherever the original input category is.

So if the original input was 0 then we set one_hot_vector of zero equals one. Notice importantly that the result of multiplying this one vector by the weight matrix gave us the zeroth row of the weight matrix. Let's look at the second example again. 0 1 0 is a one hot encoding of the input one. Not surprisingly the result of multiplying this one-hot vector by the weight matrix gave us the row at index 1. The same pattern follows. When we look at our third example 001 is a one encoding of the input 2.

And not surprisingly the result of multiplying this one-hot vector by the weight matrix gave us the row at index 2. So how do we generalize this. Well if we have an input word k where the one-hot vector contains all zeros except the one at index k then when we multiply this one-hot vector by the weight matrix it's the same as just getting the row of the weight matrix. So basically these two code snippets are equivalent. But importantly the second method is much faster than the first and much more memory efficient.

As you know creating new objects in memory takes a lot of time. So we'd rather not do that if possible. So with this method there is no need to create one-hot encodings anymore. This also saves us a lot of space because now instead of having to create an array that contains V values we can just use our existing word index which is just one number now some of you might object to this because as you may have noticed we're not working with just one input word.

We have a whole sentence full of words. So the question is if I have an array of input word indexes which represents a sentence how can I efficiently get the equivalent of multiplying the one hot inputs times W1? It may surprise you to learn that NumPy actually supports selecting multiple rows at the same time in an array. So for example if I do w of 0 1 this is going to give me both a row at index 0 and the row at index 1 if W is of size V by D then the result will be of size 2 by D.

Since there have been two rows selected. Similarly if I do w 0 0 that just gives me a 2 by D array with the row at index 0. Repeat it twice. And finally if I do w of inputs I will get a matrix of size N by D where N is equal to the sentence length. Note that this is the same as if I had multiplied the one hot and coded version of the inputs by the same weight matrix that would have been N by V times V by D. And since the inner dimensions disappear when we do matrix multiplication we get an output of size

N by D. By the way if you're not convinced this works which is totally natural What I'd recommend is doing some simple examples yourself in the console. The best way to learn is to actually have your hands on the keyboard typing. This is not a philosophy course this is a programming course. So start programming and you will see the benefits. So now that you know the basic trick you realize we can pretty much apply this at every step of the code.

So far we've discussed how to get the predictions. So instead of doing that one-hot inputs times W1 we do W1 of inputs and then the rest is done as usual. Since these two methods give you the same result. Another example of where we can do this is calculating the cost function. You may see me do this in the code before. In fact we've been doing it ever since the learning part 2. So instead of one-hot coding the targets and calculating the cost from one-hot targets times the log of predictions we can instead use double indexing where we index both the rows and columns at the same time.

Basically for the rows we index zero to N minus 1 in that order essentially selecting every row one at a time and for the columns we just select the targets. Remember that this is allowed because the one-hot coded targets contained mostly zeros. So they don't actually contribute anything to the result and hence when we multiply them it wastes a lot of time. Anything multiplied by zero is zero. So it's better to just select all the non-zero entries instead.

This is one of the more confusing NumPy notation. So let's break it down. Essentially you are indexing both the rows and columns with two lists of equal length each list contains the corresponding index for that dimension. So for example because the first value in the row index is 1 and the first value in the column index is four then the first output is going to be x[1, 4]. Because the second value in the row index is 2 and the second value in the column index is 5.

And the second output is going to be x[2, 5]. The third place we can apply these improvements is the update equations. If you reason about this in terms of back propagation. You can immediately see which value should be updated and which should not if we have one input word this is going to affect all the hidden values but all the hidden values propagate to all the output values and output values contribute to the error. So this tells us which parameters actually need to be updated for the hidden-to-output weights, all the parameters need to be updated. For the input-to-hidden weights, only the parameters corresponding to the input word need to be updated.

Let's start with the hidden-to-output weights. If you look at the original update equation you'll notice that we have a problem. Originally the targets were one-hot encoded. So both the predictions and the targets were N by V. Now only the predictions are N by V and the targets are a vector of length N containing only word indexes. But again we can use the double indexing trick. Notice that when we subtract the one hot uncoded targets we are mostly subtracting zeros.

In other words they have no effect. We only need to subtract a 1 wherever the corresponding target is which we can achieve using the double indexing trick. The target tells us which columns we want to subtract one from again. If you find this confusing. That's totally normal. The way to debug this and to gain intuition is to try simple examples in code once you can see for yourself how it works. It's easy to understand but no amount of explanation from anywhere else is going to have any effect.

It's not magic it's just programming. Practice makes perfect the most non-trivial part of the updates is how to update W1. Remember that we only need to update the row of W1 that corresponds to the input word. The problem is we're doing batch gradient descent and we're going to have multiple input words and even more problematic is that we have the same input word show up twice. For example in the sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.

The word "the" shows up twice. This isn't much of a problem if it shows up on the right side of the equation because that's just what is getting assigned to the left side. But it's not kosher in programming to do an assignment to W of inputs when the inputs might select the same row of W more than once. Sure you could test the code to see what kind of behavior it has. Maybe the first assignment supersedes the others. Or maybe the last assignment does or maybe they all come together.

Who knows. But generally speaking we don't like ambiguous code like this in programming so it's best not to do it at all. One solution is to just fall back on the slow one-hot encoding method. Actually when you look at the original equation for this you can see why it can't work. Using the indexing method a problem is needed to transpose the one-hot input matrix. But if the input is just a list of word indexes it's not straightforward to transpose it. However since the goal of this lecture is to use the indexing method we would prefer not to do this.

In fact what I'd strongly recommend doing is to compare this method to what I'm going to show you next. Both are not ideal and you'll see why soon but one is faster than the other. The solution is to pretend we are doing stochastic gradient descent and updating one input word at a time. The reason this is not ideal is because we don't like having to do Python for loops which are slow when we can make use of vectorization instead. But in this case we can't use vectorization So we have to do this.

Notice that the error term which I'm calling dhidden is calculated in the same way as the one hot version. So you see that with this loop. The reason we need to do a loop at all is because the thing that indexes W1 is different from the thing that indexes the hidden. For W1 we want to index it using the current word w but for dhidden we want to index it using the corresponding index I which just goes from zero to N minus 1. As usual if you don't immediately understand how this works I'd recommend drawing it out on paper using a simple example.

Use the one and coded version and see what output expression you get. Then compare it with the output expression you get. If you'd used the for loop. And as I mentioned on the last slide neither of these is ideal because in this version you have to do a for loop and in the other version you have to do one-hot coding but it's a great exercise to try and determine which is faster. So with that said I think you know what the next coding exercise is going to be.

You want to take neural_network.py which we just looked at and modify it so that it uses the indexing trick everywhere rather than one-hot vector. Good luck.

9. Improving Efficiency in Code
===============================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at the code for how to use the indexing trick in our bigram neural network the relevant file for this lecture is neural_network2.py. Hopefully you had a chance to try this out for yourself before watching this. Now as with the last code lecture the amount of code changes from the previous file neural_network.py are minimal.

In fact I showed you most of the exact code in the theory lecture. So there isn't too much to discuss here but it's good to review. So let's scroll down to the relevant part. So the first part that changes is that we no longer need to one-hot encode the inputs and targets they just remain as 1D lists. Next. When we get the hidden values in predictions the thing that changes here is we index W1 with the inputs rather than multiplying. So this saves us a lot of computation.

The next thing that changes is how we calculate the loss. So as per the theory lecture we're using this double indexing trick where we index both the rows and columns at the same time. And if you recall this is what we've been using to one-hot encode the inputs and targets in the previous code lectures. The next part is the hard part which is training. Notice how I reversed the loss calculation and the training from the previous scripts before I did the training first and then I calculated the loss which depends only on the predictions.

In this case I need to switch them because of how we need to calculate the gradient for W2. Remember that I can no longer do predictions minus targets because Target is no longer one-hot coded. So what I want to do is subtract one from the relevant parts of the predictions Matrix. But when I do the line doutput equals predictions remember that in Python this only copies a reference. So both the output and predictions now point to the same array in memory.

Hence if I modify doutput, predictions will also be modified and since I need predictions to calculate the loss I can't do that after since I just modified the predictions one work around would be to call the copy function. But as you can imagine that adds some slowness to the code that isn't necessary. So instead we just calculate the loss first and then we can freely modify the predictions array. The next hard part is updating W1 as we discussed in the slides we can't use the inputs list directly because what we actually need is the transpose of the one hot and coded inputs.

So we're forced to do this loop over the inputs. But luckily this gives us the same answer. Hopefully you had a chance to work it out on paper. And by the way I left some code in here so that you could compare the one hot version and see which one is faster. So hopefully you had a chance to test this and you know which method is faster. The next thing that changes is that we can of course apply the same trick to the original bigram model. If you recall we converted this to logistic regression where we can also use the same indexing tricks for both the predictions and the loss.

And since we know that all machine learning interfaces are the same. We know that the rest of this code doesn't change from what we had before. So let's run this and see what we get. Notice how this is our fastest version yet we get a huge speed up because we're not wasting time multiplying and adding a bunch of zeros anymore. We also get that initial speed up from using a neural network which reduces the parameters in the model. And because this is numerically equivalent to the first neural network we wrote the results aren't going to change this code improves the implementation but it doesn't change the model itself.

### 4. Word Embeddings and Word2Vec

1. Return of the Bigram
=======================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this section of the course. We are going to look at the word2vec algorithm. If you've been following along so far then you are in a great position to learn about word2vec. This is because everything we're going to do in this section is strongly dependent on the previous sections. In fact it's going to appear like we're only making an incremental improvement on what we've already done.

But if you haven't been following along or you try to skip ahead to this section without covering the previous sections first please go back and do them without the previous sections. This section is not going to make much sense. OK so let's get started. First what did we look at in the Review section. The review section focused on creating a language model using bigrams recall that bigram model is just a model of the probability of the next word. Given the current word crucially we use a neural network to accomplish second what did we cover in the introductory section on word embeddings where we looked at how to work with Word vectors.

Now I ask you to remember something very important in this section. Do you remember what it is I asked you to remember that a word embedding Matrix is just a matrix of size V by D. There are all kinds of ways to find the actual numbers that go into this matrix like word counting TF-IDF and so on. But wait a minute. If you look at our bigram neural network model what do we see. Well what we see is exactly a matrix that could be used as a word embedding.

In fact this neural network has two matrices of size V by D because the input is size V and the output is also of size V. OK so what's the important lesson here. Well it's that if you want to use neural networks to find word embeddings look no further than the bigram model which conveniently We already know how to implement. Now you might ask is this bigram model going to give us good word vectors. And of course how good or bad they are is another story entirely.

In fact I'll tell you right now that they are not that good but that's what this section is all about. I want you to understand that word2vec is not some mysterious algorithm instead word2vec is just a very simple extension on something you already know how to do namely creating a bigram model using a neural network in the next few lectures. We'll look at what these extensions are and then how to implement them.

2. CBOW
=======

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at one extension on the simple bigram that makes it into a word2vec algorithm. This is called CBOW. CBOW stands for continuous bag of words. The basic idea behind CBOW is this instead of having just the one input word to try to predict the next word. Instead we're going to have a whole list of words trying to predict a word in the middle.

Now that might sound very complicated and abstract to some of you. So let's do an example. Suppose I have a sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. So an example of a training sample for CBOW would be input "brown", "fox", "over", "the" and output "jumps". So you can see that I'm trying to predict the middle word "jumps" from the surrounding words. Brown fox and over the in this example I would say that my context size is two because I've gone two words to the left and two words to the right.

So the actual context in this case would be double the context size which actually sounds a bit odd when you say it that way. But depending on which author you're reading they may refer to the context size is four, meaning that the total number of surrounding words is four. In practice a context size somewhere between five to 10 inclusive is usually what is used. This is of course a hyper parameter that may require some tuning. Another thing that might not be immediately clear is the input weight W1 is the same shared weight for all the input words.

This is just the input weight in the background. Repeat it however many times you have a context word. So let me give you some intuition on why this kind of model might make sense if you recall. This is not the first time we've seen this. If you've taken my easy course if you haven't taken that course don't worry since I'm going to explain everything on this page. So one of the projects we did was called article spinning. If you don't know what article spinning is let me give you a quick background in Internet marketing.

It has been said that content is king as a general rule. If your web site has a lot of articles on it then it probably has a lot of information which means it's probably useful for a lot of people. And if it's useful for a lot of people then it will rank higher in search engines like Google which means you'll get more traffic to your site and so on. But of course internet marketers try to capitalize on this and instead of writing their own content they try to steal other people's content.

Of course Google is smart and Google knows how to catch this sort of thing. And so if your site contains duplicate content instead of doing better in search rankings you will instead be penalized. And that's no good. So what is an ethically dubious internet marketer to do. Well one obvious idea is something many people have tried which is to take an existing article and replace parts of it so that it uses different words but has the same meaning if you think back to your grade school days you may realize that this idea is not new at all.

In fact schoolchildren everywhere are doing this all the time. If you change an article just enough the fact that it came from somewhere else becomes undetectable. And so that's the idea behind article spinning. Of course this is very time consuming. And so we would like for there to be a way to automate this process. You want to replace certain words or phrases with different words or phrases with the same meaning so that they don't count as duplicate content.

Now what's a very simple way of doing this. Consider the sentences I walk to the store and I went to the store. These more or less have the same meaning if I replaced one sentence with the other in a story I'm writing it would essentially have no effect on how that story is interpreted which is what I want. So one simple way of making these modifications is to come up with a language model where the current word is not only conditioned on the previous word as in a bigram but on the next word as well.

So I'd have some probability distribution over my vocabulary given that the previous word was I and the next word was to and if you think about it this is exactly like our CBOW model with the context size of one. And as usual I could find these probabilities just by counting or by using a neural network which is what we're doing in this case. OK so I think this concept is pretty simple so you probably don't need any more examples. Let's now dig into the model itself and the details of how it really works.

So that you have some idea of how you would implement it. By the way note that in this course we won't be implementing the CBOW variant of word2vec since other methods have been shown to work better and I think it's good to start with CBOW because it's the most intuitive. This picture actually gives you a pretty good idea of what's going on. So for each word in the context we grab it's word embedding we call that W1 of c, just the c-th row of W1.

And this is probably not clear from the picture. We take the mean of all those vectors or in other words we add them all up and divide by the number of words there are. Now you can see why we call this bag of words. It's just like the bag of words classifier we worked on previously. But in this case we are not yet done. This only gives us the value of the hidden layer. Let's call this h. As you know we still need to predict the middle word. So in order to do that I take H.

Multiply it by the head into output weight matrix W2 and then perform a softmax to get the output prediction one very important thing to notice here is that there is no hidden activation function normally in deep learning. We do use a hidden activation in order to make the model nonlinear. But in all of the word2vec algorithms nonlinear hidden activation functions are not used. What's interesting about this is that both word2vec and GloVe are actually both linear models.

They are part of the deep learning family but there is actually nothing deep about the models. They are based on. So that's everything for CBOW. It's really just two simple steps. Each sample is a set of context words as input and the target is the middle word. The model is simply that we take the mean of the word embeddings of the context words multiply it by the second weight matrix and do the softmax like we would for any other classifier out there. While we could talk about how to train this model.

We're not going to do that just yet. You could try to derive the gradient for yourself as an exercise but we won't be implementing this so it would be for your interest only.

3. Skip-Gram
============

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning and Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to talk about yet another way to extend the bigram called the skip-gram. What you'll see is that the skip-gram is kind of the opposite of what we just learned about which was CBOW. Let's get right into the details so you see what I mean. So let's get back to our example sentence The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.

Recall that for CBOW we take "brown", "fox", "over", "the" as the input while we try to predict the middle word jumps with skip-gram we do the opposite. We instead take the word "jumps" as input and we try to predict all four words. Brown fox and over the. Now that probably doesn't make a whole lot of sense to you. So let me try to explain it in terms we already know about take our simple bigram model one training sample. This model would be the input jumps and the target over that makes sense because we do the softmax at the end and we try to predict one word with the skip-gram.

We do the same thing except we can pretend that now there are just three more training samples for a total of four training samples. So in addition to jumps going to over we also have jumps going to brown, jumps going to fox and jumps going to the. So this is just like our bigram neural network which is adding more training samples to it in particular Instead of having just bigrams. We're also adding pairs of words that come from the context. And the term skip-gram is because it's like bigram except we skip a few words in between.

So take a minute to let that sink in. We essentially have two different ways of looking at the same problem. The first way is to think of one sample as one word with four targets. That means you have a shared output weight W2 that repeats four times you have four softmax and four predictions and the total error is of course just the sum of the four individual errors. But the other way to think about it and I think this is much simpler is to think of it as four different samples each with one input word and one target word in the end you still end up summing the individual errors because you're doing batch gradient descent.

So mathematically these two are equivalent. Now again I want to remind you of something we first encountered in the Review section here. Normally when we build a neural network classifier we're trying to get good accuracy. In other words for a certain input I'm always trying to predict a certain target but in this case that actually doesn't make any sense. And this is because I have a single input and I'm trying to make it hit multiple different targets.

So I want to remind you that with language modeling the notion of accuracy is meaningless. There is no such thing as good accuracy because a single word can be pointing to any number of other words. And we can see that directly now. Even with just a single sample. So just as with CBOW we're still going to avoid talking about the second part of this process which is how to train the model. Well you could at this point try to derive the gradients for yourself.

We won't because there is still one modification we need to make in order to make this model work better. But unlike CBOW which we won't be using we will be using skip-gram. So keep this in mind as we move on to the next lecture.

4. Hierarchical Softmax
=======================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to talk about yet another modification we need to make in order to get our final word2vec model. And this is called hierarchical softmax. Let's talk a little bit about the motivation behind hierarchical softmax. In any language model where we're trying to predict a word then our output which we have to do a softmax over is going to have a very large dimensionality.

The number of outputs has to be of size V which is the size of our vocabulary. This can get out of hand quite easily since as you've seen our GloVe vectors used the vocabulary size of 400000 and our pre-trained word2vec vectors use the vocabulary size of three million. You don't have to worry about such large numbers in this course since we ourselves will limit our vocabulary to be much smaller than this. For educational purposes. So what's the problem with having a huge number of output classes.

First is that we just know empirically based on experimental evidence that this negatively affects accuracy. But mathematically what could be wrong with this. Well consider that a softmax picks only one class at a time out of three million. That's what classification means. It means out of the number of classes we have. Pick the right one. So for each sample we're saying one word is the target and the other two million nine hundred ninety nine thousand nine hundred ninety nine become the wrong answer.

So that's a two with six nines if you're counting. I suppose that each word is equally represented at the target. What does that mean. Well it means that ninety nine point nine nine nine nine seven percent of the time any given word is not the target. The proportion of time that a word spends being the target is just 1 out of three million. So in other words we spend the majority of the time pushing the answer away from words than we do trying to get it to go towards the right answer.

Also consider the formula for softmax. It says that whenever I want to compute the average probability I need to first exponentiate each output node and then sum over all the values as you know taking the sum is O of V. And so if V is large then that's going to make things extremely slow. In any case this is all very hypothetical. So for simplicity's sake just remember that when you have a huge number of output classes it tends not to work as well.

So there are two solutions to this but we are going to spend the majority of our efforts focusing only on the method that's been shown to work best in either of these solutions. What we want to do is get rid of these softmax entirely the first solution which is the one we won't focus on is called hierarchical softmax. Please note that since we won't be implementing this in code this part of the lecture is entirely optional. I'm only talking about it for those who are interested but you can feel free to skip it.

Since the second method is the one we'll be implementing so for a hierarchical softmax the basic idea is this instead of trying to predict whether or not the output word belongs to one of the three million classes all at the same time. Let's instead just break it down into a binary tree. So for example one side might be responsible for 1 million words and the other side might be responsible for the other 2 million then 2 million could further be split into 1 million on the left and 1 million on the right and at every node we keep splitting into two until eventually we get to a single word.

So every leaf node in this tree represents a word. And this also means that every path we don't go down does not need to be considered. So here are some important facts about hierarchical softmax. First since at every node we have to make a binary decision we can use the sigmoid for this. This tells us the probability that we should go right or left. Typically we would say that the sigmoid outputs the probability of going right. So then the probability of going left is just one minus that.

Secondly also note that in order to get the final probability of a single word represented by a leaf. All we need to do is multiply all the individual probabilities along the path of the tree from the root to that node. Since this is a probability tree we know that all the probabilities at the leaf nodes will sum to one. You can check this intuitively by doing a simple example. So here we have a cat, raccoon, dog and apple. Probability of cat is just 0.4 probability of raccoon is 0.6 times 0.7 which is 0.42, probability of dog is 0.6 times 0.3 times 0.1 which is 0.018, and the probability of apple is 0.162.

And if you check these all sum to one as the softmax should. So that's why we say the hierarchical softmax is an approximation to the softmax. The third point is since we now have a binary tree. There is no longer any need for a D by V output matrix. This huge matrix also leads to slowness in the naive model the input-to-hidden matrix which is also of size V by D, is not a problem. Why is that. Well the reason is when we have an input word all we do is use that as an index into the input-to-hidden matrix.

We discussed this in the review. So you may want to check that again if you forgot. But for the output it's necessary to do a full matrix multiply. Because we're working with a dense matrix and a dense vector. The nice thing about hierarchical softmax is at every node. All we need to do is compute a scalar. So we just need a single vector of size D. Now because the binary tree has all of the leaf nodes it's also going to have all of the output weight vectors of size D.

So every split in the tree has its own D-size vector. But the trick is most of these we never need to multiply with because we don't go down that path in the tree. In fact the number of operations we have to do is now O of log V since this is essentially a binary search you realize that up until now we haven't actually talked about how this tree is formed. In fact the way that the tree is formed has a big impact on performance so it's quite important I've linked some papers in extra_reading.txt if you want to read some more but this is 100 percent optional.

So one final trick I want to talk about in regards to the hierarchical softmax is one good way of finding that tree. We know that some words like "the", "a", "in" and so on are going to show up very frequently in our data. And in fact the is the most frequent word in the English language. Whereas words like microprocessor programming and [unclear] will be very infrequent. So for more common words we would like to not have to go that far down the tree but for uncommon words we don't mind having to go far down the tree because that's just not going to happen very often.

And so we can use a technique called Huffman coding which puts frequent words closer to the top and infrequent words further down at the bottom. Huffman coding has tons of applications in computer science. So you're definitely encouraged to read about it when you get the chance.

5. Negative Sampling
====================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 OK so hierarchical softmax is pretty complicated. So let's move on to the second method which is comparatively much simpler. This technique is called negative sampling. The basic idea is this. I talked about earlier how when we use the softmax basically every word is going to be a non-target over 99 percent of the time. So with softmax what we're saying is every word that's not the target is going to be considered wrong.

What negative sampling does is instead of saying every other word is wrong. We simply take a sample of these other words and say that some of them are wrong. Let's look at exactly how this is implemented. So you get a clearer picture. Firstly I want you to consider the difference between multiclass classification and binary classification with multiclass classification and we normally use the softmax and then we use the multiclass cross entropy as our objective.

What I've written here is the positive form. So this is just the log likelihood with binary classification we use the sigmoid and we use the binary cross entropy as our costs. Again I've written the positive form here just to save space. Note that these are all the equations you've seen before but now it's in the context of a bigram neural network model instead of just a general input feature X. So for these softmax entropy The objective is just target

times log of the output for binary cross entropy. The objective is target times log of the output plus one minus target times log one minus output. Nothing fancy. So the idea behind negative sampling is instead of doing softmax and doing the full softmax cross entropy. Just forget about softmax. Let's instead do a bunch of little binary cross entropies. So let's go back to our example for quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog: the input word is "jumps". The targets are brown fox and over the and the negative samples are just randomly sampled words in our vocabulary.

Remember that you can randomly select things in an array using the function np.random.choice. So let's just say for argument's sake the negative samples are apple orange boat and Tokyo. So if we were to write this out explicitly we would first calculate the following outputs: p of brown given jumps, p of fox given jumps, p of over given jumps, p of the given jumps, p of apple given jumps, p of orange given jumps, p of boat given jumps and p of Tokyo given jumps as you can see these are all just the sigmoid of W2 times W1 indexed by the output word and the input word.

OK so now we have these eight posterior probabilities. What do we do with them. Well of course now we can calculate the binary cross entropy. So we know that for brown fox over and "the" these are all positive samples. We know that apple orange boat and Tokyo are negative samples. So the full expression for the binary cross entropy becomes what you see here again I've dropped the negative sign for convenience. Notice how when we already know the labels for each word we don't need to specify the t's or the one minus T's because we know those are always going to evaluate to either 1 or 0.

Now of course we can generalize this equation to make it a little more compact. If we let a big C represent our set of context words and a big n represent our set of negative samples then the cost becomes what you see here. Note that on these screens only when we use the letters capital N and capital C for this purpose generally speaking these letters are typically used for other things. But I think for this slide these letters work well. Now if you recall one identity we have when it comes to the sigmoid is that if we negate the logit we get the opposite probability.

So if p of y equals 1 given X is the sigmoid of the logit then p of y equals zero given X is the sigmoid of the negative logit. And so sigmoid of logit plus sigmoid of minus logit is equal to 1. And so we can write the cost even more compactly by replacing the one minus sigmoid term. This may or may not be useful for you in your implementation. Now for the previous lectures we didn't bother to calculate the gradient because we weren't going to implement those algorithms but negative sampling is something we will be implementing.

And so in this lecture we will derive the gradients. Luckily since it's just binary classification and there's no non-linearity. This is quite easy. This derivation is most closely related to what you learn and deep learning part 1. So if you don't remember how to do back propagation You may want to revisit the review section of this course. So in actuality it's easier to work with the raw form of the binary cross entropy here where we just have the usual t times log p plus one minus t times log of one minus p. Here I'm using

p as shorthand for p of y equals one given x, where y can refer to any other word and notice that I've added back the negative sign here. So you can see the derivation a little more clearly. Note also that the little n here now refers to the sample index and big N refers to the total number of samples in this batch. Unlike how we used N earlier in this lecture. So just keep that in mind now big N refers to what we traditionally use it for which is the number of samples.

The first thing you remember from back propagation is the derivative of the cost with respect to W2 is just h transposed times P minus T. Remember that this derivative is the same derivative that we got when we do binary logistic regression. So we can always go back to the file logistic4.py in the folder logistic_regression_class to confirm that this is the case. And h here just refers to the value at the hidden layer. Note that as usual we're using capital letters to refer to the full array of things.

So big P means a vector of all of the N output probabilities Whereas little p of n means the upper probability for the nth sample. So this is all currently in very general terms and our specific case. What is h? Well h just refers to the word vector for the input word. And in fact unlike the general case where we have an N by D matrix, h is just a D-size vector because it's just the word vector for the single input word. This might seem a little confusing at first.

Here's why. We know that h which is just W1 of input is a size vector and we know that P minus T is an N-size vector. So it doesn't seem like we can multiply these because they are two vectors of different sizes. It helps to think about just a single sample. See what we really want to do. So suppose we have one sample. So N is 1 then what we want to do is multiply W1 input which is a D-size vector, by P minus T which is now a scalar. Since it's a scalar multiplied by a vector.

This is allowed. Now what's the update we want to do here. Well we don't want to update the entirety of W2 because the entirety of W2 does not influence its output. In fact only W2 of output affects the current output word whatever it may be. And by the way it could be a positive sample or a negative sample. We don't care because that's handled by T. If it's a positive sample then T is 1, otherwise it's 0. So this makes sense. The gradient is W1 input times P minus T, which is a D-size vector. The vector which this gradient belongs to is W2 output.

Now what happens if we have N samples? We don't have to think so abstractly. Let's just say we have three samples then we just have three output vectors to update which are all slices of W2. So you can see here that we have three vector equations. Now theoretically you could just use a python for loop to calculate these updates. But of course we do not like Python for loops. So how can we further vectorize this operation. Well I claim that this is just the outer product of W1 input and P minus T. Note how the result would be a D by N matrix and the slice of W2 that we need to update is also D by N the full W2 is of size D by V.

And we just selected N columns out of V corresponding to either the context words or the negative samples if you want to prove to yourself that this works. I'd recommend doing a simple example on paper just as a reminder if you don't remember how outer products work. Suppose you're given two vectors a and b indexed by i and j respectively; then the outer product of a and b is a matrix C where C_ij is equal to a_i times b_j. In other words, the entry at the (i, j)th position in C is just the i-th element of a multiplied by the j-th

element of b. Ok so now we have the update for W2. What about the update for W1. Well this is both easier and harder. It's easier because only one row of W1 needs to be updated. The row that belongs to the input word it's harder because we need to apply the chain rule the further back you go in a neural network the more terms you need to consider. Again one simple way to look at this problem is to just make use of work we've already done. So for example you can look back to the deep learning part 1 and look up what the update equation is for the input-to-hidden weight.

Of course we've already reviewed this in the review section as well and alternative is you could just derive from scratch since it looks a little different from a full multilayer neural network. The important thing to remember is that there is no hidden activation function which is equivalent to the activation function being identity. Which means its derivative is one. I think the easiest way to do it is just to do it from scratch. So firstly we need to show that the derivative of J up to the activation a_n is p_n minus t_n. We've used this derivative many times in the past so I'm sure that by now it's not a problem for you.

Recall that even the activation or logit is just the dot product between the input vector and the output vector. And in this case the word vector. So once we've shown this we can just apply the chain rule to figure out the rest of the expression so we can plug in p_n minus t_n again and we can plug in the dot product for that. And we know that the derivative of a dot product is just the other vector that's not being differentiated with respect to. So we get a very simple expression for the derivative and we can fully vectorize this in code.

Notice how this makes a lot of sense. The gradient of W1 for the input word is dependent on the error at all N output nodes and the weight going to those output nodes. So at this point we've gone through a lot of material without doing any coding so let's summarize everything we've done so far. Firstly remember that everything we've discussed is just a modification on the simple neural network program we studied in the review section in this section we looked at two different modeling modifications we could make.

The first was called CBOW or continuous bag of words. And it means that instead of using the current word to predict the next word we instead use the entire context meaning a window surrounding a middle word to predict the middle word. The second method reverses the direction of CBOW and uses the middle word to predict all the context words. We call this the skip-gram. Next we looked at two different approximations to the softmax output which helps to speed up our calculations.

First we looked at hierarchical softmax which turns a big matrix multiply into a binary tree with tiny dot products at each level. Second we looked at negative sampling where instead of treating every word that's not the target into a non-target we just pick a few. So in total. Research has shown that the skip-gram along with negative sampling is what works best. So in the next few lectures that's what we're going to implement.

6. Negative Sampling - Important Details
========================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 at this point we're done discussing the mechanical aspects of negative sampling. But I'm sure you still have some burning questions. In fact these details are critical to the actual implementation. So let's look at what they are. The first obvious question is how many negative samples should you choose. The answer is normally people choose a number like five or 10 or even as high as 25.

This is a hyper parameter so you should always optimize it to your particular dataset. The second question is how do you actually choose the negative samples you may have assumed that we would select them uniformly. But that's actually not the case. Instead we sample from a modified unigram distribution. But that probably sounds complicated. So let's explain what is a unigram distribution and why is it being modified. Firstly a unigram distribution is exactly what you would expect it to be.

If you take into account that you already know what a bigram distribution is a unigram distribution is just the distribution for a single word. So a unigram distribution is just the probability of a single word occurring. We can just call that p of w. How can we estimate p of w as usual we can estimate it by counting P of w is just the number of times w appears in our corpus divided by the total number of tokens in the corpus. So that's a unigram distribution but we are not done yet.

The problem with this distribution is that infrequent words are too infrequent and so they are very unlikely to ever be sampled. We can smooth out this distribution by raising all of our counts to the power 0.75 0.75 is the value people usually use. But I found that you can change this number pretty drastically and still get pretty reasonable results. And the fact that this is not the true maximum likelihood estimate of a unigram distribution is why we call it the modified unigram distribution. Third question this one's going to seem a little odd.

Consider our example sentence again. The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog jumps. Is the input word and a brown fox and over the are the context words. But what if during my negative sampling procedure I accidentally sample one of the context words. So for example "the" is pretty frequent. So I might sample it again in my negative samples in word2vec. This generally doesn't matter and so if we do end up considering one of the context words as a positive sample and a negative sample at the same time so be it it would actually take a lot of work to renormalize the negative sampling distribution for each context.

So we simply don't bother. The final little detail concerning negative sampling. I want to talk about this. Earlier I told you that for negative sampling we take a middle word some context words which count as positive samples and then we pick some negative samples randomly from our vocabulary. But in the actual implementation this is not what we're going to do. Instead we're going to do something a little simpler. In particular we still have our middle word and we still have our contacts.

So for our running example we still have four positive pairs. We have jumps going to brown, jumps going to fox, jumps going to over and jumps going to the but instead of coming up with negative samples for our context we instead just take one negative sample for the middle word. Let me show you what I mean. So let's say we sample a single random word. Let's say it's lighthouse. So now we have four new negative samples using this random word as the middle word.

But with the existing context so our negative samples are lighthouse going to brown, lighthouse going to fox, lighthouse going to over and Lighthouse going to the. OK so just to reiterate this idea. Usually when people talk about negative sampling they talk about it in terms of sampling negative context words with a fixed middle word. But during implementation we're going to fix the context words and insert an incorrect middle word. So to summarize this lecture this lecture focused on a few hidden implementation details regarding negative sampling that make the word2vec algorithm work better in the coming lectures.

We'll look at even more implementation tricks not related to negative sampling that also help the performance of word2vec.

7. Why do I have 2 word embedding matrices and what do I do with them
=====================================================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. So we've talked a lot about the bigram neural network and the word2vec algorithm. But if you just think back to the basic idea here. All we want to do is find a V by D matrix that has good word vectors. If you look at the architecture of a biograph neural network you realize it has two of these. The first is V by D and the second is D by V. Of course if you just transpose the second matrix you get a V by D matrix.

So really you have two V by D matrices here. Which one is the one we are interested in. In reality either of these could serve as our final word vector's. I'll show you what people tend to do. And you can try these out for yourself. So the first way is just to forget about the second Matrix entirely and just make the word embedding equal to the input-to-hidden weight. The second way is to concatenate the two weights together. So I can concatenate W1 and W2 transpose.

Of course this makes the new dimensionality of each vector 2D instead of just D. The third way is just to take the average of the two weights. So I add up W1 and W2 transpose and divide by two. In all three of these cases. Sometimes people normalize all the vectors so that they have unit length. This also makes it so they all lie on the unit sphere. Of course if you're using cosine distance to find analogies this actually wouldn't make a difference.

Now I know somebody is eventually going to ask me. You know that's nice that there are three ways to do this but which one is the best. And as usual I can't tell you what's going to work best for your particular data set and your particular problem. The best objective is the objective that you want to maximize or minimize. So it's important for you to do experiments and test what kind of results you get.

8. Word2Vec implementation tricks
=================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at a few implementation tricks that can be useful when you're implementing word2vec. Note that we're not going to use all of these. But I think it's useful to discuss what they are so that you have some idea of the tuning researchers have done over time. Also I want you to realize that by this point you already understand word2vec. What we're about to discuss are just details.

So if you don't care about the implementation or you just want to try it using what you know so far then you can skip this lecture. So the first thing we're going to look at is how to choose which words to train on in the English language common words are very common and uncommon words are very uncommon. If you plotted a histogram of how often words are used you'd get a very fat tailed distribution. That means we'd spend an overwhelming majority of the time updating word vectors for very common words.

If we just ran through every sentence so what we do in word2vec is we just drop a bunch of words and we do so randomly. So each time we encounter a sentence we randomly drop some words according to some probability distribution. What is this distribution, you might ask? Well it's actually something we've seen before. The modified unigram distribution, or more accurately it's a function of the modified unigram distribution. So what we do is we set up the probability of dropping a word equal to one minus the square root of a threshold divided by the modified unigram distribution.

A typical threshold will be set very low something like 10 to the minus five. So what is the effect of this let's say a word is very frequent. Let's say the probability of this unigram is 10 percent or 0.1. Then we have 1 minus the square root of 10 to the minus 4 which is 1 minus 10 to the minus 2 which is 0.9 9. So a 99 percent chance we should drop that word. Now say that unigram probability is 10 to the minus five then we get 1 minus one equals zero.

So we would never drop that word. In fact if the unigram probability is even lower than this we get a negative number meaning we also don't want to drop that word. Now again this is one of those things that isn't obvious. But after we drop the words from the sentence we treat the sentence with dropped words as the final sentence that we're going to train on. So for example if our sentence is the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. Suppose we drop the words "the" and "over", the effective sentence becomes quick brown fox jumps lazy dog.

So now if our middle word is jumps then our context words become brown fox and lazy dog. Note that before dropping any words the words lazy and dog would not be part of this context. So doing things this way technically has the effect of widening the context window. The next implementation detail. I want to talk about is learning rate scheduling. In word2vec we normally pick a maximum learning rate and a minimum learning rate and then we reduce the learning rate linearly from the maximum to the minimum.

Now this one is pretty common so I don't suspect that this is a surprising implementation detail. The next few implementation details I want to discuss are things we are not going to implement. These would take a large amount of effort without really improving your understanding of word2vec. So they are good just to know so that you can know about them. The first thing is that you can greatly speed up word2vec by using multiple threads or multiple cores.

In other words using parallel processing. So for example if you have four threads and training on one thread takes four hours. Then if you use 4 threads that will take 1 hour. Another thing you can do is you can do more of the work in C. A lot of the work in word2vec is happening in Python which is a slow language. So one thing you could try is running certain functions in Cython and/or calling C functions, and in fact the original implementation of word2vec is entirely in C.

9. Word2Vec implementation outline
==================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to do an outline of the word2vec code we are about to write. This outline is going to be the same for all of our implementations. So whether you're writing the code in pure NumPy or TensorFlow the same steps are going to be followed. So I think that at a high level you should have a pretty good idea of what we're going to do by now.

It's the same as pretty much every machine learning scripts we write. Step number one is load in the data. Step Number 2 is build and train the model. Step number three is test the model. We already know what we need to do for step 1 and step 3 because we've done them in previous lectures for example to load in the data. What you want is to turn each sentence into a list of integer word indexes and also to return a word to index mapping in order to test the model.

We're going to print out some word analogies. So the bulk of this lecture is going to focus on step two how to build and train the model. The first thing we need to do of course is initialize our parameters. These of course take the form of two weight matrices, one of size V by D and the other of size D by V. The next thing we can do is get our modified unigram distribution. Remember that this is going to be used for negative sampling and it's going to be used to set P drop which we're going to use for dropping words in each sentence as we encounter them.

Next we're going to have three nested loops. First we loop through the epochs and then we loop through each sentence inside each sentence. We're going to first drop some words from that sentence according to p_drop. Once we've done that we can loop through each word in the sentence as we loop through each word. We treat that word as the middle word and the surrounding words as context. Note that the context isn't going to be the same size every time. So for example if your middle word is the first word in a sentence then your context only comes from words on the right.

So you probably want to write a function to grab the context that makes sure you don't go out of bounds on the sentence. Once you get the context you want to grab a word to use as your negative word. Now you have all three things you need to do gradient descent: your positive middle word, your negative middle word and the context. Note that this is more like stochastic gradient descent because we're really only processing a single sample at a time.

Although having multiple contexts words makes it look like a batch. Now it's useful to have a function to perform stochastic gradient descent since it needs to do multiple things. So let's take a look at how that's going to work. So in our sgd function we do the following First of course we need to get the predictions which are output probabilities for the context words Notice something interesting here which is that our label is passed in as an input argument.

Recall that we're doing binary classification. So this is always going to be 1 or 0. But importantly this same function can be used for both positive and negative samples. So the second thing we have to do is get the gradients which we've discussed how to derive. Finally we update the relevant parts of W 1 and W2 with their respective gradients. We can also use this function to return the cost simultaneously. Since we just calculated the prediction that is.

So that's your basic outline for the code. It's actually quite simple in principle but it's also going to turn out to be hundreds of lines of code. So it's good to review how it's going to be structured.

10. Word2Vec in Code with Numpy
===============================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. You're going to look at how to implement word2vec using only Python and NumPy. The relevant file in the course repo is word2vec.py. At the top we have all of our imports most of which we've seen already in this course. After that we have a function to remove punctuation. This is useful for the tokenization process which is what's coming next.

So now we have a function get_wiki which is going to load in the Wikipedia data in this function and we choose a vocabulary size of twenty thousand. Note that you can choose another value if you like to give you a short outline of this function. We need to loop through our set of files twice the first time we loop through. We just want to count up the number of times each word appears. This is so that when we limit the vocabulary size to 20000 we keep the most common words in order to know which words are the most common.

We need to count them once we've counted them then we can loop through the files one more time and store the sentences by encoding only the top 20000 words. So when the first loop we loop through each textfile that starts with the name enwiki then we loop through each line in each file first we check if that line starts with a special character. If it does it's usually a title or some meta data so we skip those lines. Next we tokenized the line first by removing punctuation then by lower casing and then by splitting on whitespace if after all that processing the line has more than one token we update the count for all the tokens in that line.

At this point we have the counts. Now we can set our vocab size to be the minimum of 20000 and the actual vocabulary size in the Corpus. This is because if the actual corpus contained fewer words then we don't need to waste space storing 20000 items. Next we need to sort our word_counts dictionary by value so we can use the python sorted function which gives us a list of tuples containing words and their counts in descending order. Next we select the top 20000 words and we reserve a space for a special unknown token which all the low frequency words will be assigned to.

So if any word didn't make it into our top 20000 list it will be considered unknown. Next we create a word2idx dictionary so that we can map each word to an integer from 0 to 20000. After that we have our final loop where we loop through our data set one last time. This time we use our word2idx mapping to map each sentence to a list of integers using the unknown token for any infrequent words. Once we're done we return our list of sentences and our word2idx mapping. Next we have the most important function in this file, train_model.

So this is basically going to follow the outline we talked about in the last lecture. The first thing we do in this function is get the data which is our function we just made, get_wiki. Next we get the vocab size. Note that I'm using the actual variable name vocab size because I want to use the letter V to represent the hidden-to-output weight. I think calling them W and V is a little simpler in code than writing W1 and W2 which takes up more space.

Next we have some hyper parameter configurations. So we're going to use a context size of 5 a maximum learning rate of 0.025 a minimum learning rate of zero point zero zero zero one number of negative samples equals five number of epochs equals 20 and latent dimensionality equals 50. Next we set the delta for the learning rate after each epoch, which is the max minus min divided by number of epochs. Next we initialize W and V. After that we get p_neg, which is our modified unigram distribution.

We'll look at the implementation of this function later. We can also define p_drop at this point since it won't change from one epoch to the next. Next we enter the main training. First we loop over the number of epochs. Inside each epoch we shuffle the sentences. Then we loop through each sentence for each sentence. We first randomly drop words in a sentence. according to p_drop. Then we check how long the sentence is. It must have two or more words.

Otherwise any middle word won't have a context. Next we determine in what order to look at the middle words. We do this randomly for the same reason we shuffle the sentences on each epoch. Next we loop through each middle word in a modified sentence or more accurately we loop through the positions for each position and we get the word. Then we get the context using the function get_context. We'll be looking at the implementation of this later. Next we get the negative word using the distribution defined by p_neg. Next

We do two iterations of gradient descent once for the positive word and one for the negative word with a target of 1 and 0 respectively. Again we'll be looking at the implementation of this function later. Although you've pretty much already seen it at this point we also accumulate the cost. Every one hundred iterations we print how many sentences we've processed and once each epoch is done we do a final printout showing the cost and we decrease the learning rate once all the training is done.

We plot the cost per iteration. Finally I found this really helpful for debugging. Once training is done we save our weight so that we can test them later without training again. So to do this we're going to make a folder and inside this folder we want to save two things we want to save the weights W and V. But we also want to save the word2idx mappings so we know which slice of each weight matrix corresponds to which word in our vocabulary once that's done we return word2idx, W and V. Next we have a function to get the negative sampling distribution.

All we need to do here is basically count up the number of occurrences of each word raise it to the power of 0.75 and then normalize the whole thing. So it's a valid probability distribution. Next we have a function to get the context in a sentence given the middle word. This is useful because the starting point can either be the middle words position minus the context size. But if that's negative then it should just be zero. Similarly the ending point can either be the middle words position plus the context size.

But if that's greater than the length of the sentence it should just be the length of the sentence. Finally we collect all the words from start to end leaving out the middle word and that's the context. Next we have an sgd function which is pretty much the same thing you've already seen. Recall that the same function works whether the label is 1 or 0. So the first thing we need to do is calculate the output probability which is just W[input] dot V[:, context].

Next we calculate our gradients gV and gW. We derive these formulas in the theory lecture. Next we update the relevant parts of V and the relevant parts of W using gradient descent. And finally we calculate the cost and return it. Note that sometimes the weight probabilities end up being too close to 0 or 1 which ends up breaking the cost function. So we need to add a small number inside the log function to mitigate this this actually doesn't affect any of our calculations because we're not using automatic differentiation.

Next we have a function called load_model which loads in the items that were saved by train_model. So if you called train_model earlier now you can load the same model just by calling load_model. Next we have our analogy function which is pretty much the same thing you saw before. Finally we have our test model function which just calls analogy on a bunch of different words. So that's it for this file. Let's run this and see what we get.

11. Word2Vec Tensorflow Implementation Details
==============================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to outline the main changes we'll need to make in our existing word2vec script in order to implement word2vec in TensorFlow. The important thing to remember is that the main structure of the file is not going to change. Really the only thing that has to change is that we will no longer be using our sgd function and instead we'll be using TensorFlow's automatic differentiation capabilities.

Of course this also means we need to build the model and the cost using TensorFlow, which looks a little bit different from NumPy. First. Let's talk about how to make predictions. We know that in NumPy and Theano we can just index an array like normal using square bracket notation. But in TensorFlow this is not how you do this. Instead we use a function appropriately called embedding_lookup. It takes in two arguments the first argument is a tensor that you want to look things up in and the second argument is a row or a list of rows that you want to look up.

So here you can see how we can do the equivalent thing in both NumPy and TensorFlow. Now let's talk about how to get the actual predictions as discussed things are going to look a little different in TensorFlow. Because we're using an embedding lookup, it's useful to think about what the shapes are here when we index the input embedding with the input word we get back a 1 by D matrix when we index the output embedding with the context words we get back an N by D matrix, assuming the number of context words is N. There are multiple ways you can dot these but one way that might not seem obvious is that you can multiply the first embedding by the second embedding directly.

What this does is it multiplies the 1 by D matrix by every row of the N by D matrix element wise then since we want the dot product we can sum over axis equals 1 to get the final result, which is a 1D tensor of length N, and we encapsulate this operation in a function called Dot. Note that this function works whether the first matrix is one by D or if the first matrix is N by D the same size as the second matrix in which case it just does

element-wise multiplication you'll see why this is useful later in the code. So now that we have a function we can use this to get the positive output predictions and then use the binary cross entropy function to get the loss. Then we can do the same thing but with the negative input word and we can use the binary cross entropy for the negative samples as well. Finally we can add these together to get the total loss. After this it's just business as usual with the TensorFlow.

You create an optimizer and run it in a loop. One thing you may have noticed is that TensorFlow comes with a built in softmax approximations, such as the sampled softmax loss and NCE loss. While we won't study these it's good for you to know about them since they essentially fill the same role as the hierarchical softmax and negative sampling too. What's nice is that since they're built in functions they work a little faster. But what I found experimentally was that they didn't work as well as negative sampling at least for the small amount of data we're using.

Of course you are welcome and encouraged to try these out at your leisure.

12. Word2Vec Tensorflow in Code
===============================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture we are going to walk through the TensorFlow implementation of word2vec as you'll see it's almost exactly the same as the NumPy version except that we have some code in the middle to set up the TensorFlow model. And of course there's also automatic differentiation. The relevant file in the repo is word2vec_tf.py. So if we just scroll down to the relevant part it starts with where we define the variables and placeholders.

So we have our positive input word our negative input word in our context. We'll treat these all like 1D arrays. Next we have the input embedding W and the output embedding we notice how I'm transposing V. This is because TensorFlow's embedding_lookup function looks up by row, not column. Next we have our DOT function which we discussed earlier. Next we do our embedding lookups multiply the results using the dot function and then we combine the positive loss and the negative loss to get the total loss.

Remember we're using negative sampling. But you are encouraged to try out sampled softmax and NCE loss if you're interested. So you can see that I've commented out that code here. Note that when you use these you don't need to draw your own negative samples. So things are a little bit faster. Next we initialize our momentum optimizer and then we do our usual boiler plate stuff with sessions. So let's scroll all the way down to the training loop noticed how in the training loop when I loop through a sentence all I do is collect the samples without calling train_op.

And so when we do things this way we would like our input words to be repeated N times so that they match up with the length of the context. The reason we wanted to do this is because it can be quite slow to call train_op in every iteration of the loop since you need to run everything inside a session. So all we do is collect a bunch of samples. Now once we get outside the loop then we call train_op only if we've reached 128 or more samples. This gives us a big enough batch so that we don't waste time with the overhead of Session.

Also at this point we need to reset our collection of samples. So we set inputs targets and neg_words to empty lists. After this everything else in the script is the same. So let's run this and see what we get.

13. How to update only part of a Theano shared variable
=======================================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. One of the major obstacles we have in our Theano implementation is that we can't take advantage of negative sampling knowing only what we know so far. What would happen if you attempted to use the existing update functionality we would have two weights, the input-to-hidden and the hidden-to-output, let's call them W1 and W2. They would both be updated in the usual way in Theano get the gradient with respect to the cost and put the expression for gradient descent into the Theano train function.

Well this presents us with a problem because if we look at W1 we know that only the word vector for the input word should be updated. But if we use Theano's gradient descent it's going to update all the other word vectors too because they are part of W 1. This is despite the fact that the cost function doesn't depend on them so their gradient will be zero . Similarly for W2 we have only two words for the context and negative samples to update. So suppose our context is of size 10 and we have 10 negative samples.

And our vocabulary size is 2000. Then we'll be updating two thousand vectors when we should only be updating 20. Fortunately theano gives us a way to update only part of a shared variable. You're going to see it in the code but it would be useful to go through some simple examples first. The way to do this is with the functions inc_subtensor and set_subtensor as you can tell by their names, inc_subtensor is for incrementing a subtensor, so that's similar to plus equals, and set_subtensor is for setting the sub tensor so that similar to equals assignment.

In particular I suppose I want to represent the expression W[i] equals W[i] minus learning rate times the gradient of J with respect to W[i]. We could use inc_subtensor for this because it's in the form of plus equals. The interesting thing about this is that the full shared variable goes on the left just a nuance you have to remember. So now it's to an example with set_subtensor. Suppose for whatever reason you want to do the long form version of the cost function where you multiply log of P of y given X by the target indicator matrix.

Let's pretend we only have one sample so that it's a 1D vector. In this case we would first create a vector of size k with all zeros. Then we would set the element that corresponds to the label to 1. using set_subtensor. Finally we would multiply the targets by the log of P of y given x to get the cost. Now let's do a slightly more complex example. We still don't have all the information we need to update the weights of our word2vec model, in particular the hidden-to-output weights.

Why is this. Well what I showed you previously was how to index a shared variable only once to do an update. Now if Theano can accept scalars or vectors as indexes to tensors. But we still have a problem. The problem is we don't just have one index for the output weights. We have two. We have one vector of indexes that represents the context and we have one vector of indexes that represents the negative samples. There are two initial but incorrect solutions to this problem that you might think of.

First you might think just concatenate the context and negative samples and use that to index the output weights. But that won't work because Theano will complain that your inputs are not part of the Theano graph. Personally I think that's a bug. Since technically they are part of the final graph. They've just been combined to create a new variable. Suffice it to say it does not work. The second solution you might think of is to represent them as two separate updates one for the context and one for the negative samples.

So for example you might want to do something such as get the gradient for the context, get the gradient for the negative samples and then set your updates to include two tuples with the same W2. But of course this is also incorrect because the list of tuples in the updates acts more like a dictionary . So all the elements in position zero of the tuple acts as a key and they must be unique since these two obvious solutions won't actually work. The question is, what will? The actual solution is pretty ugly.

But it works. The idea is you have to represent the two updates in a nested manner. Why is this possible? Remember that inc_subtensor returns a full size tensor. So after calling it once where we index context we get back a full size tensor from which we can then index the negative samples without any problem. Now you have all the tools you need to update both W1 and W2 using Theano instead of NumPy. Give it a try and if you can't figure it out cross-reference your code with mine and see where you went wrong.

14. Word2Vec in Code with Theano
================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to continue to add to our word2vec script in particular.
