We're going to implement the fit function using Theano instead of NumPy. So let's call it fit_t, which stands for fit Theano. It takes in X, number of negative samples, learning rate, momentum, regularization and the number of epochs. Now a lot of this is going to be very similar to the other fit.

So we get N which is the length of X vocabulary size embedding dimension we call get_pnw so that it finds the negative sample distribution . Now this will be a bit different. So we init the weights but then we also make Theano shared variables. The next step is to define our inputs and outputs. So th_input is a scalar and that's the input word. The context is a vector. Negative samples is also a vector . Now we define subsets of W1 and W2. So these are the subsets that we need to update and we're going to use to determine the cost.

So this is just W1 indexed by the input W2 indexed by the context and the negative samples the positive activation portion is W1_subset dot W2_psubset. pos_pY, which is the same as what it was in the NumPy version, is just the sigmoid of pos_activation. Now we define neg_activation similarly . Except it's the negative dot product and the cost is the same. We take the log of pos_pY and sum it, take the log of neg_pY and sum it. Next step is to define the gradients.

So we only need to take the gradient with respect to the subset of variables for W2 it's going to be a little complicated because there are two different subsets indexing the same shared variable. So W1 is easy we define the update as an inc_subtensor: W1_subset incremented by minus learning rate times the gradient W2 update will be a little more complicated. So the first argument here will be another T.inc_subtensor, and then inside this nested T.inc_subtensor we increment W2_psubset.

And then outside that we increment W2_nsubset. The next step is to define the updates. So that's pretty easy it's just tuples (W1, W1_update) and (W2, W2_update). Now we define the trainer. So this is theano.function. The inputs will be the Theano variables that we defined earlier: th_input, th_context and th_negative_samples we're going to just output the cost and pass in the updates . Now we're going to keep track of the cost the same way we did in the NumPy version.

So the actual costs are going to jump around a lot. So we want the per-epoch costs; those will jump around less and you'll see them decrease when to keep track of time. And we want to shuffle the sentence indexes on each round so we get the sentence indexes. Now if the sentence is too short we will just continue to the next one and cj; we'll keep an array and take the mean at the end . So we get the start and end indexes for the context. Just in case we go out of bounds since some of the context words can be duplicated we're going to concatenate them and then put them through a set so that they're all unique.

By the end next step is to get the negative samples. Once we have the context the negative samples we can call the trainer. So this portion of the code is much shorter using Theano since we don't have to explicitly write down the gradients. We take a mean of cj, append that to costs per epoch and append that to the full costs and every 100 steps we're going to do our printout. So we're going to use stdout so that it prints on the same line overwriting the last print. And when that's all done we can take the mean of costs for each epoch to get the per-epoch cost and then append that to costs_per_epoch. Once the epoch is done.

We want to print how long it took . And so we have to make this work in the framework of the NumPy versions, so we set self.W1 and self.W2 to actual values, so use W1.get_value(). Once we done that we can plot all the cost. So the first one should be very noisy. The second one since we take the mean over Epoch it should decrease each round. And then we make sure we're calling this new fit_t in the main function

15. Alternative to Wikipedia Data Brown Corpus
==============================================

Hey everyone welcome back to this class recurrent neural networks deep learning in Python Part 5 in this lecture. You're going to look at an alternative data set to the Wikipedia data which is used by default in this course. So why would you want to do this with a Wikipedia dataset was 12 gigabytes the last time I checked. You can download it in parts but each part is still hundreds of megabytes. There are also problems with the format. Wikipedia. Data dumps are in XML format and that means we need to parse the files to extract the important parts.

There are some tools out there that will turn them into plain text but like most free tools the onus is on you to set it up properly on your machine. Since the students of this course are in many different systems it's near impossible for everyone to be able to set these up correctly. So in this lecture I offer you an alternative This alternative is the Brown corpus The Brown corpus is a famous dataset itself and has been around for many decades. It's so famous that NLTK the natural language toolkit provides utilities for importing the data in code with just one function call.

So this is the easiest way to try out the techniques you'll learn in this course without having to download and convert the Wikipedia data. If you don't have Intel T.K. How can you get it. I assume this is trivial for most of you since you already know how to install Python libraries. Simply do sudo pip install nltk. Now just because you have NLTK doesn't mean you have the brown corpus. There are lots of pieces of NLTK that are not included so that you don't have to store things you don't need.

So if you get an error retrieving the brown corpus later on then you might need to run nltk.download() and select the brown corpus from the list that pops up once you have NLTK and the brown corpus. You may want to see the data set yourself just run the commands from nltk.corpus import brown and then call the function brown.sents(). You'll notice that the result is a list of lists and each sentence is a list of individual words already tokenized.

If you're interested you can have a look at the code that loads the Brown corpus as well. This is in the file brown.py in the folder rnn_class at the top of this file. We import brown from NLTK and we set some key words. These are words we absolutely want to keep so we can do comparisons later on. This will be useful when we want to limit the vocabulary so we have to get rid of some words. We just want to make sure we don't get rid of these specific words the get_sentences function isn't really that useful.

You could just call brown.sents() in its place. Note that the brown Corpus has approximately fifty seven thousand sentences. This is a much smaller data set than Wikipedia but good enough for our experiments. The next couple of functions are designed to mirror those in the file for Wikipedia. Specifically they need to return the sentences as lists of word indexes and the words index mapping. So the first function get_sentences_with_word2idx does this.

But remember that the vocabulary can get very large and we may actually want to limit the size. So after this we have the function get_sentences_with_word2idx_limit_vocab, which takes in the desired vocabulary size and the keep words collection. The code here exactly mirrors the util.py code for the Wikipedia dataset so first we just loop through all the sentences convert them into index form and create a word to index mapping. We also keep track of how many times each word appears.

Next we restrict the vocabulary size. We do this by keeping the most common words which is why we need it to count the number of times each word appeared. We set all the other low count or infrequent words to unknown remember. Since we want the word indexes to start from zero we need to re-index all the words with a new mapping. But since we re-index the words we also need to recreate the sentences since the indexes it used previously are no longer valid.

We also do a sanity check with some asserts to make sure the words we want are in fact in the vocabulary. Once we're done this we return our new sentences with the new word indexes and our new word2idx mapping. So now that you know we can use the brown corpus in place of the Wikipedia dataset. How do you actually make sure this is being done in the code. This should be pretty trivial if you know how commenting works and how if statements work which I'm sure you do by now.

For example in the file wiki.py in the folder rnn_class you can see that there are two lines where the data is loaded and one is commented out. So if you want to use the Wikipedia data just uncomment the line that says get Wikipedia data and then comment out the other line. Vice versa if you want to use the brown corpus. Another thing you might see for example in the file word2vec.py in the folder nlp_class2 is that there is an argument for the function called use brown.

Then there is an if statement that checks this variable. If use brown is true then we use the brown corpus. If used Brown is false then we use the Wikipedia data so you can change that to be whatever you want.

### 5. Word Embeddings using GloVe

1. GloVe Section Introduction
=============================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. I'm going to outline this section which is going to look at a different algorithm for finding word vectors called GloVe. GloVe stands for Global vectors for word representation. It was invented by pretty famous NLP researchers at Stanford and you can read more about it in this paper which I've attached in extra_reading.txt.

Personally I prefer GloVe over word2vec as I find it to be much simpler overall especially when it comes to training. There are less hyper parameters to tune and less tricks you have to implement. And so it generally just works now to really get into the details of how GloVe works we have to take a detour into recommender systems particularly an algorithm known as a matrix factorization. So the outline of this section is as follows. First we're going to look at Matrix factorization in the context of recommender systems.

What this does is it's going to learn from rating's data. We have users and we have movies and we know that those users rated those movies. We can then predict what those users might rate movies they haven't seen yet. So the idea is pretty simple. If I think you're going to rate a movie very highly that I should probably recommend that movie to you using this as a starting point. We're going to derive the solution for Matrix factorization. Then we're going to see how Matrix factorization applies to the natural language processing scenario.

That's what gives us GloVe. Once you know how Matrix factorization works for recommender systems it doesn't take any more work to apply it to GloVe which is nice for us. I think recommender systems is a good starting point because for one that's where matrix factorization techniques were first invented and applied. And secondly everybody understands movie ratings.

2. Matrix Factorization for Recommender Systems - Basic Concepts
================================================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. The first thing we wanted to do in our discussion of Matrix factorization is to first just describe all the basic concepts we're going to introduce a bunch of new variables. So this lecture is all about just trying to understand what these variables mean in terms of real world measurements. Well also look at some basic intuition behind why Matrix factorization works.

Let's start with some basic intuition. We have users and we have movies or if you want you can think of movies as just some generic items or products listed on a Web site like Amazon. Now I'm going to assume you've used the site like Amazon or Netflix before. So you have some concept of ratings after you purchase a product or watch a movie. You have an opportunity to rate it and tell the system whether you liked it or not. So on some sites this might be a thumbs up or thumbs down.

Well you could be rating the product out of five stars. Now what I'm going to do is store all of these ratings and a big matrix called R. The users go along the rows and the movies go along the columns. So the entry r_ij tells me what user i rated item j. By the way a small detail that means users will be numbered from zero to N minus one. And movies will be numbered from zero to M minus one. That makes this matrix R have the size N by M. What's important to remember is that not all the values in this matrix are filled in.

In fact most of the values in the matrix R are missing or empty. Why is that. Well it's because most users have not rated most movies. Think about how many movies you watched in your lifetime and of those movies. How many have you bothered to rate on any platform. Probably a very very small amount. Now compare that to the number of movies in existence. About five hundred thousand. For this reason we say that R is a sparse matrix. Now it's important to distinguish between what we mean by sparse and what mathematicians sometimes think of as sparse.

When mathematicians say a matrix is sparse they're usually talking about a matrix that has lots of zeros. But in our ratings matrix It's not that these missing values are zero. A value of zero would just imply a very bad rating in our ratings matrix, sparse means the value is not there at all. It's not zero. It's just not anything you can think of it as undefined. In reality having missing values is a good thing. Let's think about why that might be. It helps to remember what the application is recommender systems.

The job of a recommender system is to tell you about a movie you might like that you haven't seen yet. What would be the problem with a non sparse matrix. Or in other words a matrix with all values filled in with no missing values. Well a ratings matrix with no missing values means that every user has seen and rated every single movie. In other words we have nothing to recommend to our users because there is nothing they haven't seen yet. To phrase it another way.

Having missing values in our ratings Matrix is a little bit of an inconvenience but at the same time it's a necessary condition for us to be able to build a recommender system in the first place. So now we have a rating's matrix called R and in general we understand its structure matrix factorization as an example of a class of algorithms known as collaborative filtering. The idea behind collaborative filtering is that out of all the users in our database there are going to be other users that are similar to you similar to you but not exactly the same as you.

What that means is you have some movies in common that you both liked. Of course there are still movies that you've seen that other users haven't seen. And there are movies that other people have seen that you haven't seen yet so that forms the basis of recommendations users that are similar to you that have liked a movie that you haven't seen it would be a good recommendation for you. So as an example let's look at the small table of users and movie ratings.

We can see that both Alice and Bob seem to really like action movies but they dislike romance. Carol on the other hand seems to have the opposite opinion. Carol likes romance movies but dislikes action movies. In other words we can say that Alice is similar to Bob but they are both dissimilar to Carol we can see that there is one rating missing here that Bob has not yet seen Star Wars but I think it's pretty intuitive to try and guess whether or not Bob will like Star Wars.

Keep in mind that Alice and Bob have very similar ratings what Alice likes Bob also likes and since Alice like Star Wars we can use the fact that they are similar to estimate that Bob might also like Star Wars. In turn we can recommend a star wars to Bob. Now this is not the first time we've seen a pattern like this. And in fact we don't have to look further than NLP itself. Think of a term document matrix. We have words going along the rows in documents going along the columns.

If we consider each row to be a feature vector how do we know that two words are similar. Well it's simply that there are feature vectors are close together in simpler terms that means the words quantum and gravity or similar because when the count of quantum is high the count of gravity is also high. Quantum and mitochondria are dissimilar because when the count of quantum is high the count of mitochondria is low and vice versa. On the other hand the words mitochondria and cell membrane are similar to each other.

The key property in both a ratings matrix and a term document matrix is that of redundancy in machine learning terms you can think of redundancy as two features being very correlated with one another. So that knowing both doesn't really give you any more information than just knowing one by itself. One example of that is measuring someone's height if I know your height in inches I don't need to know your height in centimeters. They both give me the exact same information.

Your height in inches is actually perfectly correlated to your height in centimeters one is just the other multiplied by a constant. Similarly documents might be redundant. I might have one million documents about physics but do I really need 1 million documents to tell me that the words quantum and gravity are related. Probably not in the ratings case. Movies can be redundant as well. Suppose that everyone who likes Star Wars Episode 1 also like Star Wars Episode 2 then it would be easy to predict your rating for one movie given your rating for the other.

In all these cases we can exploit the property of redundancy to build a model that reduces the dimensionality of the data. That sounds complicated but the key term here is dimensionality reduction. One method of dimensionality reduction that you may have heard of in the past is called SVD or singular value decomposition. Basically it means that we're going to decompose some matrix X into three separate matrices. U, S, V. X is just these three matrices multiplied together.

So what exactly makes this a dimensionality reduction. Well if X is of shape N by D then U is of shape N by D, S is of shape D by D and V is of shape D by D. In this scenario, U, S and V are able to exactly reconstruct the original Matrix X. Now one thing you might notice right away is that this isn't really a reduction. U is already the same size as X and so U, S and V all altogether take up more space than the original x. But what if we reduce the size of U, S and V.

Suppose I cut off the right side of U, the bottom right corner of S and the bottom of V transpose. Then that means U has size N by K, S is of size K by K and V is of size D by K if I choose K to be much smaller than D then I will reduce the amount of space needed to reconstruct x. Now as for why we're allowed to do this I don't want to cover SVD in this course because it's not really relevant but you can feel free to ask me about it on the Q&A.

Let's put some real numbers on these dimensions so you get some sense of the savings we get let's say N equals 1 million and D equals 500000. Let's say K equals 10. Then our original matrix X is of size N by D, which is 1 times 10 to the 6 times 5 times 10 to the 5, which is 5 times 10 to the power 11. So that's 500 billion very big number. Now how much space do U, S and V take up altogether? U is of size N by K, which is 10 million. S is of size

K by K, which is 100. V is of size D by K which is five million. So altogether we take up about 15 million divided by 500 billion which is about three times 10 to the minus 5. In other words we would save a significant amount of space by doing SVD. Now in this course. Luckily you don't have to know anything about SVD and we're not going to be using SVD unless it's just to make a comparison. The reason is SVD doesn't actually work for a recommender system.

There are several reasons for this. First is that if you recall our ratings Matrix has missing values. So even if we wanted to use SVD it really wouldn't work because SVD requires every value to be filled in. The second reason is that in its usual implementation SVD might not scale if we have a large number of users and a large number of items. Luckily this is where matrix factorization comes into play. So what is Matrix factorization. Think about factors in general that mean something can be expressed as multiple things usually smaller than the original multiplied together.

For example 15 equals three times five ten equals five times two. So numbers can be factored into a product of smaller numbers as long as they are not prime numbers. Similarly matrices can also be factored. In fact SVD is also a factorization just not the factorization we want what we want is to factor our ratings matrix R into two separate smaller matrices W and U. Let's do a short analysis again. So we get a sense of what we save by doing matrix factorization.

Let's suppose N equals one million. So we have 1 million users, and M equals 20000. So we have 20000 items then using a full dense matrix, this would take up 1 times 10 to the 6 times 2 times 10 to the 4, which is 20 billion. But suppose we only have 20 million ratings as we do in the MovieLens dataset. This is a popular recommender systems data set that we won't be using in this course but I'm including it here just for reference. So 20 million out of 20 billion is just 0.1 percent.

In other words only 0.1 percent of the values in the N by M matrix are filled in; 99.9 percent of those cells are empty. In reality the number of users and movies is actually larger. So this matrix is even more sparse. Now let's say we choose a latent dimensionality of K equals 10. Then W is of size N by K which is 10 million, U is of size M by K which is 200000. So in total we have 10.2 million parameters and 20 million ratings, so that's about 50 percent of the size of our dataset.

And that's a good thing since we never want to over-parameterize our model which could lead to overfitting now that we have a model the next thing we have to consider is how we are going to use this model. If you notice what happens when we multiply W by U transpose? Well we get a full N by M matrix without any missing values in particular all of these values our rating predictions any entry r hat ij is a prediction for what user i would rate movie j.

By the way if we wanted to get a single prediction we wouldn't have to multiply all of W by all of U but rather we would just take the dot product of w_i and u_j which is much faster. In fact this is exactly how we can make recommendations basically for any entry in R that's not yet filled in. That means it's a movie you haven't rated yet which means it's a movie we can possibly recommend to you then what we do is find r hats for all the movies you haven't seen and then sort them by the predicted rating the highest predicted rating is your top recommendation.

One question you might have at this point is why does this model make sense to begin with. Well suppose we have some vector w_i of size K and some vector u_j of size K and we take the dot product. What does the dot product tell us. Well as you know from earlier in this course the dot product tells us how similar or different two vectors are. We call it the cosine similarity since the dot product is proportional to the cosine of the angle between the two vectors.

So what does it mean to say a user vector and a movie vector are similar each of the K dimensions represents a latent feature. But we can pretend that they are real meaningful features. So for example one feature might represent the action adventure genre. Another feature might represent comedy and other feature might represent romance and so on. So let's say we have a latent dimensionality of three and the features represent action adventure comedy and romance.

If our user likes action adventure doesn't care about comedy and dislikes romance. The user vector might be one zero minus one. Now let's say we have a movie in the action adventure genre. It's pretty funny too. So it might also fall under comedy but there's no romance in it. So an appropriate movie vector might be 1 1 0. And if we dot these two vectors together we get plus one. Now let's say we have another movie which is a romance movie. It definitely doesn't contain any action adventure but it contains some comedy.

So this movie vector is minus 1 1 1. If we dot this movie vector with our user vector we get minus two with this scheme. You can see that positive correlations yield positive numbers whereas negative correlations yield negative numbers. Zero seems to be neutral which suggests that we should probably center our ratings in the implementation. But let's get back to how we evaluate our model which is very important. Essentially we can only evaluate the accuracy of our model on ratings that are known.

In particular we can call Omega the set of all existing ratings. It consists of i, j tuples where as you know i represents the user and J represents the movie. And so our cost function since this is a regression is going to be the squared error over all known ratings. In other words we sum over all the pairs i, j which are in omega and take the squared error between the actual rating and our models predicted rating r hat ij. By the way, this is analogous to what we do in SVD, which tries to optimize the squared error between the original X and the reconstructed x.

At this point I have to introduce a few new symbols but we won't know why they are useful until we get to the next few lectures. So first we have Omega_j. Remember j represents a movie. So this is the set of all users i that have rated movie J. Next we have Psi_i. i represents a user. So this is the set of all movies J that have been rated by user i. Now that you know all the symbols will need for the upcoming lectures we can summarize what we've done so far.

First we have a big ratings matrix called R where the entry r_ij tells us what user i rated movie j. Next we have the model R hat, which is equal to W times U transpose. r hat ij is the prediction for what user i would rate movie j; it's just the dot product between the i-th row of W and the j-th row of U. Next we have our cost function Big J which is the sum of squared errors over all existing rating. And next we define a few useful sets Omega, which consists of i, j pairs, tells us which users have rated which movies. Omega_j tells us,

for movie j, which users i rated that movie. Psi_i tells us, for user i, which movies j they rated. As you know, once we have our cost function our job is to minimize it with respect to the parameters of our model, W and U. The next few lectures will be devoted to showing you how to do that. In addition were going to add a few more typical machine learning features like biased Herm's and regularization. Finally we'll jump back to a GloVe and you'll see how Matrix factorization can be used in NLP to find word vectors.

I want to make one remark that you don't have to understand the upcoming math in order to understand GloVe or implement GloVe. A lot of beginners tend to lose sight of what's important to focus on. Remember we're not doing anything different from most other machine learning problems. We have some cost and we're going to minimize it with respect to W and U. The details of how that's done. Obviously uses some heavy mathematical machinery which will be extremely difficult to understand.

If you don't have the right mathematical background so it's not so important to know how we're doing what we're doing compared to why we're doing what we're doing which presumably you already understand after this lecture. Of course understanding the mechanics behind the math is very important and will make your understanding much stronger. But even if you don't understand the derivation that does not mean you can't go to the next step and implement the equations in code.

Remember that's always the exercise we're going to do. First we look at the theory and then we implement that theory in code. So just because you don't understand the equations that we ended up with in the theory lectures does not mean you can't still copy them in code which is the step that comes after.

3. Matrix Factorization Training
================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to talk about how to train the matrix factorization model. Previously we discussed that what we wanted to do is create two matrices that when multiplied together approximate our ratings matrix R. We call this R hat. Of course since R hat is our approximation of R we would like them to be close together. So how can we ensure this? Well since this is regression.

You know that the way to do this is the sum of squares. Technically we could also look at the mean squared error as we have been. But that just divides the data set by the size which is a constant and has no effect on the answer. So for the purpose of this derivation we're going to ignore it as usual. Now that we have a loss function what we would like to do is set the gradient to zero and solve for W and U. So the process is the same as what we always do.

And this is going to be our goal of this lecture so let's derive the expression for W first. What's challenging about this is you have to be very careful about which sets of data you're currently looking at. Recall that Omega with no subscripts represents the set of pairs i, j where user i has rated movie j, and Psi_i is the set of all movies that user i has rated since we are taking the derivative with respect to w_i we only care about the subset of ratings that actually depend on w_i and hence the summation covers only the movies that user i has rated. From here.

The derivative is pretty straightforward. Bring down the two and use the chain rule to take the derivative of the dot product. That's just a linear term so we get minus u_j the next step is to set this to zero and try to isolate w_i. At this point. Things might seem a little messy because you have w_i transpose and it's not immediately clear how to isolate w by itself without the transpose. As you can see it's stuck inside a dot product. The trick here is first to remember that a dot product is commutative so it doesn't matter what order you multiply the two operands in. w_i

dot u_j is the same as u_j dot w_i. The next thing to remember is that the result of a dot product is a scalar is just a number and we know that a scalar multiplied by a vector u_j is the same as a vector multiplied by scalar. So we can just move u_j to the other side. The next thing we can do is just drop the brackets. We know that everything here is multiplication so that's OK. The next step is to remember that this is a summation over j; w_i doesn't depend on j.

Therefore it doesn't actually need to be inside the summation. So once we bring it outside the summation the solution is clear. We actually want to do the outer product between u_j and itself summed over all values of J. And now we have the familiar form Ax equals b, which is a matrix multiplied by a vector equals another vector we solve for x by inverting a and multiplying that by the vector on the right. But remember that we never actually want to do this calculation in code.

Instead NumPy has a special solve function where you pass in a and b directly and it uses a special algorithm to solve for x. It's both faster and more accurate. But I'm sure if you've been taking my courses you already know this now that we've solved for w_i the next step is of course to solve for u_j. So let's do that. And actually the loss function is symmetric in both w_i and u_j. So the steps here are no different just the roles of w_i and u_j are reversed.

Let's begin as before. The next step we have to be careful with is determining what we need to sum over. Since we only want terms involving u_j that means we only want the users i who rated item j. So that's Omega_j. As before the derivative is trivial. We bring the two down and the derivative of the dot product is just w_i. We set the derivative to zero and isolate each term. Next we change the order of the dot product so that we can bring u_j outside the summation.

And now we can add new brackets to make it clear that u_j is not part of the summation and that inside the summation we have an outer products. And finally we can bring the matrix on the left over to the right side by inverting it. And of course we won't actually invert it in code. So now we're at a place we haven't encountered before. Well actually you have. If you've taken deep NLP where we covered this topic briefly but I'm going to assume that you're starting from scratch.

So the thing with this is we have sort of this two way dependency. The solution to W depends on U but the solution to U depends on W. So you might ask is it possible to plug in one equation for the other to isolate the two variables. In a sense yes that's possible but that's not actually what we want to do. The issue is we're still going to have two separate steps. There isn't just one global equation and this is the nature of our model. We have two parameters W and U and therefore they both need to be updated.

So instead the algorithm is to just apply the equations as is. In other words we start with a random initialization for W and U. Then in a loop we just apply these two equations. First we update all the W's. Then we update all the U's. Then we loop back around and update all the W's again in machine learning. This algorithm is called alternating least squares. It's been proven that on each step we always get closer to a local minimum. And one question you might ask is does it matter what order you update the parameters in.

Should W go first or should U go first. The answer is It doesn't matter. Remember that the loss is actually symmetric in W and U. They both play the same mathematical role in the equation. If you're in doubt try both. Another common question I get is since W depends on U and U depends on W, should I use the old values of W when I update U if the update for U comes second? and again the answer to this is that it doesn't matter. Well actually in this case as it is with neural networks things tend to go faster if you simply use whatever value exists in W at the time when you are updating U. Computationally.

This is better also. If you want to use the old value of W you'd have to make a copy of it on each round which is expensive and keep in mind that you're alternating anyway. So W is still going to use the latest version of U.

4. Expanding the Matrix Factorization Model
===========================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to look at our first expansion of the basic matrix factorization algorithm that we just learned. Think about the linear regression model for a moment y equals mx plus b. What you can see with this is that there are two parameters of interest. The first is the slope and the second is the y intercept the y intercept controls how much the line is pushed upwards on the x y plane.

So if you didn't have this then the line would always be forced to pass through the origin which may not actually fit your data set. Now if you think back to the previous lectures where we did an example of cosine similarity what did we see. Well if you take two vectors that are negatively correlated the result is going to be a negative number. But intuitively we know that this isn't good for five star ratings because five star ratings are generally positive not centered around zero.

First let's consider why we would need something like user bias? Well it has to do with the fact that different people rate things differently. Different numbers mean different things to different people. As an extreme example of this consider uber ratings it's well known that even though uber ratings are on a five star scale people consider an average rating of four point five to be a poor rating. So if you see an Uber driver with a rating of 4.5 out of 5 you might be very suspicious of what could be wrong with that driver.

Even on Web sites for online courses such as this one there is a large disparity between how different users think of ratings. For some users I ask they think of a rating of 2.5 out of 5 as simply average. That means the course got the job done. It wasn't a bad course but at the same time the course didn't make them feel enlightened. But for other people 2.5 means something very very bad. I think anyone who has gone to school could relate to this. Imagine getting 50 out of 100 as your final grade and of course well that's enough.

That means you failed the course. That means you did so bad that you have to repeat the course next year. But even these people have a disparity between them. Some people think of a grade of 75 percent as average. That translates to a B. Others especially if you go to a school with high performing students might think of an A minus as average. Others might think of a grade of C as average. The point of this is users have biases. Some users are very optimistic meaning they rate everything highly.

Other users are very pessimistic meaning they rate everything low. Well if on average you rate everything a 2.5. Then if you rate something a 3.5. That must mean you think it's really great. On the other hand if you rate everything a 4.5 on average than a rating of 3.5. Coming from you probably means it's pretty bad. Similarly we'd like to introduce bias terms into the matrix factorization model. Let me start with the equation itself and then I'll explain what it means.

So in this revised version of Matrix factorization with bias terms we see that the predicted rating r hat is the sum of the dot product of the use of vector multiplied by the movie vector, the user bias b, the movie bias c and mu, the global average. Now normally with a simple machine learning models like linear regression you only need one bias term. But for Matrix factorization we would like to have three. Let's start with mu, The global average you can think of this as centering the dataset.

If we plotted a histogram of all the ratings this would be like shifting it so that it has mean zero. The reason we like both a user bias and a movie bias is because both these dimensions can be biased. We discussed user bias already you might be very optimistic or very pessimistic but there's a movie bias as well. Let's see how that might be the case. Perhaps there's such a great movie that everyone loves. Think of a movie like James Cameron's Avatar your unbiased matrix factorization model might have latent features that capture the fact that it's a sci fi movie, it has aliens and it contains action with humans fighting aliens.

But are these attributes enough to predict how much you'll like the movie. Surely there are other movies that exist that have all these attributes yet perform very poorly. One that comes to mind is Battlefield Earth largely regarded as the worst sci fi movie of all time. So to conclude having a movie bias is probably a good idea. Now that we have a new model we have to reconsider how to train it. Luckily since we already derive the update equations it won't be that hard to add a few more terms.

Let's start again the loss from this we can see that we'll want to solve for w_i, u_j, b and c. Let's start with w_i since it appears first. as usual to take the derivative we just need to bring the two down and use the chain rule to find the derivative of the dot product. Note that none of the other terms depend on w_i so they all go away and the derivative you're left with is u_j. The next step is to realize that all the terms except for the one involving w_i

can be moved to the right side. From here it's simple since all we have to do is isolate w_i like we did before, and of course since the equations for w_i and u_j are symmetric. We can apply the same logic to determine that the update for u_j should be as follows. Of course you should try deriving it by hand. If you're not fully convinced yet. Next we would like to derive the updates for the bias terms. Let's start with b. This one is actually a little easier since it's just scalar.

In fact since b_i appears by itself its derivative is one and all the other terms go to zero. So we have to do to solve this is to move everything except b_i to the other side. One thing that it's important not to forget is that b_i is still inside a summation to bring it outside the summation. It's the same as saying b_i is equal to b_i times 1 and then leaving the one inside the summation and one is summed up at this many times is equal to the size of the set Psi_i.

Of course this is the number of movies user has rated. Now this makes sense because if you're summing up that many items you need to balance it out by dividing by the number of items and in fact you can see that this update is very intuitive. The bias for user i is the average deviation of the actual rating minus every part of the model except b_i itself. That's basically the very definition of b_i. b_i is essentially what would make the model more accurate compared to if the model didn't have b_i at all.

Finally we can similarly derive the update for c_j by taking the exact same steps as you can see the derivative is nearly identical because the derivative of c_j by itself is just one. The only difference here is what we sum over which is now all the users who rated movie j. As before we isolate c_j by moving everything else to the right side. And again we can see that this is just the average deviation of the actual rating from the prediction. If the model did not contain c_j all right so here's one last look at all the update equations we arrived in this lecture as a reminder.

We don't need to update mu because it's just the global mean of the trainset which you can find directly. At this point if you've already started your implementation then that's awesome and you are doing a great job. Luckily it won't be much more work to modify it to include these bias terms as you can see. It's just a small modification on what you've already implemented. So most of the hard work like getting the structure of your code right has already been done.

5. Regularization for Matrix Factorization
==========================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. I'm going to introduce the final modification to our basic matrix factorization model and then we'll be ready for implementation. To recap so far our model is the sum of the user feature vector multiplied by the movie feature vector Plus the user bias plus the movie bias plus the global mean of the ratings in the training set this lecture is all about how to add regularization to the Matrix factorization model.

As you may recall from your previous studies regularization is a technique that helps to prevent overfitting and with such a sparse ratings matrix you can imagine that this might be very useful. Let's start by looking at what regularization looks like. For linear regression. Recall that the model is just a hyperplane. y hat equals w transpose x. The cost function is just the sum of two terms. Number one the squared error which you're already familiar with and number two the squared magnitude of the weights themselves.

The second term is sometimes called the penalty term. The idea is if the weights become too large. That's a sign of overfitting, so large weights are penalized. It's also helpful to look at the solution for W since the solution for linear regression looks very similar to Matrix factorization. All right so basically the approach for Matrix factorization is exactly the same. You take all the parameters W, b, U and c and you add a constant multiplied by their squared magnitude to the existing squared error.

Note that when you do this on a matrix This is usually called the Frobenius norm. Which is why it's subscripted by F. So this is pretty trivial. Not much to see here. The next step of course is to again solve for our new updates. This is going to be slightly more difficult because now each term appears twice instead of just once. Let's start with W again. Luckily the derivatives are additive so we can just copy and paste the part. We already solved and add the new derivative.

If you can't immediately see how we got the derivative for w_i, remember that the Frobenius norm is just the sum of the magnitudes of each individual element and therefore I can also split them up in terms of each row as well. Once I do that we can use the fact that the squared magnitude of a vector is just the dot product of the vector with itself and we already know that the derivative of the dot product is just the vector itself next. All we need to do is group together like terms.

In this case everything that depends on W I and everything that doesn't from here I can see that I can factor out w_i And again we just end up with the usual form Ax equals b and we can invert A and move it to the other side to solve for w_i as usual. We don't need to go through all the same work to solve for u_j because w_i and u_j are symmetric. So just follow the same pattern and write down the solution. Again if this is not intuitive for you already then I'd suggest doing the derivative one step at a time from scratch.

So now that we have w_i and u_j we want to tackle the bias terms so let's start with b_i following the same logic as before the squared magnitude of B is just b dot b. So the derivative with respect to b_i is just b_i itself at this time it might be helpful to explicitly show the b_i terms we can see the b_i shows up twice once on its own and once multiplied by lambda as before b_i doesn't depend on j. So it can be moved outside the summation.

And so the difference here is that instead of dividing by the size of psi we also divide by 1 plus lambda that makes sense because 1 plus lambda is bigger than one which makes b_i smaller. Finally we can use the fact that b_i and c_j are symmetric, so we can just copy the same result for c_j. So to summarize here the final update formulas once again for W, b, U and c. As promised they all follow the same format as the solution for linear regression. At this point we've done all the modifications necessary to create a good matrix factorization model.

In the next few lectures we'll start to look at the implementation by the way. I hope you followed my advice to actually do this derivation on pen and paper. I know it's a pretty normal thing today to try and take notes using your laptop but for things like math this approach really does not work. I can only imagine what kind of torture you're putting yourself through if you're trying to derive this using keys on your laptop or worse yet trying to do it in your head.

You don't necessarily have to understand this derivation in order to implement the code since you can just copy these formulas. But if you want to fully understand the concepts being taught in this course it would be a useful achievement.

6. GloVe - Global Vectors for Word Representation
=================================================

Hey guys, welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to discuss an alternative to word2vec for creating word embeddings called GloVe you'll see with GloVe that basically we're just going to do some work up front and then it just becomes the matrix factorization problem which we already know how to solve. Similar to word2vec what we want to do is incorporate context. Specifically we are not going to build a term-document matrix like we did for easy NLP.

You're going to build a term-term matrix. In our term-term matrix, X_ij should be higher if word I appears in the context of word j often and lower if word i appears in the context of word j less often. With GloVe, it's the details about how we build this matrix X that are important. So let's discuss those. First thing is we want to incorporate context distance. If a word is right beside another word we'll count that as one. If a word is within the context of another word but has one word in between them.

We'll count that as one half if word i and word j have two words between them. We'll count that as one third and so on. Secondly remember that words often have long tailed distribution. That means for most of these entries we're going to have zero values. Lots of them. This is going to give us a very sparse matrix similar to what we had with our recommender system. But whereas with recommender systems we had missing values. These values are not missing.

They are just zero. But both of them can be referred to as sparse matrices. Where we do have nonzero values. Those values are going to be very large in the hundreds of thousands or millions depending on how many documents you have in your corpus. A common technique for scaling things down when they grow exponentially large is taking the log. So that's what we'll do. Now we can't take the log of 0. So we'll add a 1 to every entry and then take the log. This will be our target matrix.

Now we could construct a cost function from just what we have so far but we won't just yet. We are going to add one more thing which researchers have found to work well. What we're going to do is we're going to weight each (i, j)th entry by its X value. If its X value is sufficiently large we'll give it a weight of 1. If its X value is small we'll give it a weight of less than 1 the function used in the original paper was X divided by X max to the power of alpha.

So X max is 100, alpha is 0.75. Now how do we solve this problem in the original paper. They use gradient descent with bias terms but we'll use our knowledge of Matrix factorization and recommender systems and we'll try alternating least squares with three bias terms. These derivatives are actually easier to solve because you don't have to think about a set of values you need to consider under the summation. Since there are no missing values in the target matrix you still want to try to solve this problem on your own as an exercise to make sure you have the skills to do so.

One interesting aspect of GloVe is that the training time you spend on fitting the parameters doesn't depend on the amount of training data you have. Since you're always fitting to a VxV Matrix this is good because if we make a mistake later on in our code we don't have to do all the work to create X again we can just save it and reuse it. So these are the equations for how to update the model parameters so you can check your answers. So this is for W and U and this is for the b and c biases

7. Recap of ways to train GloVe
===============================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. I want to summarize all the different ways we've looked at for how to train GloVe since you're going to see several different implementations very shortly. Of course as always you should try to implement these algorithms yourself before looking at my implementation. So please remember to consider the following code examples as exercises.

These are the answers you should look at only after you've attempted to write the code yourself. The method that we focused on in this course is called alternating least squares. This is different from what they discussed in the paper which just used gradient descent. Luckily deriving alternating least squares requires us to calculate the gradients anyway so implementing the gradient descent method won't take much more work. The gradient descent method will be implemented in several ways.

The first which I think is the most educational is to implement it in pure NumPy. The second is to make use of deep learning library such as Theano and TensorFlow which as you know can automatically take gradients. In addition libraries like TensorFlow also give us adaptive learning algorithms such as Adam with no extra work and to take advantage of the GPU which allows us to speed up the performance in a later lecture. We'll look at yet another method which as far as I know hasn't been done.

Which is just to use SVD. If you recall earlier I said we can't use SVD for recommender systems because we have a matrix with missing values with a GloVe. We don't have missing values since sparsity comes from the fact that the context count between most pairs of words is simply zero. Remember that 0 is different from missing although we refer to both of these scenarios as sparse. In addition since we're going to limit the size of our vocabulary we don't have to worry about scaling.

So while SVD won't work for recommender systems it can possibly work for GloVe. Now does that mean it will work. Well that's another story but at least it's a nice experiment to try since computationally it's feasible.

11. GloVe in Tensorflow with Gradient Descent
=============================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at how to implement GloVe in TensorFlow because TensorFlow has automatic differentiation we'll be using gradient descent rather than alternating least squares. Also note that because TensorFlow has its own built in optimizers we aren't limited to just plain gradient descent. We can try other optimizers quite easily just by switching their names.

The relevant file for this lecture is glove_tf.py. So the first important thing in this file is the GloVe class in the constructor we just set some hyper parameters such as the embedding dimension vocab size and context size and the fit function is where all the interesting stuff is going to happen. So the first thing we need to do in here is check if our co-occurrence matrix already exists. If it does we would like to avoid having to count up all the co-occurrences again because that actually takes quite a while.

So if it doesn't exist then we have to make one. So we start by initializing a matrix of zeros of size V by V. Next we loop through each sentence in our corpus. Next we get little N which is the length of the current sentence. It's easier to loop over the positions rather than the word indexes themselves because we need the position in order to calculate the left and right bounds of the context window. So just remember that I refer to the position and not the actual word.

W is the corresponding word. Next we get start, which is i minus context size. But if this is negative then it should just be zero. Next we get end, which is i plus context size. But if this is greater than the length of the sentence it should just be the length of the sentence. Next since our corpus contains start and end tokens we need to account for those we don't want these counts to be 0. Otherwise later on we're going to end up dividing by zero which is no good.

So if the left side of the context goes out of bounds. Then we add a small number 1 over i plus 1 to this entry of x. If the right side of the context goes out of bounds then we add a small number 1 divided by n minus i to this entry of x. This is sort of a hidden implementation detail but we would like the Count to reflect the distance between the input word and the context word. So we have a one over distance drop off for each word in the context. Next we add the counts for the rest of the words and the contacts.

First we loop through the words on the left side then we loop through the words on the right side. Now it's probably not obvious at first glance how i minus j or j minus i gives you the distance here. But if you write this down on paper where you try to draw a diagram it will probably help also note that X is a symmetric matrix so if word i is in the context of word j, so too is word j in the context of word i. That's why we add the same number of points to both entries of the matrix.

Finally since this is a lot of work and it's very slow we're going to save the results once we're done. This helps with debugging so you can try different learning rates and so forth without having to rebuild the co-occurrence matrix every time. Once we're done building the co-occurrence matrix we are still not done building the matrix. We want to factorize it. So first we have to weight it with our function f. Remember this is just X divided by X Max to the power alpha with a maximum value of 1.

Finally we take the log of X and we add 1 to its argument to avoid taking the log of zero next it's time to build our model. So we start by initializing all the parameters W, b, U and c. We also want mu which is the grand mean of log x. Next we want to set up our TensorFlow placeholders and variables. So we need a variable for all parameters W, b, U and c. Then we need a placeholder for our data log X and f(X). Next we need to build our cost. First we can calculate Delta the difference between our prediction and the actual value log x then we can calculate our cost which is the squared error,

but with every entry weighted by X finally we can add a regularization if you so choose. Next we create an optimizer to minimize the cost. I've chosen the momentum optimizer but you are of course free to try something else. Next is a very tiny block of code which is all just boilerplate stuff. We create a session initialize the variables and then run the train op in a loop. As you can see this loop is much smaller than the NumPy version because all the work is being done with TensorFlow automatic differentiation capabilities.

Finally when we're done we save W and U and plot the cost per iteration. This allows the rest of the script to be no different than the NumPy version. The GloVe class also has a save function which saves the instance variables W and U to a file so you can load it back in later. Next we have the main section. This just loads in the data instantiates the model fits the model and then saves it. Finally we have the main section of the script where we call the main function and then test out some analogies.

So let's run this and see what we get.
