14. Training GloVe with SVD (Singular Value Decomposition)
==========================================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to return to this idea of applying SVD to GloVe. If you recall SVD simply takes a matrix X and computes a low rank approximation of it. By decomposing it into three separate matrices W, S and U. As a side note. Note that I've renamed the variables here to make them closer to what we use for Matrix factorization. Typically for SVD we call the three matrices U, S and V.

But in this lecture we're going to refer to them as W, S and U. Now I won't go into too much detail on how SVD works in this course since I already have a course on that which comes earlier in the deep learning series. I will however go over the points you need to know to understand what we're doing. this lecture. First let's again think about this in the context of recommender systems since I think it's the most intuitive. The idea is we're going to have an N by M matrix called X or if you prefer.

You can keep calling it R, and we're going to decompose it into three separate matrices W, S and U. W is of shape N by K so you can think of it as the user's matrix. Each row corresponds to a different user and each of these rows one for each user, is a K-size latent vector representing that user. S is a diagonal matrix that tells me the variance of each of the K latent dimensions. Again that's outside the scope of this course but you can ask me about if you want using the Q&A so you can sort of think of variance as a proxy for importance if the variance is higher.

That means this latent dimension gives me more information. Finally U is an M by K matrix so you can think of it as the movie's matrix. Each row corresponds to a different movie. And so each of these rows one for each movie is a K-size latent vector representing that movie. All right though structurally there's really only one minor difference between SVD and Matrix factorization. SVD has three matrices whereas matrix factorization has two. Theoretically I could just multiply S by either W or U transpose to get W prime or U prime.

And they would look exactly the same. So this begs the question can I use SVD instead of Matrix factorization. On our current matrix and this lecture that's what we're going to try to do. It's worth remembering why SVD won't work in the general case for recommending systems. Recall that our ratings matrix is sparse in the sense that most of the values are missing. They simply don't exist whereas for our GloVe Matrix it's also sparse but it's sparse in the sense that most values are zero.

They're not missing since 0 is a legitimate value from our dataset meaning that two words never appeared in each other's context. In addition SVD is generally not scalable using default implementations like what's included in scikit-learn so if our vocabulary size was too large this wouldn't work. Luckily we've already limited our vocabulary so we know that this will work. Although as a side note you could represent the co-occurrence matrix as a SciPy sparse matrix which would take up much less space allowing you to use the full vocabulary size and then do SVD on this very large sparse matrix.

One problem with that is that it still might take a long time the next thing I want to mention that makes this different from regular GloVe is that we can't incorporate f(X). As you recall f(X) is a weighting that we apply to the squared error cost function which is a function of the Corcoran's Matrix itself. This is because SVD by default minimizes the normal squared error. As far as I know there's really no way to apply this weighting and scikit-learn.

So I'm just going to ignore it for the purpose of this experiment as you'll see. Our results are still pretty good. Now we can start thinking about how we're going to implement this in code. We'll be using the TruncatedSVD class which is part of scikit-learn as you know in scikit-learn we have the usual standard interface for dimensionality reduction: fit on X and transform on x. So what do these do. The short answer is that fit is going to find W, S and U, the singular value decomposition transform X calculates a linear transformation on X which gives us Z equals X times U. Now again I don't want to get into all the heavy theory behind SVD in this course so I'm not going to explain why this is but if you would like to know more please ask me about it on the Q&A so Z equals X times U.

The problem is we don't care about Z. We care about W. Unfortunately scikit-learn's SVD doesn't save w after it finds it. Luckily it's not too hard to reconstruct w by using z. Let's recap what we've done so far. We have X which is our input data we call Fit transform which finds W, S and U but doesn't save w and instead just gives us back Z so Z equals X times U. Luckily we know the expression for x. This is just W times S times U transpose. So we can plug this in for x.

We know that U transpose times U equals identity so that goes away again. If you don't know why this is. Don't worry about it. But if you are worrying about it please ask me about it. On the Q&A and I can direct you to the appropriate resources. Next we have Z equals W times S. Luckily S is a diagonal matrix so it's very easy to infer. Now we get W equals Z times S inverse. And this is how we are going to calculate w. The next thing we have to do is figure out how S and U are actually stored in the code.

It's important to read the scikit-learn documentation so that you know which math symbols correspond to which variable names in particular. If we say that X equals W times S times U transpose, then U is referred to with the attribute components_ and S is referred to with the attribute explained_variance_, and since S is a diagonal matrix it's just stored as a 1D array. And if you recall you can use NumPy's diag function to turn it back into a diagonal matrix.

So these are the attributes we have to make use of in the code components_ and explained_variance_. Now we are ready to look at the code the relevant file in the course repo is glove_svd.py. Now if you've been following along this code is going to be extremely simple. In fact it's more an exercise in deleting code rather than adding code. So essentially our whole gradient descent and alternating least squares loops go away, our bias terms go away, our regularization goes away.

You don't even have to randomly initialized the variables. All we're left with is the block where we use SVD and find W and U. So here is the relevant block of code in the python file. Everything around this is pretty much the same as before. So we start by creating an object of type TruncatedSVD class with D components. That's how we tell it we want D latent variables. Next we call fit_transform on our co-occurrence matrix. Notice how I'm subtracting mu from it.

This is important since SVD assumes your data is centered before you pass it in. Now this gives us Z. The next step is to compute w. Now as we discussed we want to find the inverse of S, which is just the inverse of the diagonal matrix containing the explained_variance_ array. The next step is to set w equal to Z times s inverse and U is just the transpose of the components attribute. Next we can calculate the cost. Although this is just for completion sake we don't really have anything meaningful to compare it to.

Either way. Note that one alternative here would be to set w equal to Z itself. Currently we're ignoring S, which would just scale each of the dimensions in w and u but we could make this more like Matrix factorization by absorbing S into W, so we can say W prime equals W times S. In this case we get Z equals X times U from the transform function. But now X equals W prime times U transpose. So Z is just equal to W prime, which means we can make our W prime equal to Z.

the output from fit_transform. Now I haven't tried this myself but it would be nice to experiment with. Now we are ready to look at the results. So it seems that the results are on par with if not better than the results we have achieved so far. As an exercise try adding more analogies to test how this algorithm performs.


### 6. Unifying Word2Vec and GloVe

1. Pointwise Mutual Information - Word2Vec as Matrix Factorization
==================================================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. In this lecture we are going to wrap up our discussion of word embedding methods by looking at how we can unify the concepts from word2vec and GloVe. This discussion is motivated by a recent paper called neural word embedding as implicit matrix factorization. The link has been provided in extra_reading.txt. If you want to check it out now just from the title we can sort of try to guess what approach this paper is going to take.

We know that neural word embedding means word embeddings learning from a neural network model like word2vec. And we know that matrix factorization is the model that we use for a GloVe. So what we're trying to do is show that word2vec can be framed as a matrix factorization problem. What I'm going to do is walk you through the important parts of this paper and hopefully give you enough of an idea of how you'd implement this. It should be pretty easy since we've already written code for Matrix factorization.

All you have to do is modify the objective which is what we're about to look at. So firstly let's look at what Matrix factorization looks like at a very basic level for a GloVe. We have a big matrix called X_ij, which is just the weighted log word-context counts between word i and j. And this is approximately equal to the input vector for word i dotted with the output word vector for word j. In this lecture we're going to try to come up with something in the same format but with a different matrix that we want to factorize.

First let's start with our objective function. It's going to look slightly different from what we've seen before. But rest assured it's the same to start let's just walk through what all these symbols here mean. So capital W represents the set of all words in our vocabulary. Little W stands for the input word w with an arrow on top it means the corresponding word vector. We don't normally draw arrows on vectors in my courses but we're doing it now to differentiate the word from its vector.

Next we have C which stands for the context word; c with an arrow means the corresponding word vector which by the way refers to the output weights of the neural network whereas the input word of course uses the input weights of the neural network. Next we have N which is a word drawn from negative sampling as before we don't usually use the letter N for this purpose. So just remember that this is for these slides only since n is a negative sample and it's being drawn from P_n, then we know that P_n stands for the negative sampling distribution which is also the smoothed unigram distribution.

And finally we have k which is just the number of negative samples. So at this point you know what all these symbols mean. So let's break down this equation first. If you look closely this is just the binary cross entropy or log likelihood. The first term is for the positive samples and the second term is for the negative samples. As you can see the sigmoid in the second term has a negative sign in front of the logits which means that this is the probability of the output being zero.

Second you'll notice that we have this count in front of the rest of the expression. Why? You'll notice that we're not summing over each sample in our data set like we usually do. Instead you see that this sum is explicitly taken over every possible middle word with every possible context word and of course in our dataset that middle word and that context word may appear several times. So this objective is the total objective for the entire dataset not just one sample and not just one sentence.

Now the second term is even more interesting you can see that it has this expected value symbol. Why might there be an expected value here. Well of course it's because negative samples are probabilistic. There are samples that we draw randomly so we don't know their actual values. We can only reason about their statistics. And we want to multiply this by K because for each word context pair we will have negative samples. All right. So hopefully you have a good handle on this equation.

Since everything that comes next is just manipulation of what you see here. So the next thing we can do is split out the objective into two separate terms. This way we can see how we can simplify the second term which is a little more complicated what you can see is that nothing actually depends on the context words c. So we can effectively just count the W's without even considering C. So that's one minor change we can make. The next thing we can consider is the expectation itself.

Note that the expectation in the limit is just the count of that item divided by the total. So we can write the expectation in terms of counts here I'm using the letter omega to represent the corpus. So the two vertical bars around Omega means the size of the corpus. Now what you can do with this is consider that our current input context pair is w and c. So I can somewhat trivially split out the context word c from this sum just so that it sits by itself.

So why do I want to do this. Well now I can take what I had before and then combined them to take things a step further in order to do this derivation. I really only need to consider a single entry in the target matrix meaning this entry depends only on the word vector W and the word vector c. So anything that doesn't depend on w and c I'm going to throw it out so I can look at this full objective function which contains the entire dataset and I can pick out only the part that depends on the current W and the current c. That allows me to get rid of all but one term from the negative sampling expectation.

I'll call this the local objective for the specific input word w and the specific context word c, so subscript WC. And what this has allowed us to do is get rid of all these summations and expectations from the original objective. Now this next part is definitely optional so I'm just going to skim through it. What's important to understand about this is the high level idea. Of course you're certainly encouraged to work through the technical details yourself which will help you understand how this works.

So what I'm going to show you are all the steps and all the little mechanical details you can probably do by yourself if you know calculus. As long as your calculus skills are decent So what I would like to do is find out what logit, and remember that's the thing that goes inside the sigmoid, optimizes this local J. Why do I care about this. Well whatever this logit is is equal to the dot product between w and C and I know that the dot product between w and C is equal to the (w, c)th entry of whatever matrix.

I'm trying to factorize again. I still don't know what Matrix I'm trying to factorize but now I can see clearly that w dot c gives me a matrix entry and this matrix entry will optimize this J. So I want to solve for whatever will optimize J. The first thing I want to do is replace w transpose C with a new scalar X and then optimize J with respect to x. How do I do this. Well of course we turn to our old friend calculus set the derivative to zero and solve for x and you might think that just because there's a sigmoid in there it's not possible but in fact it is.

So first we get an expression for the derivative. And remember I'm just walking you through the high level steps here. I believe that most people taking this course would have the skills to step through the details if they wanted to. Of course if you get stuck feel free to ask about it on the Q&A once we get this derivative we can manipulate it a little bit and recall that this is equal to zero. The next step is to recognize that this looks a little bit like a quadratic equation except that there is an exponent.

So if you set a new variable y equals e to the x then you get a quadratic which you can solve. Finally you can plug in the original variables to get that w dot c is equal to the log of this big term. Now you'll notice that I separated these terms out in a very specific way. The reason I did this is because these count proportions as you might guess represent probabilities. And in fact when these specific probabilities are combined in this specific way we arrive at a quantity called the pointwise mutual information or PMI matrix.

This is a well-known quantity in information theory just like the cross entropy and KL divergence. In general the pointwise mutual information between two discrete random variables x and y is log of P of x and y divided by p of x and p of y. Now you notice that in the expression we arrived there is this extra minus log k term. This just means it's something called the shifted PMI Matrix. But of course the basic idea is the same. And for a K equals one we would get back the unshifted PMI matrix.

Now one thing we can do is manipulate our PMI a little bit to express it in terms of quantities we already know about. So one term specifically the one that represents context comes from the unigram distribution, and we know that one simple modification we can make in word2vec is to smooth out this distribution by raising it to the power 0.75. And so expressing it in this way allows us to use the smooth distribution instead. One interesting thing that allows us to relate this to GloVe is if you discard most of the terms you can see that the Matrix we want the factorize is in both cases proportional to the log of the count of every word-context pair.

So this should give you some confidence that both word2vec and GloVe are doing very similar things. The last thing I want to talk about in this lecture is what the code is going to look like. So since the implementation uses Matrix factorization it's going to look a lot more like GloVe than it does like the original word2vec. Luckily we've done most of the work here. We know what the derivatives are. We know how to do alternating least squares to get the solution.

So most of the work you have to do is really just the counting part. You have to count up all the word context pairs and build the matrix you want to factorize. So if you feel like you're up for the task I would strongly recommend trying to implement this yourself.

2. PMI in Code
==============

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture I'm going to walk you through the code to implement the PMI version of word2vec in Python and NumPy. The relevant file for this lecture is pmi.py. First we have all the usual imports. Additionally we have some sparse matrix functions from SciPy, which help us work with sparse matrices where most of the entries are zero. Now at some point in this script we have to convert from sparse to dense array.

So as an exercise you might want to try to figure out how to keep the array as sparse over all the operations we do. So after that we have our data loading code which you've probably seen already. First we get the input files which are all the text files they start with enwiki. Next we have a function that removes punctuation. Next we set our vocab size V to 2000 and the number of context words to 10. Next we loop through all of our files to find the top V words.

This means we're going to count up all the words and then try to sort them. Note that because the vocab size is so small if we want to see certain words later we have to force their counts to be high. So here we have a set of keywords where we set their counts to infinity. Next we sort our word counts which gives us back a list of tuples where the first element is the word and the second element is the count. Next we reset V to be the min of current V and the actual vocab size.

We want to do this just in case the vocab size turns out to be smaller in which case you could use a smaller matrix. Finally we set our top words to be the top V words leaving a space for the unknown token. Next we reverse our top words array to get our word2idx mapping. Next we're going to build our PMI matrix. Note that this takes quite a while. So we only want to do this if there is no current PMI matrix saved. So the first thing we do is initialize a sparse matrix of size V by V.

Next we loop through each line of the file. Remember that we want to skip any lines that start with certain characters. Since these are just headings or metadata. Next we remove any punctuation from the line. Lowercase the line and then split the line on whitespace. From there we can convert each sentence into a list of word indexes. Now we have the data in a format we can use. So for each sentence we're going to loop through each word which we're going to consider our input word for each input word.

We're going to grab it's context the start position will be the current position minus the context window size. But if that's negative then we take zero the second position will be the current position plus the context window size. But if that's bigger than the length of a sentence we just take the length of a sentence finally we update our matrix of word context counts for each word-context pair we encounter. Once we're done we save the sparse matrix to disk since it takes such a long time to build.

Next we have the else part of this statement where if the file already exists we just load in the file to get the counts matrix. At this point we have the counts but we still need to build the PMI matrix. So first we need to get the unigram distribution. Currently we have counts over w and c but we need just count over c so we sum over axis equals zero. And remember we also want smoothing. So we raise it to the power 0.75. Next we normalize it. So we get probabilities and then we reshape it to a 2D array so that when we divide with it later it divides along the rows.

Now we're ready to calculate our final PMI matrix. So this is the word-context counts, divided by just the word counts, divided by the unigram probabilities. For the context words. Finally we take the log of this matrix to get the actual PMI matrix. Note that I've just called this log x in the code. For simplicity's sake since it was also called log x in the GloVe code. So there are a few hidden implementation details here. Firstly we want to add a one inside the log just in case one of the entries is 0 in which case this would break.

We also set any negative values in the matrix to 0. This is another thing they talked about in the paper if you want to check it out. After this it's time to do alternating least squares to find the word embeddings first. Note that I'm using the full model with all bias terms here. So we have the grand mean mu but also the input embedding bias and the output embedding bias. This shouldn't be too surprising since we already did the same thing for a GloVe next Let's scroll down to the super vectorized version of alternating least squares.

So in this code we can actually use np.linalg.solve not just on a matrix-vector combination but also on a matrix matrix combination. I just call it vectorized to be consistent with the GloVe code. So the sole function can solve anything in the form Ax equals B where x and B can also be matrices which is good because that's exactly what we have. And we can use that same strategy to update all the other parameters as well in a full vectorize form once we're done we print the training duration and plot our costs.

After that we do some test analogies which I'm sure you know what that looks like by now. So let's run this and see what we get. All right. So not surprisingly we get decent word vectors just like we did with word2vec and GloVe.

### 7. Using Neural Networks to Solve NLP Problems

1. Parts-of-Speech (POS) Tagging
================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in these next few lectures we're going to cover some classical NLP problems and discuss how neural networks can be used to solve them. The first of these problems is determining parts of speech or parts of speech tag is a category that you can assign to a word according to its syntactic function in English. The main parts of speech are noun, pronoun, adjective, determiner, verb, adverb, preposition, conjunction and interjection.

Now this is not an English course but if you learned English at some point which you must have in order to understand this course then you probably would have learned about these in grade school. In other words probably a very long time ago. If you aren't comfortable with how these work that shouldn't be too much of a problem. Remember that machine learning is about solving problems abstractly. If you can think of the models that we learn about in a general way rather than a specific way then your usefulness as a data scientist will be limited.

So how are we going to solve this problem. Well let's first look at the data. You can get the data from this URL. We want to download the files train.txt and test.txt and put these in a folder inside the class folder called chunking. Here's an example of what the data looks like. So each token is on a separate line and the tag is beside it. We are interested in the first two columns. You can see that every word has a corresponding tag a good thing to do when you're faced with a machine learning problem is to find a baseline. What's a very simple model

we know that can be used for classification logistic regression. You can see how this could be applied to logistic regression very easily. You would just one-hot encode the words and the tags and then do softmax you'll see that we can already do pretty good with just that over 90 percent accuracy. That means over 90 percent of the time a word has the same tag. It's very rare that one word can be used in two different ways. For example I drink milk this morning versus this company is milking its customers money.

We will also calculate the F1 score which is the harmonic mean of the precision and recall which are common measures in information retrieval and NLP. One question you might have is can we use the fact that these words show up in a sequence and therefore can we use the context to help us make better predictions. A great tool for that might be recurrent neural networks which we covered in deep learning part 5. So once we've established the baseline We'll look at how well a recurrent neural network can do.

We are still not done. In the precursor to recurrent neural networks you learned about Hidden Markov models in particular you can think of the words as the observed sequence and the tags as the sequence of hidden states so hidden Markov models seem perfect to solve this problem. There is one small twist however if you took the HMM class you remember that we used an algorithm called expectation maximization to train the HMM. Do you think we need that here.

The answer is No. Of course not. Here we are in an interesting situation where we actually know the hidden states of the training data. It's one of those cases where the hidden states are not some abstract hyper parameter but an actual real thing. Remember that HMMs are defined by three distributions: pi, which is the initial state distribution, A, which is the state transition matrix and B which is the observation probability matrix. We can actually calculate all of these using maximum likelihood directly.

For example pi would just be the frequency of the start tags. We know that a is Markov. So we can just count up all the transitions and divide by the row sum to get a. And the observation probabilities depend only on the current state. So that's just a probability of a word. Given the tag which we can also calculate just by counting. So to summarize here is how we'll use what we know to solve the parts of speech tagging problem. First we're going to use logistic regression to establish a baseline.

Second we're going to use recurrent neural networks to see if considering sequences of words can give us better accuracy. Third we're going to use hidden Markov models which also work on sequences to see if a simple generative algorithm can give us better accuracy.

2. How can neural networks be used to solve POS tagging
=======================================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to take a quick look at the models we'll be using to solve the parts of speech problem. As a reminder. The three models we'll be looking at are logistic regression. The Hidden Markov model and recurrent neural networks. This is going to be a high level lecture just to cover the basics for how these models work and how that allows them to be used for parts of speech tagging.

First stop is logistic regression as you may recall logistic regression which we reviewed earlier in the course is not designed for sequences in particular. We have an input and we have a target. We map from input to target with a weight matrix and at the output we take the softmax to get a posterior probability distribution on the possible labels. So in the simplest case the input is just a single word. The output is its parts of speech tag. And just like before since the input is just a word index we don't actually have to do any matrix multiplication.

We just index the weight matrix with the word index and take the softmax over the output. Of course we know why logistic regression is limited. It can only possibly create a one to one mapping from word to tag but we know that we can have the same word with multiple different meanings. As an example I just drank a glass of milk versus the thief wanted to milk her victims of their savings. So we know that context matters nonetheless. Logistic Regression achieves decent accuracy since most of the time context does not matter.

The next model will look at is the recurrent neural network a recurrent neural network in its simplest form looks almost like a feedforward neural network except that it has a feedback loop where the hidden layer goes back into itself. This allows the neural network to take into account data from past in the context of sentences that means any prediction I make now will depend on all the previous words in the sentence as usual. Since this is deep learning the way that it takes those previous words into account is by multiplying by a matrix.

So this is why taking past words into account is very important. Consider that we want to find p of tag given milk. Of course this is ambiguous because we don't know the context of the sentence. Milk can be a noun or a verb. We don't know but if we have the previous words in a sentence I just drank a glass of milk. Now we can see that milk should be a noun. It would make absolutely no sense for a verb to be there to make that even more clear. You could try plugging in another verb there to see how it doesn't work.

For example pick a verb like run. I just drank a glass of run. That doesn't make any sense. Now of course in modern times we use modern recurrent units such as the GRU and the LSTM. These have a ton of more parameters and a ton of more formulas but basically you can think of them as a little system of mini neural networks. As you can see each formula is just its own logistic regression which is a neuron at a high level. All you have to remember is that these units help us learn long term dependencies in the data and they help us avoid problems such as the vanishing and exploding gradient.

You can also plug and play with any recurrent unit since they all have the same API so you can replace a simple recurrent unit with a GRU and that's not even a one line change that's just changing the name of a class. So when the code if you see a GRU and you want to try to replace it with an LSTM that should be very simple.

Part 6 in this lecture we're going to use a recurrent neural network to solve the part-of-speech tagging problem. So let's begin by doing our imports. We've got numpy. So let's define our RNN class. It takes in the hidden layer sizes, so you're going to have multiple recurrent units, and the vocabulary size. So we're going to fit to X and Y. Default learning rate of 10 to the minus 5 0.99 momentum, we'll have 30 epochs, show_fig true, activation is ReLU by default, default recurrent unit will be GRU.

You could also try an LSTM if you want, and a flag to normalize the word embeddings . So let's just write a little bit of Main now to make some space at the bottom of this file. OK so let's assign D and let's assign K to make them easier to use. N is the number of sentences. So we'll initialize We as a V by D matrix. Now we need to initialize the hidden layers. So the first input is of size D. And then we loop through all the hidden layer sizes, create recurrent units and then add them to our list of hidden layers.

Once that's done we can define a softmax layer. So that's Wo and bo. Now we'll turn our We into a shared variable and also Wo and bo. And we're only going to assign Wo and bo to params in addition to all the params from the hidden layers but not We because We could possibly be normalizing. And so we'll update that differently. So th X will be a vector of ints th y will also be a vector of ints. And now we can start calculating the output of the neural network.

So we get the word vectors that Z loop through each hidden layer get its output and then softmax the last output . Next we can define a prediction and the cost. So it's going to be the softmax cross entropy. Next we define gradients. So these are all our params except the word embeddings we need our dparams for momentum. So we initialize those as zero. We also need a dparam for the word embeddings that really we probably don't. But it's fun.

So our update is just mu times the previous change minus learning rate times the gradient . And so now if normalized is true we will make the length 1 . OK. Next we define our update tuples . Don't forget to add the updates for the word embeddings . Next we define our cost_predict op. The inputs are th_x, th_y, outputs are cost and prediction, and we'll allow input downcast. Next let's define the train op. So the inputs are again th X and th y outputs are again cost and prediction and updates are the updates.

Now we are mainly going to use sequence indexes to index the sentences and we calculate n_total now because that's not going to change . So what we start the timer at the top. Shuffle the sequence indexes, set n_correct to zero, set cost to zero and the iterator to zero and we loop through each sequence index, call train with X[j] and Y[j], increment the cost, increment n_correct. Every 200 steps we'll do a printout: print out j, print out N, and print out the correct rate.

So we look through all the X's and Y's get our prediction and then increment n_correct. Once that's done we just return n_correct divided by n_total. Next we do pretty much the same thing for F1 score. Loop through all the Xs and Ys and we'll accumulate the predictions once we have all the predictions we can concatenate all the targets and all the predictions so that they are a flat list and then we can just return scikit-learn's F1 score. And so now let's fill in the rest of main So we get X train why train X test, Y test and word2idx from get_data.

5. Parts-of-Speech Tagging Recurrent Neural Network in Tensorflow
=================================================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to look at how to do parts of speech tagging using an RNN in TensorFlow. If you don't want to try to code this yourself although I strongly recommend you do the relevant file is pos_rnn_tf.py. The important thing to remember about TensorFlow is that the main difference between it and Theano is that TensorFlow requires all your sequences to have the same length.

So what this means is anything that is shorter than the longest sequence is going to be padded with zeros. What that means is you can think of your data as N by T by D. In other words it's a three dimensional block in terms of word vectors. That means you have N samples and each sample is a sequence of length t some of which may be just a bunch of zeros and D is the dimensionality of each word vector. Doing this is helpful because it allows us to process our data in batches which is more difficult

in Theano if you're going to have variable length sequences. So let's start out with the get data function. This is exactly like the Theano version but just in case you didn't watch that we're going to explain it again here. The first thing we do in this function is taking an argument split sequences. We need this because the data looks different when we're working with sequences. And when we're not. So with HMMs and RNNs we're working with sequences and with logistic regression which is our baseline we're not inside the function.

We have some useful print statements that warn you if your data isn't in the right place or if you haven't downloaded it yet. Next we get to the main part of this function. So we need two dictionaries one to keep track of the inputs which are words and one to keep track of the targets which are tags. One important difference between the Theano code and this code is that with Theano code we're starting counting from zero in the tensor code we're starting counting from 1.

Why do we want to do this. Well it's because TensorFlow needs all the input sequences to be the same size and in order to do that we need to pad the short sequences. The way TensorFlow does this is that it pads each sequence with zeros and therefore 0 is a special number that we can't use for anything else. So we need to start from one. Next we initialize the lists. X and Y train are going to be our final lists we return. So each element will be a sample and a sample will itself be a list containing word or tag indices. Current X and Y will contain a list of the current words in each sentence collected so far.

Next we loop through each line of the train file remember that each line contains one word and one tag and each sentence is separated by a blank line. So we do line.strip to check if the line is blank or not. If the line is not blank then we split the line to get the word in the tag. Then we check if this word is in our word2idx dictionary. If it's not we make a new index for this word and increment the index counter. Then we add the current word index to current X.

Next we do the same thing for the tag check if we have an index for it and if not make one then we add the tag to current Y. Next we go to our else clause. Remember that this means the line is blank only if split sequences is true. Do we have to do something here because remember if split sequences is false then the input is simply a word. And each target is just the corresponding tag. And we just ignore the blank lines. But if split sequences is true that means current X and current y are holding the sentence that we just read.

So we need to add that sentence to X train and Y train and then make current X and current y into empty lists to collect the next sentence. Next we do the exact same thing again. But for the test set I'm not going to walk you through this because it's almost the exact same thing. There's one minor but important difference that we're going to use the next word index to mean an unknown word a word is unknown. If it didn't appear in the trainset this is because we don't want the test set to contaminate the training process.

So just keep this in mind since it'll be important very shortly. Finally we return everything we just created: X train, Y train, X test, Y test and word2idx. As an exercise. It might be helpful to think about why we don't really need a tag to index. Next we have a flattened function. This is useful because our data is currently a list of lists. So if we need to grab a set of all elements in that list of lists we need to flatten it first. The next thing we do is make use of the functions we wrote, so we call get_data with split sequences equals true.

We set the vocab size to length of word2idx plus two. Now you might be asking why plus two? Recall that we started counting from 1. So this means that the highest index has to be at least one more. But I also recall that we use the final word index for unknown words in the test. So that's why we have to add yet another one for a total of plus two. The same thing happens with K the number of classes except that we can generally be confident that there aren't any unknown classes in the test.

Of course this code wouldn't even work if that were true. And by the way you can see here how we use the flatten function. We flatten each y to get a list of tags. Then we convert it to a set to get a unique set of tags. Then we get the union of the train and test tags and then we get the length of their union plus one that's the number of classes. Next we have some training configuration. Number of epochs learning rate etc. Nothing very exciting. We do need to get the max sequence length because we need this in order to be able to pad our sequences which is what comes next.

So to pad the sequences we can make use of the newly included keras module TensorFlow. Keras used to be a library built on top of TensorFlow. But recently Keras was added into TensorFlow itself which is nice. In any case this is just a NumPy function so it's totally fine to make use of it here. Recall that we need to pad everything to be the same length. So that includes the train inputs and targets and the test inputs and targets. Next we can start our TensorFlow code.

The first thing we need to do is to define placeholders for our inputs and targets. They're int32 because they're either word indexes or tag indexes and their shape is None by sequence length, where None is the batch size. We also want to get a symbolic variable for the number of samples since that will be useful later on. Next we can define some of our model weights in particular the word embedding matrix and the weight and bias of the final dense layer after creating them with their initial values.

We can wrap them TensorFlow variables. Next we create the RNN unit which of course goes in between the embedding and the final dense layer. Note that this will have parameters of its own but we don't really need to know what they are because TensorFlow is going to do all that calculation for us. I'm using the GRU here but of course the simple RNN unit and the LSTM both have the same interface. So you can feel free to use the unit of your choice Next we want to get the output of our model and from that build our cost.

So the first thing we need to do is pass the inputs through our embedding layer that's just tf.nn.embedding_lookup. Next we pass the output of the embedding into that GRU to do that. We call this function get on an output and pass in the GRU we just made and the data X one thing to keep in mind is that TensorFlow RNNs work in a very strange way instead of accepting data as a big 3D tensor of N by T by M, which other libraries do, TensorFlow does something different.

It requires a list of size t where each element is a tensor of size N by M. So that's odd but at least TensorFlow now gives us a function to do it called unstack. In earlier versions you would have had to do this manually. Hopefully in future versions of TensorFlow RNNs look a little bit nicer. So after we get the RNN output it's going to be in the wrong shape. So instead of being N by T by M where batch size comes first the T is going to come first because that's how it went into the r n n.

So we have to transpose the output to switch N and T. Once we do that we get back something of size N by T by M which is what we want but unfortunately we are still not done because the rest of TensorFlow still doesn't like 3D objects in particular matmul, which only works on proper matrices, which are 2D tensors. The cost function, which is the sparse softmax cross entropy function, also does not like 3D objects so we need to flatten our data to put the N and the T dimensions together.

But keeping the features separate. Once we've done that we can matmul with Wo and add bo to get the logits and once we've done that we can make our predictions which of course is just the argmax but we don't want these to be our final predictions because right now. the N and the T dimensions are mixed together. So to get our predict op we want to reshape yet again to split out the samples and the sequence length dimensions. Next we need to build the cost but remember the labels are still currently N by T.

So we need to flatten that as well. Once we've done that we can create our cost op and our train op And of course you are welcome to try different optimizers and different hyper parameters. So at this point most of the hard stuff is over. Now we just have our training to start we initialize our session and our variables just standard boilerplate code. Then we enter the training loop in order to calculate accuracy. We don't really want to include the padded portion of the data.

So we're going to keep count manually using n_correct and n_total. So for each batch we call cost_op, predict_op and train_op we accumulate our cost and then we have a little loop to keep track of our accuracy. So remember that 0 means paddings so we filter out all the entries where the target is zero. This gives us yi and pi. Then the number correct is just the number of entries where yi equals pi and the total number is just the length of y and every 10 steps.

We print stuff out at the end of the epoch we calculate the test accuracy using the same method as before then we print out a summary of that epoch. And finally we plot the loss. So let's run this and see what we get.

6. How does an HMM solve POS tagging
====================================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. The next model we'll look at is the HMM or hidden Markov model. Now for this course which is about deep learning, you don't have to know about HMMs if you don't want to. I'll teach you what you need to know in this lecture so you understand the basics and you understand the interface of the code. But you can safely ignore the implementation if you don't care about it.

Just understanding what's going on at a high level is adequate. If you have studied HMM before which is quite possible because I have a course on it then you will of course get more out of these lectures. So the HMM is technically an unsupervised model which just aims to maximize the probability of observing whatever data you give it. On the surface that doesn't sound so exciting. We know that RBMs do the same thing. The idea behind the HMM is that it's a latent variable model.

In other words there is some latent or hidden variables that cause what we see which we call the observations. We'll call these latent variables hidden states. Let's look at a very simple example of an HMO. Just to give you the basic idea. Suppose you're an office worker and I'm sure many of you can relate to this. Imagine that every now and then someone steals your lunch from the communal fridge. The problem is you have no idea who could be stealing your lunch if you frame this problem in terms of an HMM what you observe is either today nobody stole my lunch or today somebody stole my lunch.

So we'll encode these as 0 and 1. But you don't know what the hidden cause of your lunch being stolen is what you can do is attribute these observations to some hidden states. One key point is in an HMM when it's not necessary to know what the hidden states represent. In fact there's no requirement that they have to represent anything tangible. But in this case since it's a pretty clear cut problem we will represent the hidden states as something tangible.

So let's say you have two coworkers Alice and Bob. We might have three hidden states. One state is Alice works that day but not Bob. Another state is Bob works that day but not Alice. The third state is both Alice and Bob worked on the same day. For simplicity's sake assume that you are at work every day so your lunch is always in the fridge. As you might have guessed from the name the hidden states have a Markov relationship. So the probability of going from state 1 to state 2 depends only on state 1.

The probability of going from state 2 to state 3 depends only on state 2 and so on. The nice thing about hidden Markov models is it's very easy to interpret all these probabilities so you can actually measure the probability of each event very easily. This is just like we did in our bigram example in the Review section. You just count up the number of times something happens. Very simple. Notice how there are two relevant distributions here. First we need to know the distribution for going from one hidden state to another hidden state.

Second we need to know the distribution. For an observation given that you are in a hidden state both of these are easy to count for a simple example. As an example it's easy to count up the number of times you've had your lunch stolen when Alice happens to be in the office in the case where your hidden states have no explicit meaning and you can't measure them you would have to use more sophisticated techniques like expectation maximization. You definitely don't need to know that for this course but you are encouraged to look into it.

If you're interested one of the main algorithms we can perform on an HMM is the Viterbi algorithm. This allows us to look at the problem in the reverse direction. Normally we think of hidden causes as producing the observations that we see. This is the direction of causality. The Viterbi algorithm allows us to ask given a sequence of observations. What is the most likely sequence of hidden states. So an example or observation sequence would be a sequence telling us which days you had your lunch stolen and Viterbi algorithm would tell us the most likely sequence of states.

Or in other words who was working in the office on those days. You could then use that information to infer who was stealing your lunch now again the Viterbi algorithm could be a few lectures on its own. So we're not going to discuss that in this course. The most important thing is to be aware of and understand the API the input to Viterbi is a sequence of observations x1 all the way up to xT; the output is a sequence of corresponding hidden states z1 all the way up to ZT.

So how can we apply this to parts of speech tagging at a high level what are we trying to do. We're doing parts of speech tagging. We're taking a sentence which contains words and for each word we want to map that to a tag. So in this case the words are observed we are given those but the tags are hidden states. That's what we're going to use Viterbi algorithm to figure out. Now remember that in an HMM some hidden states have a Markov structure. Why does this make sense in the context of parts of speech tags.

Well it actually makes perfect sense. The rules governing how parts of speech go together is basically grammar. For example we would expect noun going to verb to show up very often. We would expect a verb going to verb. It's probably not common at all. So parts of speech tags are basically a probability model of grammar as an example consider the sentences. I walked to the store I ran to the store and I drove to the store and noticed that in these three sentences walked, ran and drove were all verbs.

Note that I is a pronoun. It makes sense in this case for a verb to come after the pronoun I. Would it make sense for any other part of speech to occur here. Probably not. So for example take a noun let's say house. Does this make sense. I house to the store. No the sentence does not make sense at all. That's because we do not expect a noun to come after the pronoun we expect a verb. So you can see that the ordering of parts of speech tags have to follow our rules of grammar.

And so a sequence model such as the Markov model is very appropriate for this. So to summarize here is how we're going to use HMMs for the parts of speech tagging. Step number one is to find the properties of our model. That means finding the distributions that tell us how to go from hidden state to observation and from hidden state to hidden state where again the observation is the word and the hidden state is the tag. Importantly in this problem during the training phase the hidden states are not really hidden because we're given the tags and the training data and therefore we can find all these probabilities simply by counting step number two is to use the Viterbi algorithm that lets us map from an observed sequence to a sequence of hidden states.

So in our case the input is going to be a sentence and the output is going to be a sequence of corresponding parts of speech tags.

====================================================

So we need a function to compute the accuracy because there is no real way to check if the hidden states are correct. It's not really part of the HMM. So we write accuracy custom. And we also write F1 score just to calculate it over all the individual sequences. So we assume that at this point we've already made all the predictions.

So we just concatenate everything in the targets concatenate everything in the predictions and then we call F1. Let's make some space at the bottom by calling main and now write the main function with a parameter here for smoothing so that we can smooth our state transition matrix and our observation matrix. So we'll get the data from the get data function split sequences is going to be true again. So the vocabulary size V is length of word2idx plus one.

And now the number of hidden States has to be the number of POS tags. So there are various ways of finding it. But this is a simple one just find the max and add one. So the state transition matrix will be an M by M matrix. We'll initialize it with all smoothing values. We'll initialize pi with zeros, so that will not have smoothing, and then we'll loop through all the Ys. These are the hidden states. y[0] is the start state, and y[i] to y[i+1] is a transition.

Once the loop is done we normalize all the distributions so that they all add up to 1 . Now we do the same thing for B. We add smoothing we loop through x y. So you have to be careful here. So the first dimension in B is the state and the second dimension is the observation that the state is actually a target so y[i] comes first. Then we normalize B. And then we set all the HMM parameters to the pi, A and B that we just found. Next step is to calculate all the predictions.

So we have a function from HMM that already does this. It's called get_state_sequence. So we do this both for the train set and the test set . Once we've got all our predictions we can print out the results so we print the train accuracy. 8. Named Entity Recognition (NER)
=================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture we're going to discuss another classical NLP problem named entity recognition . This is similar to parts of speech tagging where we want to tag each token in a sentence. However with named entity recognition we're interested in what kind of entity the token is. For example it could be a person a company or a location. These are all proper nouns and we want to be able to train models to identify these.

One difference between this problem and parts of speech tagging is that NER data is very imbalanced. By that I mean most of the tokens in the data set are not entities at all they are just regular words as I'm speaking right now. You'll notice that almost 100 percent of the words I say are just words. They're not anyone's name or place or company. So that's something we may or may not have to correct for now what is one way to make NER really easy? In classical NER solutions.

They would use the fact that the first letter was capitalized to tell if it was a named entity or not . You can see how this might be considered cheating. We would rather use the structure of a sentence than the fact that it starts with a capital letter to determine whether it's a named entity. In modern times that approach wouldn't work very well anyway. Why is that. Well on sites like Twitter and in chat messages people often don't capitalize proper nouns so we can't use rules like if tokens starts with a capital letter and has a higher probability of being a proper noun.

At that point we're not even doing machine learning. We're just doing regular programming because of this in our code. We're going to lowercase everything first. We also don't want to consider usernames as actual names since that would just also be regular programming in which you could achieve 100 percent very easily for any problem. We are again going to first establish a baseline and then see what happens when we use sequence modeling with recurrent neural networks the baseline with logistic regression is going to have the exact same format as the parts of speech tagging problem inputs are one-hot encoded words and outputs are tags .

Similarly the RNN is also going to have the same format. So for both of these We're going to just leverage our existing code to get the data for this example go to this GitHub repo, just download the file and put it in the same folder as the code. It's a relatively small data set of some tweets. It's again a very simple format. Each token is on a separate line and the tag is beside it.

================================

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We are going to talk about how to frame the named entity recognition problem so that it is no different from the parts of speech tagging problem. As you'll often hear me say this is just a consequence of my rule that all data is the same. Now how might this be useful. Well actually it's very useful because it means we don't have to write any new code all the code we wrote already.

Well, the only thing we need to change is how we load the data. But once the data is in the same format as the POS data then all the same code we wrote earlier can be applied. So let's remind ourselves what parts of speech tagging data looks like we have sentences and for each word in each sentence we have a tag. The tag is just a category which means we're doing classification. Now let's look at what a named entity recognition example looks like.

We have sentences for each word in each sentence. We have a tag. It's just that in this case the tag is simply a different set of categories. In particular they tell us whether or not that word is a named entity and if so what kind of named entity it is so you can see now why NER is not a different problem from POS tagging and that this is because all data is the same. We have a similar situation in computer vision. For example if we're building a CNN to differentiate between pictures of dogs and cats this is no different from a CNN that differentiates between pictures of cars and trucks even though the data has changed the code doesn't have to because the structure of the data has not changed.

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6. So in the previous two examples we did parts of speech tagging and named entity recognition. So what can we learn from this. Well it goes back to what we learned about in my linear regression class. When you add too much complexity you overfit. So you need to decrease the complexity until you hit a sweet spot of good accuracy on the train set and good accuracy on the test set. Another thing you learned about is the law of diminishing returns. A lot of the time a simple classifier is going to do very well. You don't necessarily need anything complex.

And when you do use something complex it's going to take a lot more effort perhaps it's going to take 100 times longer to train and yet only provide a 1 percent increase in accuracy. At this point you have to ask yourself Is it worth it. Engineering is all about making tradeoffs. Sometimes we use linear equations to model nonlinear systems because it makes things easier for us. If the error is tolerable then it's a good tradeoff. There is no need for more complexity as an engineer you should think about these things and balance your results and the effort it takes to get those results.
