### 8. Recursive Neural Networks (Tree Neural Networks)

1. Recursive Neural Networks Section Introduction
=================================================

Hi everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. We're going to start a new section of the course which is on recursive neural networks to motivate this section. Let's look at how we've progressed in terms of how we've been able to represent sentences in machine learning models. The first and simplest way that we always learn about in machine learning is bag of words. That means there's no order associated with the feature vector that represents a sentence.

Consider the phrases dog toy and toy dog immediately we can see that these two phrases have totally different meanings and yet a bag of words model treats them exactly the same. So machine learning model wouldn't be able to tell a difference between these two. The data itself is fine the information is there. It's just that the feature representation is limited. Next we looked at recurrent neural networks. We know that these are more powerful because they let us model sentences as sequences of words and so we have the context of all the words in the sentence that came before the current word.

This is powerful but is it the most powerful. We know that learning long term dependencies is a challenge for RNNs. Even with modern recurrent units such as the GRU and LSTM. This makes sense because even for us humans reading a long sentence takes a lot more effort than reading a short sentence but consider how we as humans have learned to parse really long sentences. We don't really just read it from start to end do we instead we may read a sentence multiple times we start to compartmentalize parts of the sentence turning it into a sort of nested structure.

In other words a sentence becomes hierarchical or in other words like a tree. And so it makes sense then that in order to understand language we should build a neural network to have the same structure as the sentence it's trying to model. So let's outline what you're going to learn in this section of the course. First we're going to spend quite a bit of time on theory. The theory in this section is not easy. Sure trees are easy to understand and visualize at a very high level but to actually implement them takes significantly more effort.

So the first thing we need to talk about is what does the data look like and how can we convert that into a tree data structure in code. And this is all without even discussing neural networks. Second we're going to look at two different models and also two different ways to build them. So the two different models we're going to look at are the plain recursive neural network and the recursive neural tensor network or RNTN. Basically the difference between these two is that the plain recursive net uses a linear transformation and the RNTN uses a quadratic transformation.

We'll also look at two different ways to build these models. The first way is a very naive approach and involves building a separate symbolic graph for each sentence in our data. This is of course required because each sentence has a different tree structure. That means each neural network we use to represent those sentences must have a different structure. And that means if I have N different data points then I need N different neural networks. But do keep in mind that these neural networks have shared weights and of course this is going to totally kill your memory.

So the second method We'll look at is implementing a recursive neural network as a custom built recurrent network. How can this be possible you might ask. Well this goes back to your algorithm studies which is something you would have learned in your undergraduate computer science courses if you took such a program. The gist of it is we can uniquely convert a tree into a sequence using different tree traversal algorithms. This gives us a one to one correspondence between each tree and each sequence.

Of course once we have a sequence we know that RNNs will work. And with RNNs we don't need a unique network to represent each sentence. OK so we're converting recursive nets into recurrent nets. So let's just summarize that since this lecture has been quite long already. This is what the outline of this section is going to be. First we're going to talk just in a general sense about sentences and trees and some of the algorithms involved.

We'll also look at the data and how it's structured then we're going to look at a few different implementations. First we're going to look at what happens if you try to build a recursive net the naive way which is to use actual recursion. We'll see that this will kill your memory. And if you don't believe me you can try running that script yourself. Note that it is possible to reduce memory usage by continually saving and reloading the weights and in the process destroying any of the graphs lying around in memory before the previous save.

But we're just not going to bother doing that since we know there's a better way to implement recursive nets then we're going to look at both plain recursive nets and RNTN in Theano. When you build them the smart way then we're going to look at RNTN in TensorFlow again building them the smart way. Finally I want to make some meta notes about this direction. First is that when I originally created this section TensorFlow did not have the capabilities required to implement an RNTN, or at least they were not well known.

Today the situation has changed and you can now build an RNTN in TensorFlow the same way you would in Theano. It is however a very heavy exercise in what I call API hunting. In other words a lot of the work is just to find the right function that does what you need it to do. So even if you know how the thing works in principle and you know exactly how the math works it's another matter entirely to try to find the right functions to call second.

I want to note that this is one of the most challenging sections in all my deep learning courses so don't be afraid if you dont understand it all right away. It requires skills from algorithms which is also one of the hardest courses you can take in addition to being able to build an RNN from scratch. So take your time and make sure you implement as much code as you can. Implementing things in code even just the data processing parts is critical to your understanding.

2. Sentences as Trees
=====================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture. I'm going to show you a very simple example of how and why we can represent a sentence as a tree sort of as a warm up to the next lecture which is going to be more compact and concise. So the first thing you need to know for this lecture is what a parts of speech tag is. Good thing we covered that earlier in this course. So not too complicated a noun is a person place or a thing a verb is an action word.

For example walk run or jump a determiner has a more complicated definition but you can think of it as a word that describes a relationship with noun. Some examples are "the", "a" and "my". So for example if I say a dog that has a different meaning than my dog saying my dog means the dog belongs to me but saying a dog implies that the dog could belong to anyone. OK so why do we need to know parts of speech? It's because that's generally how sentences are split up into trees.

These are best demonstrated by example. So here's the sentence John hit the ball. So first we can see the parts of speech tag for each individual word. John is a noun. Hit is a verb. The is a determiner and ball is another noun. We can see that "the ball" goes together to form a noun phrase. We can see that hit goes with the ball to make up the verb phrase. And finally we can see that John goes with hit the ball to make up the final sentence. So we can see the language is hierarchical.

If we look at very long sentences we can start to see patterns form. In particular we can see that a noun phrase is often followed by a verb phrase and a verb phrase is replaceable by a verb. So you can sort of think of them as the same. In computer science we talk about class hierarchies. So for example you might have a Shape class and from that you can have derived classes like circle or square and you can always trust that if you have a circle object it is always also an instance of shape.

So it's kind of like that. So here we have this sentence the angry bear chased the frightened little squirrel. But if we replace the verb phrase with a simple verb we would still get a valid sentence. For example the angry bear ran. We could also replace the noun phrase with a simple noun. And that still gives us a valid sentence. For example Bob chased the frightened little squirrel importantly for this section we're doing a task called sentiment analysis.

This means that we want to build a model to tell us how positive or negative something is. For example this movie sucks has negative sentiment whereas this movie is great has positive sentiment in this section. Our sentences will not only be parsed as a tree but each node in the tree will also have a label to tell us the sentiment. The reason we want to be able to do this is to handle the problem of negation which has traditionally been very difficult for existing NLP models but with a tree you can see that even if an entire phrase is negative or positive combining it with another phrase can reverse its meaning.

For example if the review was just "there are slow and repetitive parts", of course we would just consider that a negative review. But we need to consider the other phrase in the sentence it has just enough space to keep it interesting", which we might regard as a positive. So that second the positive phrase sort of takes over the meaning of the whole sentence and that's probably thanks to the word "but", which we can see here has neutral sentiment. Typically when we use the word "but" it's in the form of a sentence like here's what I thought momentarily but this is what I think overall.

So when you see the word "but" it's usually the second part of the sentence that matters and representing the sentence as a tree allows us to model that relationship. Here's another example we can see that the word humor has very positive sentiment but it's also very far down in the tree. So the overall sentence has the potential to change its meaning and indeed we can see that the sentence starts with this film doesn't which negates the positive part of the sentence.

Also notice that when we're doing sentiment analysis we don't actually care what the parts of speech are. That only really helped us to parse the tree itself but once that tree has been parsed we don't need that information any longer. So that was your simple one minute introduction to how sentences can be represented as trees instead of sequences in the coming lectures. Well look at how these trees are actually represented in our data files and how we might parse them in code which will bring us to how we can create recursive neural networks.

3. Data Description for Recursive Neural Networks
=================================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture we are going to take a closer look at the data we'll be working with for the next few lectures on recursive neural networks. So far we've seen two ways of representing sentences bag of words in a sequences but the third and most powerful way is to think of sentences as trees. Sentences have a dependency structure. For example think of a very simple sentence just a noun and a verb: Johnny walks Johnny is the noun and walks is the verb.

But we can make longer sentences with the same structure which make up a noun phrase and a verb phrase . So for example Big John walks quickly. So here Big John is the noun phrase and walks quickly is the verb phrase. Big John can then be split up into its constituent parts and walks quickly can also be split up into its constituent parts. Now we have a tree now just as an aside. How were these trees built. Surprisingly this is also a machine learning problem.

We won't discuss how to do it ourselves. We will just use the results as a more philosophical thought. Can you imagine an entire system of neural networks all performing different tasks such that the end result is something actionable in the real world. So for example if you were building a brain the language processing system would be just a small part but you might have a neural network to do parts of speech tagging another neural network to do named entity recognition and another neural network to parse sentences into trees and that's just a small part of an entire brain and even more challenging problem might be to figure out what is a general architecture we can use so that we don't even have to tell the system to learn these things.

In other words a network of neural networks where each neural network can figure out what it should do on its own and be useful for the overall system in a global manner. In any case let's return to the problem at hand. There are a few ways to represent sentences as parse trees in my easy NLP course you saw an example of a tree that included relations In general a node in a tree can have any number of children such as this one for us we will be working with binary trees.

That means each node will only have up to two children. The binary tree will be using such that if a node has children it always has two children. So there will be no situations where a node only has one child. When we did recurrent neural networks the task we did was next word prediction. So we were able to build a language model by maximizing p of x of t given x of t minus 1 all the way to x0. Now we have trees. Is there such a thing as next.

No because trees are not a sequence. They are hierarchical. Instead we are going to do sentiment analysis. Recall that we also did sentiment analysis in our easy NLP class using bag of words as input. Remember that the disadvantage to this is that it can't handle negation. If you have one-hot code words adding a "not" or "isn't" to the input vector we'll give you another vector very close to the original vector. So it's very hard for any machine learning model that uses bag of words to handle negation.

What's interesting is that recursive neural networks finally solve this problem. Research on sentiment analysis got about 80 percent accuracy at best until recursive neural networks came along. Now we can achieve about 85 to 90 percent accuracy. So why do recursive neural networks work so well. Well if you have negation then it's very easy to just reverse whatever is in the phrase that it's negating because the parse tree is structured to identify these dependencies.

The neural network can use them to do negation. Now what does this suggest. It means that we are going to have a label for every node in the tree. So let's go back to the sentence tree which we talked about a little bit. All we know so far is that it's a binary tree. And each node has either 0 or two children. So let's add to this to be able to understand negation will have labels for every node. One more fact you need to know that you could easily figure out yourself just by looking at the data is that only leaves of the tree represent words any inner node represents a phrase made up of words that are its descendants.

All right so now you know everything about the structure of the tree. So what does the data actually look like. First I'm going to remind you where to get the data you want to download the file trainDevTestTrees_PTB.zip. It's just a bunch of movie reviews. You might be wondering how did the researchers actually get all these labeled data. Well they use the service called Amazon Mechanical Turk where people do random tasks for beer money .

So they were all given very short phrases down to the word level and asked to rate them from one to five, five being the highest sentiment one being the lowest sentiment and three being neutral in our data. Of course these are labeled 0 to 4 because the data needs to be represented as text. The researchers use parentheses to separate each node. So for example a great movie would be represented by this text and it would be a height-one binary tree that represents the sentence great movie as an exercise.

I want you guys to try to write code to parse these files and turn them into tree objects. Each tree object will have a left and right child which can either be None or more tree objects. Each tree object will have a word attribute that can either be None if it's an inner node or a word if it's a leaf node. This is an algorithm exercise and it's the type of question you would get on a job interview. Just a heads up. This can be a relatively challenging task.

You will need to think in order to solve it. As a side note one pattern I've noticed is that people who do not make sure they can write this code for themselves have a very hard time progressing. I think it's pretty obvious that the reason the courses are separated as they are is because each one is more challenging than the last and you need to build up your skill level for practice and exercise .

4. What are Recursive Neural Networks  Tree Neural Networks (TNNs)
==================================================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to talk about the architecture of recursive neural networks. We are going to talk about how you can create a recursive neural network both for binary trees and for trees with any number of children. Sometimes people refer to recursive neural networks as RNTN but then it becomes too easy to confuse with recurrent neural networks.

It's an unfortunate coincidence. However you'll see later why it might be appropriate. I'm going to sometimes refer to them as tree neural networks because that's what they are. As a bonus it'll give us a separate acronym to use TNN. The easiest way to understand them is to think about the parse tree of a sentence because we're using binary trees and our data will discuss binary trees for us. Your recursive neural network will essentially have the exact same structure as the parse tree.

The question is Where do the weights go and how do we compute the output. Because the structure is recursive. We can only define a node's value by its children. When you look at its children those are also nodes. So those nodes can only be defined by its own children and so on. Because of this we only need two weights one for the left to tell us how the left child connects to the current node and one for the right to tell us how the child connects to the current node.

Notice how there's only one bias term. As an exercise, think about why having two bias terms might be redundant. So here we are showing how to calculate the value at the hidden node H1. Now let's calculate the value at the root. You can see that it depends on h1 on its left and W3 on its right. The question you might have next is what is the size of these weights. Well remember that the structure is recursive so that at any node its children might be words or its children might be other nodes but the weights have to fit with both.

Therefore the weights must all be the same size and its input size must match its output size. In other words if your embedding is of size D then all the W's must be of size D by D and all the biases must be of size D. Note that the activation function f can be any of the usual activation functions we use. Relu, tanh and so on. So what's next. Well if we're doing a task like sentiment analysis and we have five classes or two classes depending on how you frame the problem how do you get an output of size 5 or of size 2? Remember that any node can have a label.

So we must be able to calculate P of y given H for any h, an inner node, the root node or a single word to do that all we need to do is multiply that node's value by the output weight at the output bias and softmax as usual. By doing softmax we can have any number of classes two or five would be treated exactly the same. Note that even though the inner nodes are labeled We don't necessarily have to use them during training . In fact we'll write code for both.

And you can do your own experiments to see if there is a difference in the results. Now that we fully defined what a binary recursive neural network would look like what about a recursive neural network with any number of children you can see that by summing over each child we naturally extend the definition of the binary relationship. So you might wonder how do we even get a tree that's not binary in the first place? Stanford's NLP tools actually give us a way to generate our own parse trees from arbitrary text.

It's outside the scope of this course but you can try it if you want. The disadvantage of doing that is you won't have a label data set. Now what's interesting is that they do not have a tool that's as easy to use to get binary trees. Which is why we're very lucky that they've done the work of generating the data for us. They've written some Java code to produce binary trees. But you would need to know Java in order to use it and it would still not have labels.

In any case the number of possible parent child relationships is obviously limited. So let's call that limit R. So there are R possible different dependency relations because of this we can make a big W matrix that contains all the R different D by D matrices and multiply the input by the appropriate relation weight to get its output value. You can think of the binary tree as a simple case of this where there are only two types of relations left and right.

So let's summarize what we learned in this lecture. First you learn that recursive neural networks or TNNs are just trees of the same shape as the sentence that we're using as input. We looked at how to calculate the node values and the predictions in a binary tree and we looked at how to extend that to n-ary trees. You'll learn that we can use either only the root label or all the labels to train. And as an exercise you're going to try both on your own for our first code example.

We will code our TNN so that it can support an arbitrary number of children

5. Building a TNN with Recursion
================================

Hey guys welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6. Before we go on to implement a recursive neural network it is important to discuss how we're actually going to build it rather than me just walking you through the solution. You really want to try building one yourself before watching me do it. Otherwise the aha moment you'll get when you see the solution is not going to happen. In fact you shouldn't even watch this video until you try building a recursive neural network yourself .

So if you haven't tried yet stop this video now. I'll be right here when you get back. OK. So hopefully you try to build a recursive neural network yourself using the recursive properties of trees to make your implementation clean. I will actually demonstrate later how to build a recursive neural network using recursion. It will be TensorFlow since that's the original language I wrote it in, and it's not vital for this course and it's completely optional to watch but it will show you the downsides of implementing a TNN the obvious way.

Another bonus of it being written in TensorFlow is that you can't cheat. I wanted you to be able to write a recursive net in Theano so you'll still need to write yours from scratch. Let's look at what your recursive neural net might look like. Suppose we're doing sentiment analysis and we want to use the labels only at the root node of the tree . So the input into our fit function is a list of trees and we'll train our model on each tree using stochastic gradient descent.

Usually getting a prediction from a neural network in Theano is very straightforward. You're just passing your input vector through the multiple layers, softmax at the end and then you have your output but our input is not a vector. It's a tree object and that tree object only has vectors at the leaves. In fact it doesn't directly even have vectors; it has word indexes and we have to pass those into our word embedding matrix to get the vectors. So let's start simple.

We usually have a forward function that takes in a symbolic input and produces a Theano output. The thing that's returned isn't an actual number array; it's just a graph node which has to be evaluated by calling a function. This gives us somewhere to start from. Let's say in our recursive neural network we have a forward function but it takes in a tree. We'll call it forward_hidden since it only needs to calculate up to the nodes value. We only care about p of y given X for the root node.

So we can do that outside this function. Since this is recursive the forward hidden function actually needs to call itself. In particular it needs to call itself on its left child if the tree has a left child and it needs to call itself on its right child. If the tree has a right child otherwise it should get the word embedding for the word that this tree node represents . Now we have everything we need to get the value at the root node. We simply call forward_hidden and it does its recursive calls and we get the root node value by calling forward_hidden on the root node.

Once we have that we can run it through the softmax layer and we get p of y given x. Then we calculate our cost as usual gradients as usual and updates as usual and so on. Actually that was pretty simple. Hopefully you did it on your own before watching this lecture. So what's the problem with this solution. Well remember how Theano automatically finds gradients and does its updates. It creates a graph and the error travels backwards along this graph to do back propagation.

Well the problem is every input tree is going to result in a different graph. So that means for every sentence in the input will have a different graph different gradients different weight updates and so on. What does that mean. It means Theano has to figure out all that stuff. For every single sentence in your corpus resulting in huge RAM usage in addition to taking a very long time. So the moral of this story is while this solution seems like the obvious one at first,

it's not ideal

6. Trees to Sequences
=====================

Hey everyone. Welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we're going to look at the solution to the problem we discovered in the previous lecture and that's how to avoid having to compile a different graph for every tree. The basic idea is that we want to transform our recursive neural networks into recurrent neural networks . Now you know why this is deep learning part 6 and why it comes after deep learning part 5 which focused on recurrent neural networks.

Let's think about what our TNN is for and what are our RNNs for? Well TNNs are for trees and RNNs are for sequences. So the way that we are going to convert our problem from a TNN into an RNN is to convert our parse trees into sequences. How can we do this. It again involves the use of algorithms. If you've never taken an algorithms course I would highly recommend doing so since it will greatly improve your programming skills. One simple way of turning a tree into an array is to arrange the array elements in such a way that all the children appear to the left of its parent and the value of each element can be its parents index .

We'll call this the parents array. Notice that we use the null value minus one for nodes that don't have parents. Only the root should not have a parent. So what's the algorithm that allows us to construct trees in this way. We know that a parent has to come after it's children in algorithms terminology. This is called post-order traversal. It means you visit the left node first then the right node then the current node and you do this recursively for our specific dataset.

We want to know how a child is related to its parent. So it could have a relation defined by the parse tree or the relation can be a simple left right that we have in the binary tree case. So you can see that this structure will work for both binary trees or trees with any number of children . Notice that we've again use the null value of minus one in the case where there is no relation. Finally we want to know what word is stored at a node if any. So we need to store another list the same length as the others that stores either the word index.

If it's a leaf node or minus one if it's not a leaf node. So to summarize that we need three separate arrays to store a tree the parent's array which tells us where to find the parent of a node. The relation's array which tells us how a child is related to its parent and the words array which tells us if the node has a word associated with it and what the word indexes to create these arrays. You need to make sure that a parent always comes after its children which requires you to use post-order traversal .

Now that we know how to turn a tree into a set of lists let's discuss how we would run this through a recurrent neural network. This is slightly more complicated than what we did in deep learning part 5 because we only had one array the array of word indexes. Now we have three. So what do we do with them. The idea is we want to keep a running list of all the tree neural values and then update these values as we go through the arrays. We can go through each element of each array one by one.

So for example we'll look at parents of 0 relations of 0 and words of 0. At the same time then we'll look at parents of one relations of one and words of one at the same time . Why is this OK. Because everything we need to determine a node's value depends only on its children because its children are always to the left of its parent in the array. We are certain that we've calculated those values first . So in terms of the Theano scan function we can just loop over range(n) where n is the number of nodes and then use the current sequence value to get all the necessary information from the parents list the relation's list and the words list we can consider the parents list the relations list and the words list as non sequences.

So what should we do in the recurrence function. Two things. The first thing is let's calculate the value at the current node n. If it's a word then it's just the word vector for this word. If it's not a word then it's just f of its current value. Notice our use of set_subtensor here, which you learned about when we did word2vec. You'll see shortly why hiddens of n already contains all the right stuff. By the time we get here it's because the second part is to feed the current value into its parent.

So whether the current node is a word or it's an inner node it doesn't matter the way it feeds its value into its parent is the same: it's just its own value times Wh for its child parent relation add it to whatever value is currently at the parent. If the node is the root and it doesn't have a parent we won't do anything here. Next we need to clean our code up a little bit. You might wonder can Theano handle if statements. It cannot. Because remember that Theano needs to create a graph and the graph can't be made up of arbitrary Python code.

As an analogy we also use scan instead of for loops. So instead of an IF statement we are going to use the Theano switch function. The first argument is the condition. The second argument is what to return if the condition is true in the third argument is what to return if the condition is false. Now that we have all the tools we need to convert our trees into lists and implement tree neural networks let's move on to the code

7. Recursive Neural Network in Theano
=====================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we are going to implement a tree neural neural network in Theano and we're going to do the sentiment analysis task. So you should have done that on your own PTB stands for Penn Treebank. By the way. So let's create our recursive NN class. Vocabulary size embedding dimension and number of classes go into the constructor. Now let's define a function. So even though there are really x's and y's they're all contained in trees. So we just have trees as input. Don't worry about these learning rate mu and regularization values going to change them later. Although you are encouraged to play around with your own.

Now we have a flag here for train_inner_nodes. So that's for training on all the labels on every node or just training on the root node labels. Make some space at the bottom for calling main. Now let's go back to the fit function . So we reassign D, V and K for easy access, f is self.f, the activation, and N is the number of trees. We initialize the word embedding, we initialize Wh to a 2 by D by D matrix. So init_weight probably could have been defined better such that we could use that for both.

But something you can do on your own and we have Wo and bo for the softmax layer. Next step is to turn all these into Theano shared variables. So that's We, Wh, bh, Wo and bo. Next we set these to our params list for easy access later on . Next we need to create our Theano inputs. So we have three lists that represent our trees: words parents and relations. And remember we also need labels. So we have four lists in total. Next we define a recurrence function.

So it takes in n, which is the current sequence value, hiddens, which is the output, in words parents and relations which are non sequences. So we have our switch statement which basically means if W is greater than or equal to zero we will set this hidden to the corresponding word vector. Otherwise we'll take the activation function of the activation plus bias of course. Next we get the relation and we get the parent. So the relation is from this child to its parent.

Now if p is greater than or equal to zero. We add this current value to the parents hidden. Otherwise we just reassign hiddens. And then at the end we return hiddens. So hiddens starts out as an N by D matrix, or you can think of it as a sequence of N D-size vectors . Now we create our Theano scan so the function is recurrence, outputs_info is hiddens and steps is the length of words. So there should be words, not word; we'll change it later. Sequences is T.arange(words.shape[0]) and non_sequences is of course words parents and relations.

So here we're doing the softmax of h[-1]. Verify, convince yourself that h[-1] is what I want here. If you want you can try debugging this code and looking at the shape of h it would be a good exercise for trying to figure out how scan works . So we get the regularization cost and train_inner_nodes is true. We want to take the cross entropy of P of y given X at every node. Otherwise we just want p of y given X at the last node which corresponds to the root.

So labels minus 1 also corresponds to the root. So now we calculate our gradients. We have our dparams for a momentum . Next we define our updates. So it's just the usual p equals mu times dp minus learning rate times g. And then we have the momentum parameter updates. Next we define our cost_predict op. Inputs are the four lists: words, parents, relations and labels. Outputs are cost and prediction, and we'll set allow_input_downcast to true. Next we define the train pretty much the exact same thing except we also have the updates.

Next we create an empty costs list and then we enter the main training. So since we want to calculate the correct rate as we go we need n_total. So it depends if we're training inner nodes or not. If we are training inner nodes then n_total is the number of total nodes, otherwise n_total is just big N. Which is the number of trees. So we start the timer so we know how long each epoch takes, we shuffle sequence indexes on each round and will accumulate cost and have a variable it to count.

Since J is not going to be in order. So we get the words parents relations and labels from trees of j, we call train_op with those four lists . We get back cost and prediction. We update the cost If train_inner_nodes is true then we sum up all the things we got correct. Otherwise we just check the root. Don't forget to increment it and every one step will do a printout so a printout iteration number. How far along we are the correct rate and the cost .

So once that loop is done we print our epoch printout. So we want to print i, the epoch number, the cost, the final correct rate and the total time that this epoch took, and we append the cost to our costs. Once that's all done we plot the costs. OK. So we're ready to write our score function. This just takes in trees, which really represents x and y. So n_total is just the number of trees. We'll accumulate n_correct. So for the score we only care about the label at the root node.

So we just get the prediction pass in the same four lists and accumulate n_correct looking at only the root node and we just return n_correct divided by n_total. OK so now we need to write some data processing functions so add_idx_to_tree. Every tree needs an index. And this is going to correspond to its index in the list that it eventually lives in. So this is recursive. Remember that it's doing post-order traversal So we look at tree.left first, then tree.right.

And then the current index. Notice how we're just passing around current index incrementing it on each call . Next we write tree2list. So this is going to return four things. It takes in tree, parent_idx, is_binary for the labels, is_left and is_right, meaning is it a left child or is it a right child. And so the four lists that we want to return are the ones we were working with above . And so if this is a left child we set the relation to 0. Otherwise we set the relation to one.

That's how we index W H from before the word is either minus one or the word index. And we also call tree2list list recursively using post-order traversal. OK so you can tell that this returns words, parents relations and labels. So the tree that it takes in right now is tree.left and is_left is true, then we call it on the right side with tree.right and is_right as true and as the parent index we pass in the current index.

This is why we defined add_idx_to_tree above. And so now we construct the return values so that the words list will be everything from the left and everything from the right and then the current word the parents will be everything from the left. Everything from the right and the current parent and the relations will be everything from the left . Everything from the right and the current relation. So if is_binary is true we need to change the label. So three and four will consider positive zero and one we'll consider negative.

And if it's exactly 2 we'll return minus 1 and then we'll filter these out later. And if it's not binary we just take that label as is. That's how we construct our return labels. And finally we return all the things we constructed words parents relations and labels. Next step is to define main takes in is_binary. Default is true . 8. Recursive Neural Tensor Networks
===================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture we're going to extend the recursive neural network to get an even more expressive recursive neural tensor network. It's a very fancy name but as you'll see it's a very simple concept. Note that everything in this lecture assumes we're working with binary trees. So you already know how a recursive neural network is built. Let's look at an alternative view of the TNN since this was how it was represented in the paper that first introduced this model. To get the inner node value we concatenate the values from the left and the right child.

Call this X then the value at this node is just h equals f of W transpose times x plus B. Note that this requires W to be of size 2D by D because all the inner node values must be of size D and after concatenation X is of size 2D we still have the same number of parameters as before because before we had W left and W right which were both of size D by D. So what's a natural way to extend this model. Well we can add a quadratic term. So a is the quadratic parameter.

Now what should the size of A be? Notice I've only shown one component of H here. It's written such that each term is a scalar since it's difficult to represent tensor multiplication in terms of matrices in order for the quadratic term to be a valid multiplication. A_j needs to be a matrix. It needs to be a matrix of size to d by 2D Since x is of size 2D. Since there are d components of age so that J goes from one to D. That means A is of size D by 2D by 2D the functional form should remind you a lot of something we've seen in the past in particular in my deep learning prerequisites logistic regression class.

We looked at the closed form solution for logistic regression which we could find if both classes had a Gaussian distribution using Bayes rule remember that if both Gaussian distributions had the same covariance then the separating hyper surface was linear. This is also called linear discriminant analysis. If they had a different covariance then the hyper surface was quadratic since the covariance terms did not cancel out. This is called quadratic discriminant analysis.

What's another way to think about this. Well if you break down the individual components of X and let's say d equals 2 then we have x left 1, x left 2, x right 1 and x right 2. Well the result is we're now getting terms like parameter times. X left 1 times X right 1 and parameter times x left 1 times x left 2. We saw these in my linear regression course and we call them interaction terms. So that's a pretty simple addition. Now how do we implement this rather than concatenate the left child's value and the right child's value .

We are going to leave them separate and implement an equivalent formulation. Hopefully it will help you more easily picture what's happening. So let's start again with the plain recursive neural network. We can extend this by adding three quadratic terms. Of course each a must be of size D by D by D. How many weights is that in total? It's three times D to the power of three. Note that this is not the same as the original formulation, which had four D to the power of three terms .

The question you want to ask yourself is are we missing any interaction terms here. I would recommend trying both and comparing the results. All right so let's talk about how we're going to do this in Theano. It should be a small change to the plain recursive neural network so we aren't going to code the entire thing again. The only part that's different is that we are going to represent our trees as a different set of lists . In particular we'll get rid of the notion of relations and parents instead will have three lists called words, left_children and right_children children as the name suggests.

The words list will contain the word index. If that note is a word otherwise it will be minus one as usual. Left children will contain the index of the left child. If the node has a left child and the same goes for the right. Note that any node with a left child will also have a right child since all nodes have either 0 or 2 children. Now why are we doing this. Recall that in our previous formulation when we calculated the value at the current node we would feed its value into the parent.

But now we need to not only multiply the current node by a weight but we need to multiply it by its sibling nodes value as well which we currently don't have access to. So using lists for left children and right children makes this task easier. Let's go through the main code changes of course we'll be initializing more weights in particular W11, W12 and W22. The Theano inputs we initialize will now be words, left children, right children and labels.

The recurrence will now be defined in terms of the formulation I described previously. If it's a word return the word vector. If it's not a word calculate this node value in terms of its children. You can see a full set of changes in the class repo in the file rntn_theano.py. Let's now look at the results

9. RNTN in Tensorflow (Tips)
============================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python Part 6 in this lecture we're going to go over some tips for how to implement an r and RNTN in TensorFlow. In the previous section of the course we looked at how RNNs could solve problems such as parts of speech tagging and name recognition. We were able to use built in classes such as the GRU cell and the LSTM cell. But in this section while we're still building what is essentially an r n n we cannot make use of these built in classes.

The main reason is that we have four sequences that we need to loop through simultaneously. We have the words list which tells us the word index of the current word. If the current node represents a word we have left children which tells us the location of the left child of the current node we have right children which tells us the location of the right child of the current node and at each node in the sequence we need to combine these either with a linear transformation or a quadratic transformation depending on whether we're building a plain recursive net or an RNTN.

So we need to be able to have a custom loop that can go over these sequences. Luckily TensorFlow has a function for this called while_loop. The anatomy of while_loop is similar to scan so you can see how the TensorFlow function while_loop is related to how you'd write a while loop in regular Python code. Now if you're a beginner at TensorFlow you might be asking why do we need a while_loop function and can't we just use a normal while loop. And of course that cannot work because we are working with symbolic variables we don't know any of the values of the words left children or right children and we don't even know their length.

So we don't know how many times the loop should run and so on. So it's not possible to use a normal while loop. We can only define how the computation should proceed when we actually pass in real values. So TensorFlow's while_loop only allows us to define how the loop will proceed in the future. So let's look at what these arguments into the while loop are going to look like. Let's start with something easy. The looping condition we want to continue the loop as long as this is true.

Of course we want to loop for as long as the length of the input sentence. So for this we can use the tf.less function. Now of course if we were working with real NumPy arrays we could just say while n less than the length of words but we can't do that because words is a symbolic variable and it has no actual length. So we have to use TensorFlow functions which do the equivalent calculation but on symbolic variables. Next we have the body. This is where we actually calculate the output for this node.

Now remember that there are two separate cases. The first is if this is a leaf node in which case this note represents a word and so the value is just the word embedding for that word. The second case is if it's not a leaf node in which case the value is a transformation on the left child and right child. And since this is a binary tree every node will have two children one left and one right. You'll also recall that if we're building a plain recursive net then we do a linear transformation but if we have an RNTN then we do a quadratic transformation.

Of course since our while loop has to be in terms of symbolic variables then our body of the loop also has to be in terms of symbolic variables. So that means we need to use TensorFlow's version of an if else the function we want is tf.cond. And it takes in three arguments. The first argument is the if condition. The second argument is a function that should be called if the condition is true. And the third argument is a function that should be called if the condition is false.

It's important to remember that this function takes in functions as arguments and not tensors which is what you might have expected. So if you want to return a tensor you can use Python's lambda notation now that you know the basic structure of the body of the loop we can look at the whole function in its entirety. So firstly notice how it takes in two arguments, the hiddens and the current node index n. Since this is a recurrence that means we also have to return two things of the same structure which will then be passed into the same function on the next iteration.

The first thing we actually need to do in this function is get the word index at n and call that w. Remember that this gives us one of two things either it'll give us a word index which means that the current node is a leaf node because it represents a word or it gives us minus one which means that the current node is not a leaf node. And there is no word. The next part is a little bit confusing. First it's helpful to think of the hidden variable as an array which contains the output of each tree node.

And so at each call of this function what we want to calculate is hiddens of n the sequences left_children of n and right_children of n tell us where the hidden values of the children of the current node are located. The really confusing part is what kind of data structure is this hiddens variable. Remember that we've encountered two major data structures in our study of TensorFlow so far we have placeholders which represent data and we have variables which represent parameters all the variables in a TensorFlow graph are updated automatically when we do gradient descent.

The problem is hiddens is neither of those. It doesn't represent data we're going to pass in via the feed_dict. Nor does it represent a parameter we want to be updated. Instead it's just an intermediate output in the neural network just like the hidden layers of a regular feedforward neural network. What we really want here is a sort of cache that can store all the intermediate values we produce as we produce them. It's sort of like a dictionary but not exactly a dictionary because again we're working with symbolic variables.

The relevant data structure in TensorFlow is the TensorArray. Basically this allows us to do the two operations we need to do during our recursive calculation which is reading from a specific index and also writing to a specific index where that index is a symbolic variable which we're calling n. So the key is a symbolic integer index and the value is a 1D tensor representing the hidden value of the current node in actuality we can't index the tensor array using square bracket notation but we can use the read and write methods which do the same thing.

Finally once we create the hidden value and write it to our hiddens array we increment n by 1. This is equivalent to n plus equals 1. But of course we're writing it in symbolic form so it's better to use TensorFlow's add function. Although I think these days TensorFlow has operator overloading so you can also use plus equals one if you want to. The next thing we want to talk about is how to build the cost just like the Theano version. We have a choice between training using only a label at the root node which means the sentiment for the entire sentence or we can train using the labels at all the intermediate notes as well.

So let's go over the single label case since that's a little simpler. First when we get back hiddens from TensorFlow's while_loop, keep in mind that it's still a tensor array we still need to pass this to our final dense layer to get our output predictions. But we have a problem in order to do this. We need to do a matmul, but matmul only accepts tensors. So right now the tensor array is an array of length t and each element is a 1D tensor of size D. So here t represents sentence length and D equals the vector dimensionality.

What we'd like to do is have a T by D matrix and to get that we can call the stack function which is an instance method of tensor array. Once we multiply this by the output matrix and add the output bias we get the logits which we can then pass in to the cross entropy loss function. Now for a single label only we can just index the logits and labels by minus one to get the final logit and label. And we know that this corresponds to the root node of the tree because we're doing post order traversal for the case where you want to use every node label.

You have to be careful. In particular we're going to do binary classification on this task even though the sentiments are labeled 0 to 4 in the original paper 0 and 1 meant negative sentiment and 3 and 4 meant positive sentiment whereas two meant neutral but the original paper did something interesting which was they removed all the neutral sentences from the data set. So that means the final prediction can be only either positive or negative. And there are no neutral sentences that actually turns out to be helpful because a majority of the samples in the dataset were neutral which led to a class imbalance.

The problem is that this still leaves some of the inner nodes with neutral labels which we use minus 1 to encode. So we have to be careful not to include this in our cost function in Theano or NumPy, this would be very simple we could just select the relevant labels and then grab all the P of y given X's at all those locations where labels is greater than or equal to zero. This works because Theano allows us to use multi dimensional indexing just like NumPy. In TensorFlow we can use the gather function.

But hopefully since you can see the equivalent calculation in NumPy you can understand what it's doing. So this has been a very long lecture and you are most likely still confused. So what I'd recommend is referring back to this lecture as you try to read through the code. It's very complex. So you're most likely going to have to go back and forth multiple times. You need the context of the code to understand this lecture but you also need this lecture to understand the code this lecture covered the most difficult parts.

But rest assured the structure of the code remains as usual. We load in the data build our model which in this case is a custom built recursive neural network that works sequentially. We build the cost and then we run our training loop which optimizes the cost. Now I know a lot of people are going to ask me this. So let's just go over this now. This is hard. Why didn't you just do this in Keras? And of course with the amount of customization required this type of thing is just not possible in Keras

as of today of course if you want to try to prove me wrong you're certainly welcome to.

10. RNTN in Tensorflow (Code)
=============================

Everyone and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture we're going to look at the code to implement an RNTN in TensorFlow. The relevant file in the course repo is rntn_tf_rnn.py. This is to differentiate it from the naive recursive version, which is rntn_tf.py. So the basic outline of this is as usual load in the data train the model print the score.

Of course the interesting part to us is how do we build the model. So we have the class RecursiveNN and it takes in some arguments into its constructor: V, which is the vocabulary size D which is the embedding dimension which is also the output dimension of every node and K the number of output classes for the activation function we're going to use tanh. Next we have the fit function. It takes in the train trees the test trees regularization parameter number of epochs and a flag that says whether or not we're going to train using the inner nodes remember that trees at this point has already been converted into sequences.

So the next step is to initialize our parameters and our placeholders. The word embedding is of course V by D. The quadratic weights are D by D by D and the linear weights are D by D. Hopefully none of this is surprising. Next we turn these into TensorFlow variables. After that we initialize the placeholders. So these are just placeholders that represent the exact same data structure as how our trees are stored. We have the words list, the left children the right children and the labels.

We also want to save as instance variables so we can make use of them in all the different instance methods of this object. Next we have some convenience functions for doing dot products. Normally we would use TensorFlow's matmul in a neural network but that's not really ideal for us. First matmul requires both arguments to be a 2D tensor or in other words a matrix. But we actually never have that in our case H left and right are vectors because we're doing stochastic gradient descent.

So we're only looking at one sample at a time. So one of our arguments is always going to be one dimensional On top of that our quadratic weights are also not matrices because they're three dimensional and because in one case we need to multiply a vector by a tensor. And in the other case we need to multiply a tensor by a vector. We have two functions for this, dot1 and dot2. So you can see how we make use of dot1 and dot2 in recursive_net_transform. This just does the recursive transformation which we looked at in the theory lecture.

Note the use of the read function on hiddens which is a tensor array because remember you can't use square brackets for this special type of array. You need to use the built in read and write functions. Next we have a recurrence function which is the body of the while_loop. The first thing we do here is get the current word which is the word at index n, and then we check whether or not this is a leaf node or an inner node. If it's a leaf node, w will be greater than or equal to zero.

And so we just do an embedding lookup. If it's not a leaf node then we do the recursive transform which we just made this of course gives us the value of the hiddens at index n, so we call this h_n, and then we assign that to the nth index in the hiddens tensor array. Finally we increment n and return both hiddens and n. Next we have a loop condition which as we discussed is just checking that n is less than the length of the words list. Next you can see how we initialize the hiddens tensor array.

So we start out with a size of zero and set dynamic_size equal to true so that it can grow as we add more elements. This makes it somewhat like a C++ vector. Next we actually call tf.while_loop to get the final value of hiddens. This also returns the final value of n, which we can ignore. Next since hiddens is still a tensor array containing 1D vectors we need to convert it into a matrix using stack so that we can get the output of the final layer.

This gives us our logits. From the logits we can take the argmax to get the prediction and we can save that for later. So we can call it in different functions. Next we build the loss or cost first we can collect the regularization cost which is just the L2 penalty. Then we check if train_inner_nodes is true or false. If it's true,. We first need to filter out any of the minus ones from the labels since those represent neutral sentiments which we are not training for.

So we use tf.where(labels greater than or equal to zero. Using this we can index both the logits and the labels to get the relevant entries. The TensorFlow notation for this is the gather function. Next, if train_inner_nodes is false we can calculate the cross entropy using just the last index of logits and labels which is at minus 1. Remember that this is because we're using post-order traversal. So the root node always appears last. Next is all standard stuff.

We create our optimizer which in this case is AdaGrad. We initialize our session and we run our training loop. Note that if you're using a GPU, using InteractiveSession will break the AdaGrad optimizer, which is just a bug in TensorFlow. So if you're using a GPU just change that to tf.Session. Inside the training loop. We shuffle the data and loop through each sample. Remember this is stochastic gradient descent. So we're just looking at one sample at a time.

Inside the loop we run the cost_op, predict_op and train_op. When it's time to calculate the accuracy. Note that we only care about the prediction at the root node not the inner nodes despite the fact that we may be training on the inner nodes at the end of each epoch we also print the test accuracy. We don't want to do this too often since it's pretty slow. Next we have our predict function which makes use of the predict_op we defined earlier and then we have our score function and f1 function, which of course makes use of the predict function. Next we have some tree processing functions.

If you already saw this stuff in the Theano code then this is just review so you can feel free to skip it. So first we have add_idx_to_tree. This is going to recursively number each node so that we can make use of those indexes later when we assign left children and right children. So this is precisely post order traversal. Next we have the tree2list function. So this is going to take in a tree object and it's going to return four lists: the words list, left children,

right children and labels of course this also uses post-order traversal. So the first thing we need to do is call it recursively on the left child and then on the right child. Next we check what we should get for the current node. So if the word at this node is None that means this is an inner node that means W is minus 1 and the left children and right children are whatever indices we found earlier. Otherwise this node is a leaf node which means w is tree.word and there are no children.

So we set left and right to minus one. Next we combine the lists we got from our recursive calls to get the current lists. Since this is post-order traversal we first add the left then the right and then the current next we check if we should do binary assignment of the labels. So for any label greater than 2 we make that positive sentiment for any label less than 2. We make that negative sentiment and for any label equal to two we set that to minus one so we can filter it out later.

Otherwise if we're not doing binary labeling we leave the labels at 0 to 4. Finally we combine our child labels with the current label and then we return the lists that represent this tree. So it's words, left children, right children and labels. Finally we have the main function which should be very straightforward compared to everything else. We looked at. So first we get the data which is currently in the form of tree objects. Then we use the functions we just made to turn the trees into lists.

We also filter out any neutral sentences if we're doing binary training which we are by default. Next we shuffle the train and test data. Shuffling the train data was more so you could test the code with a smaller amount of data just to make sure it's working. We also wanted to create a small test to act as our validation set during training. As I mentioned earlier calculating the validation accuracy takes quite long. So you don't want to have to do that often.

Finally we create the model fit it and print the scores. So let's run this and see what we get.

11. Recursive Neural Network in TensorFlow with Recursion
=========================================================

Hey guys and welcome back to this class. Natural Language Processing deep learning in Python. Part 6 in this lecture I'm going to walk you through an actual recursive implementation of a recursive neural network. I'm going to show you this so that you can see for yourself what happens when you use recursion to create a recursive neural network. This lecture is optional since it won't prevent you from learning how to write a recursive neural network without using recursion but it might give you further insight as to why you do not want to code it this way.

So the first thing you'll see is the function get_labels. As usual it's doing post order traversal. Next we have our RNTN constructor here we just initialize all the weights and turn them into TensorFlow variables that are updatable. Next we have the fit function. So here's where things get really different. We have lists to store our train ops, costs, predictions and labels. Why is that. Because remember these are going to be different for every tree. So we loop through all the trees and populate these lists.

Remember that what we want here are callable functions that need to be compiled. There's a little bit of new TensorFlow stuff here as well. Like Saver which helps us save and load the model weights when we move from one function to the next . This is because everything in TensorFlow happens in a session. Next we have our main training loop where we loop through all the functions we just created. Notice how we don't even need to pass in any input. This is because the trees define the neural network graph and we've already compiled them.

Once we're done we save the model and plot the costs. Next we have the get_cost function. Remember that TensorFlow doesn't compute its cost on the softmax output directly. It works on the logits which come right before the softmax. This is because there are some optimizations that can be done that aren't present in the direct formulation . Recall that in deep learning part two we used the indicator matrix of targets for the cost. Since then TensorFlow developers have implemented a new function that can act on the labels directly called sparse_softmax_cross_entropy_with_logits,

which just goes to show how fast things can change in this field. Next let's look at get_output and get_output_recursive. This is going to return the logits for every node in the tree. So we need to put them into a list and we need to do it in the right order. That just means we need to use post-order traversal. Notice that here is where the recursion takes place. Finally we have the score function. It's pretty long compared to our usual score functions.

Why is that because of the sentences that get passed in can be arbitrary and these are all different graphs. So we still need to compile them. There is a small optimization here where we can pass in None as the input and it will use the precompiled prediction ops. Notice that this is where we use Saver again to reload the trained model. So that's pretty much everything for this file. If you want to actually see the difference this makes. run this and the recurrent version with the same number of samples and compare how long each takes

In Theano regular variables are not updateable and to make an updateable variable we need to create what is called a shared variable. So let's do that now. And then let's tell Theano how we want to update x by giving it an update expression. So what's nice about Theano is that it calculates gradients automatically the grad function takes in two parameters.

The first is the function you want to take the gradient of. And the second is the variable you want the gradient with respect to. You can pass in multiple variables as a list into the second parameter. So now let's create the Theano train function. It's like the previous function we created except we're going to add a new argument which is updates. The updates argument takes in a list of tuples each tuple has two things. The first thing is the shared variable to update and the second thing is the update expression to use.

So this has no inputs. You'll see why later. So we've created a function to train but we haven't actually called it yet. Now notice that X is not an input it's the thing that we update in later examples the inputs will be the data and labels. So the inputs parameter takes in data and labels and the updates parameter takes in your model parameters with their updates. Now we have to write our own loop to call the training function so it converges pretty quickly.

Notice that we've converged to the expected cost and we can print the optimal value of x using the get_value function and this is also what we expect.

You can pass in a TensorFlow random normal as its initial value or you can also pass in an array you can also make your variable scalar. For TensorFlow variables you need to initialize them; you can use the initialize_all_variables function to do that. It actually returns a thing that you have to call in the session later. So let's open a session. Let's run our init. Let's just print out.

So we get. So we get none. You can also print out the values of your TensorFlow variables using the eval function but that's after you run it. So now let's try to find the minimum of a simple cost function like we did in Theano. So let's create a variable initialized to 20 just like we did in Theano. Create the same cost function. And so one difference between Theano and TensorFlow is that you don't write the updates yourself. TensorFlow instead what you do is you choose an optimizer TensorFlow has a bunch of these that implements the algorithm that you want.

So for example gradient descent optimizer is just regular gradient descent. You pass in 0.3 is the learning rate. And if you want to know more about the params you can check their documentation. And then you tell it what expression you want to minimize. So we're going to again initialize our variables. And then open our session and so we have to write the loop ourselves. So we're going to run the train function and we're going to print the cost and the value of that parameter on each iteration.

So it gives us the expected answer.
